# The scenes

The twenty backdrops the game, the Testbed and the map editor all share, plus the island the gun stands on and the glass drain in the middle of it.

Part of the BS3D documentation. CLAUDE.md holds the project overview, the build commands, the ball grid and the repo-wide conventions, and says which of these documents covers what.

## The setting, in one copy

Every executable used to build the setting for itself, and #75 ended that the way #74 ended three copies of the frame's exit (see "Color management" in `docs/rendering.md`). The island's stone cap and concrete drum, the glass drain with its two gold beads and the dark pit shaft behind it (`Prazsky.Core.Render.ArenaIsland`, which owns every mesh, texture, renderer, world matrix and figure of them); the glass plate a cluster hangs from (`CeilingPlate`); the dome-derived light rig, its hemisphere scales, its tint strength and its overcast palette (`SkyLightRig`); a scene's own point lights (`SceneLights`); the drain's collision mesh (`Prazsky.BS3D.Physics.FunnelPhysics`); the ten look values of the cloud deck and the two applies that push them (`CloudField`, which already owned the field's shape); the forest's wood — the fifteen tree, boulder and stump meshes, the twenty-five renderers that dress them, the three matte materials, the encoded tints and the six instanced draws (twenty meshes, thirty renderers and eight draws since #462 added standing snags and fallen logs; `ForestScatterRenderer`, the last piece of #75, and the one whose absence had left two of the three executables drawing a bare clearing — see "The forest"); and every question anybody asks about a `SceneKind` — `IsSolidTerrainScene`, `OpenBelow`, `SceneCount`, `NextScene`, `SceneName`, `TryParseScene`, all statics on `SceneRenderer` now — are one copy each in the libraries. They had been identical value for value, which is not something a *shared* setting should ever have been able to be, and the duplication had already cost twice: the forest was missing from **both** hand-kept `IsSolidTerrainScene` lists (below), and the seven-of-eleven scene cycle was a bare `% 7` in two files.

What the hoist deliberately did **not** move is the frame sequence. `ArenaIsland` hands its draws over in three slices — `DrawIsland`, `DrawPit`, `DrawGlass` — precisely so each executable goes on placing them itself: the Game puts the gun, the cluster and the shot trails between the pit and the glass, and its front end runs the same frame with nothing between them at all. Sky-lit enrolment stays the caller's for the same reason, each having its own list and its own reasons (the editor lights the balls, the city and the forest's scatter, there being no island, drain or ceiling of its own to light) — and all of them leave the pit shaft out, so no dome bleaches the inside of a hole in the ground. The ceiling glass sits outside both lists since #156: it stands against the sky itself, so the Testbed and the Game hand it to `SkyLightRig.ApplyToGlass` instead, which pushes the same rig with the three directional lights scaled to the sky's own brightness — the dome scenes always hid the plate by lighting it from the very palette that shows through it, and the sky-replacing scenes' full-strength sun (kept for the balls and the drain's beads) had turned the plate into a bright slab over their near-black backdrops. The Game's plate renderer may be null mid-load; the push tolerates it. Every scatter renderer takes part wherever the wood stands, walked as the one flat array `ForestScatterRenderer.Renderers` hands back, or a spruce of the variant the list missed would stand under the rig of whatever dome was up when it was made — and since a re-planted wood is thirty brand-new renderers, whoever re-plants one has to re-light it too, exactly as a refitted `CeilingPlate` must be.

**The ceiling plate is cut as crystal since #541, and in the Game it bends what is behind it.** It was a `BoxMesh`; it is a `CutSlabMesh` now — the same box with its edge **ground in facets all round**: `CeilingPlate.EDGE_PROFILE` gives the edge in section, two facets under (`BEVEL`, 0.25 back), a straight side band, and a three-facet crown over it (`CROWN`, 0.4 back) that flattens as it climbs, about 74°, 53° and 28°, the way a gem's crown does; and every vertical corner is cut round in `CORNER_FACETS` (3) flat cuts, all tangent to one circle `CORNER_RADIUS` (0.7) in from both sides, so each band turns the corner at its own angle. The widest ring is still the box's footprint (its corners cut) and the two caps still lie in its two faces, so `FootprintFor`, `THICKNESS`, `TopFaceY` and `CentreYAbove` read exactly what they read, and so do the collidable (a Bepu `Box` still), the floor net, the camera fit and the HUD's side-cut bar. Its top face carries a cut the shader evaluates rather than models, in three zones in from the rim: a **border of fine V flutes** one unit wide (`RIM_BAND`), `FLUTE_PERIOD` 0.25 apart at `FLUTE_SLOPE` 0.35, square to the nearest cut of the outline so they fan round each corner and meet at a mitre, the whole border leaning `RIM_LEAN` down towards the rim; a steep V groove parting it from the field; and the field's **diamond cut** — low pyramids on a grid turned 45°, `CUT_PERIOD` 2 units apart, facets at `CUT_SLOPE` 0.18 (about 10°). Two frequencies on purpose, a cut-glass tray's fine border round a coarse middle — the second pass on the owner's word (2026-09-25) that the edges could be more detailed, more cut; the first cut had one 45° bevel per edge and one 45° cut per corner. The cut is evaluated because the pixel shader needs the facet's normal at the point a ray *leaves* the slab, which a modelled facet cannot give it; and a ray the trace sends out through the top's plane over the crown takes the crown's own facet (`GlassRimAt` finds the outline analytically from the same figures), so the rim reads cut from below too. **The underside stays flat**, because it is what the cluster hangs from: the ceiling socket anchors a ball's top to that face, the flat part is the footprint less the bevel on each side, and the top level's outermost balls touch it at least half a unit in — at a corner the underside's rim crosses the diagonal 0.38 in from both sides, where the corner ball touches 0.5 in, exactly where the first cut's single 45° corner crossed it. The refraction itself — the copy of the frame the plate bends, where it is taken and why, and the ray traced through the slab — is under "And the glass in it bends what is behind it" in `docs/rendering.md`. **Only the Game refracts** (it is the only executable that hands the renderer `GlassBehind`); the Testbed and the editor draw the cut slab as the plain translucent pane it always was, the cut showing only in its bevelled silhouette. **And it casts since #553**: a faint shadow of the slab with a dark crown rim and the diamond field's caustics, computed in every shadow receiver rather than drawn into the map — see "The ceiling's glass shadow" in `docs/rendering.md`.

Three things came out of reconciling the copies:

- **One deliberate visible change, and it is an improvement to the Testbed rather than a no-op — the old number must not be "restored".** The two copies disagreed on exactly one figure: the ground-proximity darkening anchor the balls' and the gun's renderers are handed (`InstancedModelRenderer.GroundHeight`). The Testbed was passing **−9.49**, a value its own comment admitted was kept from the old ground-block layout whose recessed centre block's top sat at −9.5 — geometry deleted long since, so the referent no longer existed. It is the island's own top (`ArenaIsland.TOP_Y`, −8.5) in both executables now. Measured against the shader's ground-occlusion range of 2.0: a ball resting on the stone was getting a proximity of `1 − 0.99/2 = 0.505` instead of `1.0`, i.e. about half the contact darkening it should have, and the effect faded out at y = −7.49 instead of at −6.5. The Game was already correct. (The anchor stays `TOP_Y` now that the walkable top dishes below it — the shader's term saturates under the anchor, so a ball rolling down the dish keeps full contact darkening rather than an overdriven one.)
- **`OpenBelow` is defined as the complement of `IsSolidTerrainScene`, not as a second list.** It was two hand-kept sets in different files, and the negation is a consequence rather than a shortcut: the opaque near-black pit shaft those scenes need is exactly what closes the view from below, so a scene that wants the shaft cannot also give the drop cinematic a vantage under the stone. A twelfth scene is one decision now instead of two that can quietly disagree — and when the twelfth actually arrived (the Moon, #125) the complement held: it wants the shaft and not the dive. What the Moon did split is the *other* pair — it is the first scene in `IsSolidTerrainScene` **and** `ReplacesSky` at once; see "The Moon (Game)" for why that is one deliberate decision and not a drift.
- **`SceneFrame.SunColor` and the clouds' lit side are one number** (`SkyLightRig.SunRadianceTinted`): the sun's own radiance carried towards the dome's horizon colour by `SkyLightRig.SKY_TINT_STRENGTH`, which is what makes the deck lit by literally the same light as everything under it. They were two separately-computed expressions that happened to agree, written out twice per executable, and unifying them took a per-frame `Lerp` **and** a per-frame delegate allocation off the draw path in all three: the cloud hook a `SceneFrame` carries is set once at load on the rig, where a method group written at the call site built a fresh delegate every frame it was evaluated. It is taken off the **dome** and never off `KeyTint`, even though the two are the same expression wherever no scene overrides the rig — a sky-replacing scene states its own key tint precisely because a dome-derived one would be a lie, and folding that override into the sun would change what the terrain shaders are told the sun is.

Verified at runtime in all three executables, and the figures reconciled: every numeric literal of the old island code survived onto `ArenaIsland` at the hoist (the dish has since added `DISH_DEPTH` and moved the drain family that far below `TOP_Y` — see "The city and the glass arena"), and the Game still reports 315 balls / 1088 constraints on `One.json`.

## The city and the glass arena

The default of the twenty settings (NumPad2 cycles through every one of them, below) — no flag: a **small round stone island** (the drain funnel ringed by a moulded stone platform, below) in a clearing between the towers of a procedural city (`Prazsky.Core.Render.City`, shared with the map editor; see "The shared scene backdrops"). The arena used to be a big square marble-and-glass plaza sized to the ground blocks, but it ate the whole lower frame and hid the scene it stood in; it is now the round island in **every** scene, so the sea, the dunes or the city fill the frame rather than a plaza. The castle backdrop and the shadow overlay are gone, and so are the marble ground blocks — the island's only physics floor is the funnel's own mesh (see "The drain funnel").

The city stands **over** the arena; it no longer falls away underneath it. The first version cleared the blocks under the glass, which left nothing to look through the glass at; a second version filled them back in, cut off far below so a look down the drain funnel would read as a drop. **#275 took the under-arena blocks back out again, for good**: no play or drop-cinematic camera angle ever looks down the funnel's narrow throat far enough to see that shaft, so it was cost with no payoff — the clearing above reads exactly the same either way, since the roofline never reached down to it. The second correction, unrelated, put the roofline below the floor, so no tower was ever seen against the sky — only bright roofs with dark canyons beside them, which the eye reads as holes punched in a white plain rather than as buildings (it was reported as inverted normals, and it was not). `ROOFLINE_Y` therefore sits well above the play surface.

**The roofs carry equipment since #436** (`Prazsky.Core.Render.CityRooftops`, meshes in `RooftopMesh`, knobs in `CitySceneConfig.Rooftops`). The owner's report was that the towers read as bare-topped. The kinds and their proportions were drawn from references rendered locally for it (#441: a daytime roofscape, the neon city at night and a sheet of separate pieces):
- **Kinds:** triangular lattice masts in red and white sections with an aircraft-warning beacon on top, satellite dishes on posts with a feed arm at the focus, poles of three 5G sector panels, and air-conditioning units.
- **Draw calls:** one instanced draw per mesh (eleven kinds counting the mast's two colours, the beacon and the four neon rings), culled to the frustum every frame. All three executables draw it after the city, in the city's opaque state.
- **Where it pays is the skyline, and the placement follows that.** The play camera stands low and looks up past the towers, so a roof is a silhouette against the sky. A tower standing above the generator's own roofline at its distance takes a mast at 17 %, a lower one at 2.5 %. Masts and beacons are drawn at any distance; dishes and 5G poles stop at `EquipmentDistance` (320); the air conditioning stops at `ClutterDistance` (170), since only a camera looking *down* on a roof sees it.
- **A roof is split into cells** (3×3 on a big roof, 2×2 on a small one), so no two pieces stand in each other. The mast takes the middle, a dish an edge, a 5G pole a corner, the air conditioning what is left. **Every dish faces the same stretch of sky**, give or take 0.35 rad, because real dishes over a city point at the geostationary arc, and a roofscape of dishes facing every which way reads as props.
- **The placement is a function of the tower, not of generator order.** Each roof's random stream is seeded from its own centre, so a city rebuilt at another radius (a quality step) keeps the equipment on the towers it shares. The seed is an integer mix and **not `HashCode.Combine`**, which .NET seeds afresh per process: the first build dressed 7 880 pieces in one launch and 7 800 in the next.
- **The neon city carries more, from the same layout.** Its extra pieces are rolled on the same roof at `NeonExtra` (1.45×) the chances, kept at the end of each kind's list and drawn only in that scene. There every dish also wears a ring of the neon lights' own magenta or cyan (`NeonRingBrightness`).
- **The beacons flash together, on the wall clock**, the way a city's warning lights are set: a short bright flash every `BeaconPeriod` (1.6 s), over the glare threshold so each blooms.
- **Measured cost: at most 0.06 ms.**
  - **The runs:** the branch was measured against unmodified `main` in the Testbed, which can pin a camera: three alternating pairs per case, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (a 3200×1800 target), `nocap`, each run the median of its per-second lines after a warm-up.
  - **The play-level view** (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,6,-60`): **+0.01 ms** in the city (2.29 against 2.28) and **+0.04 ms** in the neon city (2.70 against 2.66).
  - **Looking down across the roofs** (`campos=10,75,-10 camtarget=70,40,60`), the worst case, with the air conditioning in view: **+0.03 ms** and **+0.06 ms** (1.91 against 1.85).
  - **The layout:** 7 075 pieces, the neon extras included.
- **What was not done:** guy wires and the second diagonal of an X-braced lattice. Neither survives a mast's distance from the play camera, where a strut under a pixel wide shimmers instead of reading, and both would only be paid for.

**The towers stand on a street since #399** (`Prazsky.Core.Render.CityStreets`, `CityStreets.fx`, knobs in `CitySceneConfig.Streets`). The owner's report was that the buildings ran far below anything anybody sees and ended over nothing: every tower went down to `BaseY` −420, and from any view that looked down the dome showed as bright slits at the bottom of the canyons. The treatment was read off references rendered locally for it (a canyon from above by day, one crossing from straight above, a paved square, and the canyons at night).
- **`BaseY` is −100**, the street level. The generator's bounds now put a tower between 83 and 156 units tall at the Testbed's 14-block radius (38 to 71 floors at the window pitch), and the island's top stands 91.5 units over the square under it. Moving it re-lays each tower's window rows, which run from its base, so which windows are lit reshuffles; each tower's neon hue is keyed off its footprint and did not move.
- **One flat quad, drawn last of the city**, after the towers and their roofs, so every pixel of street a tower stands in front of fails the depth test before the shader runs. It reaches 2 500 units out; past the city's last block the pattern fades to its own average and then into the horizon's colour.
- **The pattern is the city's own grid**, evaluated per pixel from `City.BlockPitch` and `City.StreetWidth` (a snapshot the city takes when it is built, so the map editor's panel cannot draw streets through towers it has not rebuilt). Each block's square is paved; the sidewalk reaches `SidewalkWidth` (1.2) into the street and turns each corner on a curb of `CornerRadius` (2.2); the asphalt between carries a dashed centre line, a stop line across the approaching lanes, a zebra crossing before every crossing, a manhole per segment and three slots per lane that hold a car at `CarChance`.
- **What the grid cannot say comes from a texture of one texel per block** (`City.BlockBuilt`): a block without a tower (the square under the island and the generator's scattered plazas) is laid in larger stone with a border course and a grid of trees, shaded as domes towards the sun over the shadows they throw.
- **The same texture stands in for the canyon's shadows.** A street 9 wide between towers 100 tall sees a few percent of the sky, and left fully lit the canyon floors glowed like the roofs. Sampled bilinearly, and again a block out each way, it gives how built-up a pixel's neighbourhood is; that density takes the sky down to `CanyonSkyView` and the sun to `CanyonSunView`, a crossing is open along both streets, the foot of a built block is darker again, and `FacadeBounce` puts back some of what sunlit facades throw down.
- **No haze inside the city.** The first build faded the ground from the eye and laid a tenth of the bright horizon over asphalt that reflects a twentieth of the light, which read as a brown veil; the towers take no haze, so the ground only fades past the city's last block.
- **The neon city** replaces the dome's light with `NeonAmbient`: sodium lamps every `LampSpacing` (10) along every curb, as two lattices so a pool never meets a seam; the shops' magenta or cyan spilling onto the sidewalk at a tower's foot, from the neon lights' own colours; and head- and tail-lights on every car with the beam on the road ahead, kept under the glare threshold (a glow a few pixels across is exactly what the glare catches on some frames and misses on others). The first lamps overlapped into one orange floor; at 1.3 and a narrower pool they are separate pools with dark asphalt between.
- **Where it shows:** the Spectrum's chapter intro above all (`TryGetViewpoint`'s "the canyon", which now looks at the street at −100 rather than 50 units under it) — photographed in the Game, it looks down past the island onto the squares under it, the crossings and the traffic, where it used to look into the dark; through the drain's glass from above; and from any free camera. The menu's and the play camera's views stay level with the island and do not see it, and neither does the drop cinematic, which looks up.
- **Measured cost: none — the city got cheaper.** Three alternating pairs per camera against unmodified `main` in the Testbed, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (a 3200×1800 target), `nocap`, each run the median of its per-second lines after a warm-up:
  - **The play-level view** (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,6,-60`): **−0.31 ms** (1.96 against 2.27, every pair within 0.01). The frame's lower edge reaches 26° under the horizon, so the old towers were shaded far below −100 past the island's rim and through its glass; that area is now street, whose shader is a fraction of the facades'.
  - **Looking down across the roofs** (`campos=40,70,140 camtarget=0,-60,-80`): **−0.08 ms** in the city (1.78 against 1.86), **−0.09 ms** in the neon city (2.02 against 2.11).
  - **Down a canyon to a crossing** (`campos=35,30,70 camtarget=45,-100,20`): **−0.09 ms** in the city (1.92 against 2.01), **−0.12 ms** in the neon city (2.16 against 2.28).

The island is a **cast-concrete drum with a dressed stone top and a moulded coping**, built by `Prazsky.Core.Render.IslandMesh` and assembled by `ArenaIsland`, which the Testbed and the Game both draw and the map editor deliberately does not (above). Its top is `ArenaIsland.TOP_Y` (−8.5) — a plain figure of the island's own now rather than something derived from the ground blocks that are long deleted, and the plane of the stone top's **outer arris**, from which the walkable top falls `ArenaIsland.DISH_DEPTH` (1.2) to the drain: the drain's rim, its gold bead and the pit's mouth all share the lower plane `TOP_Y − DISH_DEPTH`, and the collision floor runs between the two by construction (before the dish all four were flush at `TOP_Y`, which is what the Testbed's old `FUNNEL_TOP_Y`/`PIT_TOP_Y` aliases were saying). The top runs from the funnel's rim (`ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_TOP_RADIUS`) out to `ArenaIsland.RADIUS` and the platform drops `ArenaIsland.EDGE_HEIGHT`. The old marble-and-glass panel mosaic, its four marble bands, its mullions and the recessed centre "bath" are all gone, and with them the whole square-plaza machinery (`ARENA_HALF_EXTENT`/`ARENA_PANEL_SIZE`/the panel loop). What replaced *that* was a plain extruded washer (`DiscMesh`, now deleted), and it read as exactly what it was — a cylinder with a hole in it, every edge a raw 90° cut.

- **The shaping is all downward, and that is forced.** The physics floor mirrors the walkable top exactly (its outer arris at the top plane), so anything raised above it would be a lip balls pass straight through; every moulding is therefore cut *below* the top face — the walkable top itself included, which is the dish falling `DISH_DEPTH` from its arris to the drain's mouth so a landed ball rolls in. Outward from the arris: a chamfer, the coping's wash, a bullnose turning down to the platform's widest point, the coping's vertical face, then a **drip** where it oversails the concrete. The drum is battered slightly inward, flares into a string course a third of the way down, and **splays into a plinth** whose foot returns to very nearly the coping's radius.
- **What makes an edge read here is the hemisphere ambient, not a shadow** — there are no drawn shadows in this scene. An upward-facing chamfer takes the sky and draws a bright ring; the underside of the coping's overhang takes the ground bounce and draws a dark one. That pair of lines around the rim is most of the difference between a platform and an extruded circle, and it costs nothing.
- **The plinth flares rather than tapering, for two reasons.** A base that widens carries weight; one that narrows reads as balanced on a point. And the foot's radius is what covers the hole the five solid-terrain scenes cut out of the ground (`TerrainHoleRadius`): the island floats half a unit over that ground, so a foot drawn in much narrower than the platform opens a slot at every grazing angle and the sky shows through it as a pale ring. An earlier profile chamfered in to `r - 1.15` and did exactly that.
- **The physics ring stops at `ArenaIsland.FLOOR_RADIUS`, not at `ArenaIsland.RADIUS`** — `IslandMesh.FloorRadius` of it, the dish's own outer arris rather than the edge of the platform. Carried out to the widest point it would hold a ball up on air over the coping's wash.
- **Two meshes because it is two materials**, split at the coping's drip and built from one polyline so they cannot drift apart: the stone cap (coursed slab joints until #550, an outcrop of rock without them since — below; the sky reflection turned down so it is matte) and the concrete drum (no joints — it is cast, not laid — a coarser relief and barely any reflection). `Prazsky.Core.Render.LatheMesh` is the general machinery: a surface of revolution from a cross-section polyline, per-point creases, and an optional radial irregularity (a function of angle and *world height*, so two lathes sharing an edge are displaced identically along it) that keeps the drum from reading as a machined part. Its triangles are wound clockwise from outside, so unlike `DiscMesh` the island now takes ordinary back-face culling — which is what would *show* a winding mistake rather than hide one.

**The island takes each scene's own material since #404**, the issue's first concrete pass. Until then one island design, the grey dressed stone and concrete that came out of the Testbed, stood in all twenty scenes, so a desert, a volcano, a polar ice sheet and deep space all stood their cannon on the identical slab.
- **What changes.** `ArenaIsland.LookFor(scene)` gives the cap's and the drum's albedo, how polished each is (`SpecularAmbientStrength`) and the cap's slab size, read off the island references #441 rendered and off each scene's own ground:
  - sandstone for the savanna and the desert;
  - red sandstone for the outback, rust rock for Mars;
  - blue-grey granite for the mountains, mossy stone for the forest;
  - black basalt for the volcano, wet dark rock for the cavern, rain-dark slate for the storm;
  - glacier ice for the polar sheet, frosted stone under the aurora;
  - pale coral limestone for the tropical beach;
  - pastel marble for the dream, regolith concrete for the Moon;
  - polished pale concrete and steel for the city, dark polished granite for the neon city (so the neon has something to lie in);
  - machined plates for space, glossy black for the grid.
  
  **Material only**: the geometry, the joints, the relief and the detail textures are the same everywhere.
- **The meadow and the sea keep the authored stone and draw exactly as before, untinted.** It was always their stone.
- **How.** The colour rides the renderer's per-draw diffuse tint, which reduces the authored material to its luminance and multiplies by 1.25 first, so `TintFor` divides that out and the wanted albedo lands exactly. The look is resolved **per draw** from the scene `DrawIsland` is now handed, not pushed on a scene switch: the callers set their scene in several places (a startup argument, a level load, the scene page), and a look one of them forgot to push would stand in the wrong scene.
- **It costs nothing measurable, and it should not.** The single-world `Draw` the island used already went through the one-instance overload the tint rides on. Measured in the Testbed against `main` from the play camera (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, 3200×1800, `nocap`, three pairs): the desert −0.01 / 0.00 / 0.00 ms, the polar sheet 0.00 / +0.01 / +0.01.
- **Tuned by looking at every scene's front end before and after.** The first pass had the savanna's and Mars's sandstone too orange, and space's plates, the aurora's frost and the storm's polished slate close to white. All five came down, and the tropical limestone lost its cream.
- **The rest of #404 was split on the owner's word (2026-09-23):** the geometry is #533 and done below ("The island's shape per scene"); the dressing — lava cracks, ice standing in ice, driftwood, hazard stripes, running lights — is one issue per family, #534–#538. The drain's half of the question is answered next.

### The drain per scene, and the analysis that got it wrong (#404, second pass)

The first pass changed the stone **under** the gold band in nineteen scenes and never asked whether the band still separated from it. It does not, in three of them — and the three are not the ones the obvious analysis picks, which is why both are written down.

- **The albedo analysis, and it is convincing.** CIEDE2000 between the ring's authored diffuse (`FUNNEL_RIM_COLOR`) and each scene's cap albedo makes gold weakest on the four **warm rocks**: outback 13.0 dE, Mars 13.0, savanna 14.0, desert 16.9, against the meadow's 21.8 — the authored stone the whole drain was designed on — and 39–44 on the neon city, the Grid and the polar sheet. The discriminant looks like **hue**: gold sits at Lab h 77°, and those four are the only chromatic caps anywhere near it (desert 78°, savanna 81°, Mars 60°, outback 54°), while every other cap is either achromatic (C\* under 12, where gold pops whatever its luminance) or far round the wheel — the forest 49° away, the dream 112°.
- **⚠ Sampling the actual frames refuted it.** Twenty captures from one fixed camera (`campos=0,-1,24 camtarget=0,-9,0`, 1216×832, `nopost nooverc sceneseed=0`), ring against cap in the **shaded** picture: **desert 5.7, tropical 7.9, savanna 13.1** — and **Mars 24.1, outback 24.9**, comfortably above the meadow's 15.8. Two of the four the albedo model condemned are fine, and the beach it cleared at 30.6 dE is the worst in the game. *Albedo is not what the eye gets*, and on this surface it is not even close.
- **⚠ And the sampler itself lied first.** Its three stations were a left, a right and a top patch on the band; the **top** one sat on the stone above it, because the band is only a few pixels deep at the far side of the ellipse. It was caught by a property no real sample could have: that patch came back **byte-identical between two builds whose pictures visibly differ**. A patch that cannot move is not a sample of the thing that moved. The figures here are the left and right stations only, each verified against a marked-up copy of the frame.
- **The mechanism is the polish, not the hue.** The rims draw at `SpecularAmbientStrength` **1** with `Metalness` 1, so the band is a *mirror* of the sky tinted by its own reflectance rather than a gold surface catching light. Under a bright dome over pale stone it stops being gold at all: on the tropical beach the band samples **0.82, 0.76, 0.58** against a cap of **0.80, 0.76, 0.64**. The three failures are exactly the three brightest, palest caps under the sunniest domes; the two red rocks pass because their dimmer, warmer light never blows the mirror out.
- **So the fix is to stop those three mirroring so hard**, not to change the metal: `ArenaIsland.BLEACHED_SKY_POLISH` (**0.15**) lets the gold's own diffuse carry the band again — **desert 5.7 → 13.3, savanna 13.1 → 14.4, tropical 7.9 → 23.6**, with the other seventeen sampling *identically* across all three builds, which is what says the change is confined. Swapping in a cool metal — the albedo model's answer — would have been **worse**, because steel bleaches paler than gold does. The gold stays the one piece of furniture the game keeps in all twenty.
- **⚠ The polish is not a dial to split the difference on — the response is V-shaped, and 0.45 sits near its floor.** Lowering it walks the band from *mirror of a bright sky* towards *its own gold*, and a pale warm cap lies **between** those two, so a middling value moves the band **onto** the stone rather than off it. On the desert: **1.0 → 5.7 dE, 0.45 → 5.5, 0.15 → 13.3**. This pass shipped 0.45 first and measured no change at all, which is the only reason anyone looked further; the beach, whose cap is paler than both endpoints, rises monotonically (7.9 → 16.0 → 23.6) and on its own would have hidden it. **Sweep a dial like this end to end before picking a value in the middle of it.**
- **⚠ The savanna is the borderline one and it is in on the mechanism, not on the symptom.** Its captures show the band already legible before the change — a yellow ring on tan stone — where the desert's and the beach's are not; at 13.1 dE it is simply the third-most bleached, and the same dome does the same thing to it. It gains the least of the three (+1.3). It is in because one mechanism with one threshold is easier to keep true than two special cases, and it is called out here because it is the one of the three a look could reasonably reverse.
- **The glass is deliberately left alone everywhere.** It never has to separate from the cap: the ring is between them by construction, and behind it is either the dark pit sheath or the scene itself. Nothing measured says it merges with anything, so tinting it per scene would be taste applied to twenty scenes at once.
- **Cost: nothing, and it should be nothing** — the change is one float written to a renderer property per frame and one `ModelInstance` filled, on a draw the cap already went through. Testbed against `main`, desert, pinned camera, `nopost nooverc sceneseed=0`, alternating builds, medians: at 1600×900 ssaa 2 both halves sat at **2.54 ms** across three pairs — which is the CPU submit ceiling rather than the frame's GPU cost, and is exactly the side a per-frame float write would show on; at 3840×1600 ssaa 2 (target 7680×3200, genuinely GPU-bound at 10.3 ms) **main 10.34 / 10.34 against 10.35 / 10.35**.
  - ⚠ **The `[build]` line cannot tell these two halves apart on its own, and that is a gap worth knowing.** `BuildStamp` stamps the **entry assembly** and the compiled shaders; `ArenaIsland` lives in `Prazsky.Core`, so a library-only change leaves `[build] Testbed.dll …` and the shader `set` hash both untouched. Here the two halves came from different worktrees so their `Testbed.dll` hashes differed anyway (`ec9eadca` against `34c006b1`) — but in a same-tree A/B of a library change, nothing on the line would say which build was running. Check the library's own `LastWriteTime` against the source until the stamp covers it.
- **What the references say to do instead, and why it is a follow-up.** The #441 island renders were asked for a gold band and the model drew one — by laying a **broad brushed-steel apron** between the red rock and the gold (`404-island-mars-v2`). That is the right answer and it is *geometry*: a cool plate ringing the drain gives the gold something to read against without changing the metal. It belongs with the rest of the dressing, not with a colour pass.

Three things it cost to get right, all worth knowing before touching any other stone surface:

- **`Ground_8.png` does not tile, and never did.** The stone covers only the left ~1073 columns of its 2048² canvas; the rest is pure black. The triplanar projection therefore multiplied roughly half of every surface it was mapped onto by zero — which is what left the island's wall reading as a black band, at *any* detail scale, since no scale avoids the black. Both members now use `Prazsky.Core.Render.SurfaceTexture` instead: procedural concrete and stone generated at load on a noise lattice that wraps at the texture edge, so they tile by construction, with a linear-space mip chain. No content-project change in either executable, and no asset to get wrong.
- **That broken texture was silently acting as an exposure control.** Same vantage, same nominal albedo, only the texture swapped: the top face measured 151 grey with the photograph and 181 with a texture whose mean is 1 by construction — half a stop brighter for a number nobody changed. **Any albedo carried over from a surface that used it arrives overexposed**; the island's came down accordingly. `SurfaceTexture.LinearMean` exists so `DetailBoost = 1 / LinearMean` makes the texture a pure modulation about 1, leaving the material colour the honest albedo and `DetailStrength` a pure contrast dial.
- **A neutral vertical surface in this world reads distinctly warm, and that is the light rig, not the material.** The key light is carried halfway to the horizon colour (`SkyLightRig.SKY_TINT_STRENGTH`) and half of the hemisphere ambient a vertical face sees is the ground bounce, which is that horizon again. Measured in one frame: the cannon's steel 89,57,35 and the island's concrete 82,51,31, with the city's facades in the same family. A first pass read this as a fault in the concrete and pushed its albedo blue to cancel it — which would have made the platform the one object in the scene not taking the light everything else does. The albedo is a plain concrete grey, within a hair of `CannonRig.STEEL_COLOR`. Two more dials that misled on the way: the relief is a **sum of sines**, and past about 0.02 it stops reading as a rough surface and starts reading as the waves it is made of (a regular diagonal weave across the whole drum, at 0.045); and because the **specular ambient is not multiplied by albedo**, it and not the material colour is the dial that decides how *polished* a face reads — halving the stone's colour barely dimmed a top face whose brightness was mostly that term.

**The arena is on screen for every second of every level, in all fifteen scenes, and until #151 nobody had measured it.** That is the whole reason the issue exists, and it holds whatever the number turns out to be: the island is the one piece of geometry the game is never without, and it had never been in a quality tier for the same reason it was never measured — it is not a scene. **Two method notes from that first round, both paid for and both still standing**: a camera must be screenshotted before a number taken through it is trusted (#151 was filed against `Sea.fx` on a view that turned out to be mostly island), and the sea's four-octave chop and 2000 spray billboards both measure at **zero**, the same not-ALU-bound answer #104 and the tier sweeps got.

**The figure this issue was opened on — roughly 27 ms of a 42 ms frame on the weakest machine — has now been measured there, and it does not hold: the arena is 10–14 % of that frame, not 64 %.** The original was a **residual, not a measurement**: 42.4 ms in the sea view, less the sea's own pixel shader (9.2, measured) and a 6.9 ms control frame, with everything left over called "the arena". Two things were wrong with the subtraction. The remainder also holds the gun, the ceiling plate, the crosshair, every draw call's own overhead and the terrain the island is standing on — and the 6.9 ms control was **not a sky-only frame at all**. `campos=0,0,0 camtarget=0,80,0` looks straight along the up vector, so `Matrix.CreateLookAt` degenerates and *nothing is drawn*: two different scenes through it produce byte-identical flat frames of the clear colour, while a camera tilted 14° off vertical from the same point draws the dome and its clouds normally and costs 30.2 ms against the degenerate frame's 7.8. What that control bounded is the post chain over an empty frame — which is a legitimate bound on the post chain, since it is full-screen and scene-independent, but it is not what the label says and it is not a bound on the frame's fixed costs.

**What it actually is, measured member by member on the machine the ratio was opened about** (Ryzen 7 5700U + integrated Radeon), Testbed, meadow, dome 13, `nocap`, windowed **1600×900 at ssaa 2**, the arena drawn nine ways *inside one process* (`alt=`, below), two runs with the cycle order reversed, median of the per-cycle differences:

| taken out or drawn flat | top-down, `campos=0,30,0 camtarget=0,-13,6` | play view, `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0` |
|---|---|---|
| *the frame itself* | *24.69 / 24.45 ms* | *26.53 / 26.01 ms* |
| **the whole arena** | **2.664 / 2.544 — 10.8 % / 10.4 %** | **3.776 / 3.685 — 14.2 % / 14.2 %** |
| the stone cap, shaded flat | 1.479 / 1.197 | 3.365 / 2.754 |
| the stone cap, not drawn | 1.514 / 1.352 | 2.949 / 2.555 |
| the glass | 0.470 / 0.324 | 0.362 / −0.447 |
| the pit | 0.354 / 0.353 | 0.362 / 0.114 |
| the concrete drum | 0.119 / 0.030 | 0.499 / 0.214 |
| the gold rims | 0.060 / 0.000 | 0.000 / −0.377 |

- **The cap is the arena here too** — about 80 % of it at the play camera — and every other member is at or under the noise, which is what says the class of machine does not change the *attribution* even though it changes every absolute. That is worth stating explicitly, because the benchmark skill's standing rule is that attribution does **not** travel between the desktop and the APU (#102 measured a cavern cut at nothing on the 6900 XT that #250 then measured at 20.7 → 17.9 ms here). This is a case where it happens to; it was checked rather than assumed.
- **The sign, not the size, is what separates signal from noise on this machine.** Each cycle gives one reading of every variant a second apart, so the per-cycle *differences* are what get medianed. Taking the whole arena out is cheaper in **96–100 %** of cycles; the drum and the rims land at **42–67 %**, i.e. a coin toss, which is what "measures at nothing" looks like when it is written down honestly.
- **Shading the cap flat and not drawing it at all come out within noise of each other, and at the play camera the flat one is *dearer*** — the same inversion the reference desktop measured, for the same reason: the cap occludes the terrain behind it, so removing it hands those pixels to the scene. The flat-colour probe stays the honest bound.
- **In the sea, at the top-down camera, the arena did not separate from noise at all** (0.193 ms, cheaper in 62 % of cycles, in a run whose own interquartile spread was ±2 ms). Removing the island there uncovers the sea, and the sea costs about what the island cost. So "what those three expensive views have in common is the arena" is not what the isolation says either — in the one scene the issue was originally filed against, the arena is nearly free *at the margin*.

**The instrument that made any of this measurable is `alt=`, and the machine is why it had to exist.** On the reference desktop #151 compared whole runs and its paired baselines landed within 0.15 ms. This laptop cannot do that: an integrated Radeon shares one 15 W package budget with the CPU, so its sustained clock moves with whatever else the box is doing, and two 125-second runs of *one unchanged variant* came back **33.6 and 25.7 ms** apart — a spread wider than the arena being measured. Worse, the drift is not a scale factor: while a stray process was hammering the machine, every variant compressed towards the same number, so a sweep taken then reads as "nothing costs anything". `alt=<members>[/<probe>];…` (`TestOptions.Alternation`) redraws the arena a different way on **every `[fps]` window**, so the variants share one process, one clock and the same neighbours, and the line already names the members and the probe so each reading labels its own variant. It is the same step `capprobe=` took — one build drawn several ways rather than several builds measured against each other — carried one further to one *run* drawn several ways. **Two operational notes that came with it:** the contention was real and named (`find / -iname …` had been pinning a core for three and a half hours; killing it moved the same pin from 26.7 to 24.5 ms), and the `[fps]` line's arena field prints a flags enum, so it is **itself comma-separated** — a harness regex that stops at the first comma silently drops every multi-member run, which cost one whole sweep before the logs were re-read rather than re-run.

**The isolation is done now, and it names the stone cap.** Measured on the reference desktop (Ryzen 9 5900X + RX 6900 XT), Testbed, meadow, dome 13, `nocap`, windowed 1600×900 at **ssaa 4** — 23.0 Mpix, within 7 % of what fullscreen 3840×1600 at ssaa 2 shades, so it is that machine's real `High` load in a small window — two passes a variant, each member taken out of an otherwise complete frame in turn (`arena=all,-cap` and so on, below):

| taken out | top-down, `campos=0,30,0 camtarget=0,-13,6` | play view, `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0` |
|---|---|---|
| the stone cap | **0.698 ms** | **1.091 ms** |
| the glass | 0.129 | −0.140 |
| the pit | 0.099 | 0.005 |
| the concrete drum | 0.038 | 0.004 |
| the gold rims | −0.007 | −0.016 |
| *the whole arena* | *0.989 of 8.25* | *1.240 of 7.74* |

**The cap is the arena** — 71 % of it from above, 88 % from the view a player actually has — and every other member lands at or under the run-to-run noise, the glass coming out slightly *negative* in one camera. So #151's own first suspect is refuted: the translucent `CullNone` drain over the opaque `CullNone` pit, right where the camera looks, costs nothing worth naming. The overdraw is real and is not what the frame is spent on. The drum's zero is a **coverage** result and not a shader one — neither camera the game ever uses sees much of a wall that faces outwards under the coping — so nothing here says the drum's shading is cheap, only that it is never asked for. What was left of the suspect list is the cap's own per-pixel work: `SurfaceReliefWorld`'s seven octaves feeding `PerturbNormalFromHeight`'s `ddx`/`ddy`, the triplanar detail's three taps and the procedural masonry joints, all on the one surface that fills the lower half of a play frame — and the last of those turned out not to be work the game had any use for.

**A quarter of the cap was drawing the castle that was deleted years ago.** `TriplanarPS` carried a construction-pattern block — coursed stone joints, sawn-timber board seams, and `WoodGrain`, a *second* seven-octave `SurfaceReliefWorld` — selected between by `lerp`s on `isPatterned` and `isWood`. Both were **always zero**: nothing in the repository ever set `MasonryStrength` off its `0` default and nothing ever called `SetMeshSurfaceStyle`, so every term was computed per pixel and multiplied away, on every surface of every executable. It is the remains of the castle backdrop that went with the square plaza; `ForestScatterRenderer`'s own comment had been declining to declare a timber style for bark, against an API that could no longer do anything. The shader's comment claimed a branch "would save nothing anyway", and the measurement disagrees. All of it is deleted — the block, `MasonryStrength`, `SurfaceStyle`, `SetMeshSurfaceStyle` and `PixelFootprint`, which went with its only caller. `MortarDepth` survives as `CavityHeadroom`, at the same 0.055: the cavity range is the one place the patterns reached that outlived them, and every surface in the game has been shaded through that number since the joints stopped being drawn.

**Measured as a paired A/B of two whole builds run alternately**, six pairs, order swapped within each pair (see below on why that mattered): **−0.260 ms**, sd 0.036, 95 % CI −0.29 to −0.23, the new build faster in 6 pairs of 6. With `arena=none` — the same two builds, the arena simply out of the frame — the same comparison gives **+0.006 ms, CI −0.036 to +0.048, and the new build faster in only 2 pairs of 4**, i.e. nothing: **the whole saving is the stone cap**, which falls from 1.09 ms to about 0.83. The look is unchanged and that is not an argument from the code: two captures under `nopost`, diffed pixel by pixel over the 386 224 px of cap, differ in 76 — 60 of them the overlay's own frame counters, and **not one pixel outside it by more than a single LSB of one channel**, which is float reassociation and not shading.

**Two measurement notes, both paid for here.** Unpaired runs of the two builds in separate sessions put the unchanged side at 7.744 and 7.517 ms — a spread as large as the effect — because a session an hour into hammering the GPU drifts; alternating the builds inside one run is what made a 0.26 ms difference legible at all. And one pair had to be thrown out and *said so*: its reading of 21.3 ms began at 127 FPS, matching its neighbour exactly, then collapsed to 35 and stayed there — the benchmark skill's own trap 6, a stray process, visible only because the per-second lines were kept rather than the average.

It is **fill-bound**, confirmed rather than assumed: the whole arena costs 0.242 ms at ssaa 2 against 0.989 at ssaa 4, a 4.09× for 4× the pixels. And the 27-of-42-ms figure above stays the weak machine's own — it has deliberately **not** been extrapolated onto this one, where the arena is 1.24 ms of a 7.74 ms frame, i.e. 16 %. What makes re-running it there a few minutes' work rather than a rebuild is `ArenaIsland.Members` and the Testbed's `arena=` argument, which is the third method note: a suspect nothing can take out of the frame is not a suspect that can be tested, and every candidate in this list had stood unfalsifiable for exactly that reason.

**And the cap's own pixel shader is 13.6 % of a play frame, of which only the relief can be cut.** Following the isolation above — the cap is the arena, and a quarter of it was the deleted castle — the last suspect list was `SurfaceReliefWorld`'s seven octaves into `PerturbNormalFromHeight`'s `ddx`/`ddy` and the triplanar detail's three taps. Both are testable now for the reason `arena=` made the members testable: the cap draws through one of five cut-down copies of its own pixel shader on demand (`capprobe=N`, `InstancedModelRenderer.TriplanarProbe`), so this is **one build measured several ways** rather than several builds measured against each other. Reference desktop, Testbed, meadow, dome 13, `nopost`, the play camera `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, windowed **1920×1080 at ssaa 4** — 33.2 Mpix, more than this machine's own fullscreen 3840×1600 at ssaa 2 — `fpscap=150`, four interleaved rounds with the order reversed on alternate rounds:

| the cap drawn as | ms | saving |
|---|---|---|
| the shipped `TriplanarPS` | 10.971 | — |
| three relief octaves instead of seven | 10.635 | **0.336** |
| the height field, without `PerturbNormalFromHeight` | 10.672 | 0.299 |
| no height field at all | 10.311 | **0.660** |
| one triplanar tap instead of three | 10.942 | 0.029 |
| no triplanar taps at all | 10.834 | 0.137 |
| a constant colour | 9.481 | **1.490** |
| *not drawn at all (`arena=all,-cap`)* | *9.767* | *1.204* |

- **The three taps are the fourth suspect this issue has named and measured at nothing.** 0.029 ms for two of the three, 0.137 for all three — against 0.660 for the height field. The texture units are not what this pass is waiting on.
- **The whole pixel shader is 1.490 ms of a 10.971 ms frame**, and the relief is 0.660 of it. The remaining ~0.83 is `ShadePixel` — the light rig every surface in the game is shaded through — which is not this issue's to cut.
- **Taking the cap out of the frame is *cheaper* for the frame than shading it flat, and that is not a contradiction**: 1.204 against 1.490, because the cap occludes the terrain behind it and removing it hands those pixels to the scene instead. So `arena=all,-cap` **understates** the cap, and the flat-colour probe is the honest bound on it. The member sweep above is a lower bound on every member for the same reason.
- **The saving follows the shaded pixel count**, which is what says it survives down to the tier that will use it. The same variants at **2560×1440 ssaa 4** — 59.0 Mpix, 1.78× the pixels — read 19.841 ms shipped, 19.317 coarse and 17.534 flat: the coarse saving grows 0.336 → **0.524** and the flat bound 1.490 → **2.307**, i.e. 1.56× and 1.55× for 1.78× the pixels. `Medium` and `Low` shade at 1× rather than 4×, so what they collect was put at about a quarter of the figures above — **derived from that scaling and not measured**, because `fpscap=150` (this desktop's own condition, see the benchmark skill) puts every ssaa 1 and ssaa 2 frame of this camera against the ceiling, where nothing can be read.
- **Measured since, on the machine the tier exists for, that entry buys nothing anyone can detect.** Weak machine, meadow, the play camera, windowed 1600×900 at **ssaa 1** — which is what `Medium` and `Low` actually shade — the coarse field against the shipped one reads **0.00 to 0.05 ms of a 13 ms frame, cheaper in 45–60 % of cycles**: a coin toss. At ssaa 2 it is the same answer (−0.12 / −0.06 top-down at 54–71 %, and *dearer* by 0.2–0.3 at the play camera). This is not a contradiction of the desktop's 0.336 ms — it is that figure's own scaling carried honestly to the end: 1600×900 at 1× shades 1.44 Mpix against the desktop pin's 33.2, and 0.336 ms scaled by that is hundredths of a millisecond. **What is wrong is the "about a quarter", which was the one step taken by arithmetic rather than by measurement**, and the tier's own cap is smaller still: the cap's *entire* pixel shader is 0.21–0.46 ms of that 13 ms frame, and the relief is a fraction of that. The entry costs a shipped frame nothing and it does pay at higher pixel counts, so it stays — but nothing here should be quoted as a saving this class of machine collects.
- **Dropping the height field entirely cannot ship, and the picture says so rather than taste.** `SlabGroove` is part of the same field, so the cap loses its coursed slab joints with it — the only structure the dressed stone had at a scale the eye can see (it has none since #550, and the coarser relief that replaced them is the structure now). Three octaves keep the joints and give up the finest grain: diffed over 106 600 px of stone under `nopost`, the coarse field moves a pixel by **2.9 of 255 on average** (ssaa 1; 3.4 at ssaa 2), against **10.5** for no field at all.

**So the arena finally has a quality-tier entry, and it is the first thing in the tier that is not a scene.** `ArenaIsland.SurfaceDetail` — 1 the authored look, below it the coarse field — wired from the Game's tier exactly as `SceneRenderer.SceneDetail` is, and reduced at **`Low` alone** since #298 (it was everything below `High`, which is what left nothing between the two lower rungs; handing it back to `Medium` is safe because it measures 0.00-0.05 ms there on the weak machine). The cap alone and deliberately not the concrete drum: the drum is triplanar too and its relief is most of what makes the two read as different materials at a distance, but it measures 0.004 ms because neither camera the game uses sees much of a wall that faces outwards under the coping — reducing it would trade a look for nothing. A second compiled technique (`InstancedModelTriplanarCoarse`) rather than a uniform, for the reason #155 measured and the glass bubble restates: a runtime branch over an alternative shading path costs the union of both register allocations in every wavefront of an occupancy-bound pass.

**Two things this round cost, both worth keeping.** A run whose window is **maximized** renders at the panel's size instead of the pinned one and reads as a 3× frame cost, with nothing on the `[fps]` line to say so except the size it names for exactly this purpose — it happened twice and it is why the harness now throws away any run whose back buffer is not the one asked for. And the same harness's per-second lines caught a run that opened at 91.8 FPS, matching its neighbours exactly, then collapsed to 31.5 and stayed there; a **median** of the per-second readings rather than a mean is what stops one of those from moving an answer, and the collapse is still visible in the low reading beside it.

Note `ReliefShadowStrength` and `ParallaxScale` were dropped from both renderers rather than carried over: the triplanar path builds its own height field and never runs the self-shadow or parallax marches, so both were dead where they were being set. Measured on `Dense20x10x15` in the city, uncapped: 188.2 FPS before the rework and 189.8 after — one extra draw call and a couple of thousand triangles against a 3000-ball scene.

### The island's shape per scene (#533)

**Since #533 the island's silhouette is a function of the scene as well as its material.** #404's two passes changed what the slab was *made of*; a desert, a volcano, a coral lagoon and deep space still stood the cannon on the same round slab with the same coping and the same profile. `IslandShape` (eight of them since #538, six at #533) is resolved beside the material — `ArenaIsland.ShapeFor(scene)` next to `LookFor` — and `IslandMesh` builds one cap-and-drum pair per shape from its own polyline:

- **Basalt** (the volcano): a flat-topped block cut in two hard tiers — the top tier's face and tread, the drum recessed a course under it and stepping back out to the foot — with the renderer's joint grid cut on the drum too (`ReliefFor`: `DrumSlab` 1.3, deep joints), which on a vertical face is vertical lines, which is **columns**. The #533 reference stood a parapet of columns *on* the block's top, the one thing no shape may do (below).
- **Ice** (the polar sheet, the aurora): a round lip turning over and *under* into a hollow the water has eaten out of the rim, the whole edge wandering by half a unit; no joints anywhere, a broad low relief. (#534 bent a joint grid into "fractures" on its top; the owner's ruling of 2026-09-24 took them out — below.)
- **Coral** (the tropical beach, the sea): a soft weathered nose with no crease below the floor, the drum a pitted lump; no joints, a pitted relief.
- **Machined** (space, the grid): the top flat out to a 45° chamfer, a true cylinder with one recessed groove, a stepped foot, no wander — a machined part is what the authored stone's wander exists to stop the island reading as, and here that is the point; panel lines on the top (the material's plate size) and the hull.
- **Plinth** (the two cities): a hard arris, a straight side battered barely at all, a shallow foot, a cast surface's trace of wander; expansion joints on the top, the side plain.
- **Monolith** (the outback, #538): a monolith's stub — the top rounds over a shoulder into one leaning sweep of rock down to a foot that splays back out, no coping, no course, no plinth, wandering half a unit — with the renderer's flutes cut down the side (`ReliefFor`: the drum's grid every 0.9 of a unit, 0.12 wide and 0.05 deep, bent 0.8 by `DrumWarp`, so they are runnels worn by water and no two run parallel — a runnel is not a tile) and **no joints on top**: one rock.
- **Pad** (the Moon, #538): a landing pad cut into regolith — a thin flat slab with a plain cut edge that barely wanders, and round its foot the spoil the cut threw up, banked against the edge and spreading three quarters of a unit **past the rim** onto the ground: the one shape whose foot stands outside the coping's radius, which the foot rule below allows (wider covers the terrain hole better, and the Moon's ground is at the foot's own height). No joints anywhere; **craters cut into the dish as geometry** (below).
- **Stone** (every other scene): the authored dressed stone and concrete, byte for byte the profile that was there — and since #550 an **outcrop of rock** rather than a paved floor: no joint grid on the top, a coarser relief (below).

**What a shape may and may not move, and why each rule exists.** The arris at `IslandMesh.FloorRadius` is the physics floor's edge (`FunnelPhysics.Build` is given it) and stays a true circle at y = 0 in every shape — its ring never wobbles, or the drawn floor would reach past the collision and a ball would sink through stone; nothing stands above y = 0 at the rim, or it would be a lip balls pass through. The bore's lip is where the drain's gold bead closes and never wobbles. The foot stays within a fifth of a unit of the rim at the underside — or outside it, as the pad's spoil bank does (#538) — because it covers the hole the terrain scenes cut under the platform (`TERRAIN_HOLE_RADIUS`). `ArenaIsland.RADIUS`, `TOP_Y` and `FLOOR_RADIUS` are untouched, so `GameCameraFit`, the gun's walk, the drain and the ceiling plate read what they always read. A rim that wanders wanders on the cap and the drum alike, by one amplitude and the same per-ring wobble at their shared point — `LatheMesh`'s seam rule.

**How the swap costs nothing.** All six pairs are built at load (twelve lathes at 128 facets), and `InstancedModelRenderer.SetMesh` re-points the one cap renderer and the one drum renderer at the scene's pair when the shape changes — so the material, relief and joint settings, and the renderers' places in every host's sky-lit list, are untouched, and a scene switch is two buffer swaps. The relief and joints per shape are written per draw like the material (eight field writes). **Measured against `main`** (Testbed, the volcano — the shape with the most joints — at the play pose `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, 3840×1600, `nopost nooverc fpscap=400`, 20-second windows with the first four readings dropped, two alternating pairs out of two copies of the exe whose `[build]` library stamps differ): **12.23 and 12.34 ms → 12.31 and 12.28**, +0.08 and −0.06 ms — the sign does not hold across the pair, which is the measurement floor, as a lathe of the same size under the same renderer predicts.

**Photographed from one Testbed camera in one scene per family, before and after, with the references beside them** (five prompts, Z-Image-Turbo, in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\533`, not in the repository): the basalt's tiers read as a stepped block of columns, the floe's rim as broken ice, the coral as a worn lump, the disc as machined, the plinth as poured — and the meadow's stone as exactly what it was. The dressing on top of each shape is #534–#538.

### The island dressed: the volcanic and underground family (#535)

**Two terms on the triplanar surface, and the volcano's and the cavern's islands are dressed in them.** `InstancedModel.fx`'s two shipping triplanar techniques (`TriplanarPS` and the coarse one the lower tiers draw — not the cost probes, which share their lines verbatim and must stay what they measure) took `JointGlow` — linear radiance the slab joints' *floors* put out, read at the groove squared so the bevel stays dark and only the deepest part of a crack is incandescent — and `TopDustTint`/`TopDustStrength`, a modulation of the albedo on up-facing faces keyed to the geometric normal, so a slope takes less, a wall none, and the relief's grain does not put dust on and off across the top. Both are zero by default; the glow sits behind a `[branch]` on its uniform with the groove's derivatives taken outside it, and the dust is one lerp. `ArenaIsland.DressingFor(scene)` states them beside the material and the shape:

- **The volcano**: the basalt block's joints are **cooling cracks** — the cap's at 0.3 of `LavaCool` (a crust's glow, not a river's), the drum's columns dimmer — and **every burst of the eruption pushes light up through them**: the hosts write `ArenaIsland.EventGlow` from `SceneRenderer.VolcanoEruption` on the same wall clock the crater's lamp and the thunder read, so the cracks brighten with the crater and never on a beat of their own (`EventGain` 1.5: two and a half times the crust glow at a full burst). **Ash** on the top — a warm grey over the black basalt, half the surface — so the block stands in the fallout it stands in. The reference (`C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\535`, not in the repository) drew exactly this: red seams between dark slabs, grey ash in patches, a stepped rim of columns.
- **The cavern**: the joints carry the walls' own **mineral veins** — `VeinColor` half again over the walls', steady, on the cap and the drum — and the cap is **wet**: `CapPolish` 0.30 → 0.48, `DrumPolish` 0.20 → 0.36, so the river's and the crystals' light stands on it as a sheen rather than a dampness. No dust. The reference drew a dripping wet slab with cyan veins in its cracks and the crystals' colours lying on it, which is what those two dials produce.
  - **⚠ In patches, not along every joint.** The first cut lit the joints along their whole length and photographed as a **neon grid** laid over the stone — every joint of the cap and the columns glowing evenly, a circuit board. A cooling crack glows where the crust is thinnest and a vein runs in some fractures and not others, so a low world-space noise (`GradientNoise3` at 0.23 per unit) gates the glow: a stretch of a joint burns, fades and goes dark along the line, about a third of the grid lit, and the intensities came down by a third with it (the cap's cooling cracks at 0.3 of `LavaCool`).
- **Not done, and named so nobody mistakes it for an oversight:** a crystal or two grown against the cavern island's drum (the clusters are SDFs in `Cavern.fx`, and a mesh crystal is a new kind on the island), a rim *chipped* rather than turned (the #533 basalt's tiers and wander are what stands in for it), and dripping water.
- **The drain ring keeps its contrast**, measured as #404's second pass measured it (the ring camera `campos=0,-1,24 camtarget=0,-9,0`, 1216×832, `nopost nooverc sceneseed=0`, two stations left and right on the band — checked on a marked-up frame — CIEDE2000 against the cap beside each, mean of the two): **volcano 35.6 → 33.6 dE** (the ash lightens the black cap a little; the meadow the drain was designed on measures 15.8), **cavern 35.4 → 35.3**. Neither is near the 13 the desert failed at.
- **Measured against `main`** (Testbed, the volcano at the play pose `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0` — the island filling the lower half of the frame — at 3840×1600, `nopost nooverc fpscap=400`, 20-second windows with the first four readings dropped, two alternating pairs out of two copies of the exe whose shader `set` stamps differ): **12.26 and 12.29 ms → 12.53 and 12.50**, +0.27 and +0.21 ms. That is the glow branch taken on every island pixel in the two dressed scenes (a second `SlabGroove` and one `GradientNoise3`); in the other eighteen the branch skips and the dust is one lerp, so they pay nothing measurable.
- **Photographed** from the #533 camera in both scenes before and after, and from the ring camera, with the references beside them: https://claude.ai/artifact/6Us179KKyrVvDtmHtp6SvU

### The island dressed: the built family (#537)

**A seam in a made thing is lit evenly; a crack in a rock is lit where it is thinnest.** #535's joint glow lit about a third of the grid in patches, and that is the whole difference between the two families — so `JointGlowPatchiness` (a renderer dial, a shader uniform, 1 by default) says how much of the glow the world-space patch noise gates, and the built family's dressings set it to 0.

- **The grid**: the disc's seams carry the plane's own **vector palette** — the top's a shade over `GridSceneConfig.LineColor`, the hull's panel lines the line colour itself — every seam lit along its whole length, so the platform is a plate of the world it stands in rather than a slab lowered onto a Tron floor.
- **Space**: the station plates carry faint cool **running light** along their seams (0.11/0.13/0.17 on the top, dimmer on the hull — the first cut, at twice that, photographed brighter than the grid's own seams), well under the grid's — a station is lit by a sun, and its seams are marked, not blazing.
- **The neon city**: the dark granite plinth is **wet after rain** — `CapPolish` 0.42 → 0.62, `DrumPolish` 0.28 → 0.40 — so the towers' signs mirror into the top; `SceneLights` already put the neon on it, and the polish is what lets the top show it.
- **The day city**: the poured plinth's expansion joints (#533) *are* its dressing — a plaza plinth is plain. **Not done, and why:** the issue's steel edge rail and service hatch are meshes, and a rail round the rim is the one thing the shape rules forbid — it stands above the floor's plane outside the physics floor's arris, so a ball rolling off the coping would pass through it (the #533 rule); a hatch flush in the top is a decal the triplanar path has no channel for yet. The emissive *edge strip* round the rim the issue names is the same shape problem — the joint grid is world X/Z and cannot draw a ring — and would want a ring in the lathe's own profile (a groove with its own glow), which is worth doing only if the seams alone do not read.
- **The drain ring's contrast is untouched** (the #404 ring camera, two stations left and right on the band, CIEDE2000 against the cap beside each, mean of the two): **space 24.9 → 24.9 dE, the grid 48.9 → 48.9** — the seams glow between the stations, not under them. The ring *as a fitting in the plate rather than a gold band* is not done: the gold is the one piece of furniture the game keeps in all twenty (#404's second pass), and a steel fitting would be a per-scene metal, which that pass measured as bleaching worse than gold under a bright sky.
- **Measured against `main`** (Testbed, the grid — every seam lit — at the play pose `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, 3840×1600, `nopost nooverc`, 20-second windows with the first four readings dropped, two alternating pairs out of two copies of the exe whose shader `set` stamps differ): **1.82 and 1.82 ms → 2.04 and 2.04, +0.22 ms** on both pairs — the same glow branch on every island pixel #535 measured at +0.21–0.27 on the volcano. ⚠ **The first pair was taken at `fpscap=400` and read 2.50 ms on both builds**, which is the cap and not the scene: the grid is the cheapest backdrop in the game and idles under 2.5 ms, so the capped recipe every other pass uses measures nothing here. `nocap` is the instrument for a scene under the cap — #404's own drain pass hit the same floor at 2.54 ms and said so.
- **Photographed** from the #533 camera in all four scenes before and after, and from the ring camera on space and the grid, with the references beside them (four prompts, `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\537`, not in the repository): https://claude.ai/artifact/TMAXzTJsQZ9uQH4QfBRFXd

### The island dressed: the cold family (#534)

**⚠ The fractures described below are gone (the owner's ruling, 2026-09-24): a joint grid bent by a noise is *wavy paving* whatever it is called — "vlnité spáry na dlaždičkách, to je blbost" — so the ice tops carry no joints again, as #533 left them, and the faint light in the fractures went with them (`DressingFor` zeroes it; the rime on the drums stays). `SlabWarp` stays in the shader and the renderer for the one thing that may wander, a runnel on a drum (#538's monolith). The paragraph is kept as the record of what was tried and how it was measured.**

**Ice standing in ice, on both sheets.** The floe (#533) had no joints at all; a sheet of ice is not jointless, it is *fractured* — a net of curved lines round cells of about one size — and that net is the paving grid bent: `SlabWarp` (a renderer dial and a shader uniform, 0 by default) offsets the grid's own coordinates by a low world-space noise before the joint is cut, two reads of a 2D noise per pixel of a surface that asks for it, one per axis, behind a `[branch]` on the uniform. At 2.4-unit cells and 0.9 of a unit of warp the lines wander and the cells lose their corners, and thin, shallow (0.03 wide, 0.02 deep) they are lines *in* the ice rather than grooves cut into it. Two more dials came with it: a faint cold light in the fractures (`JointGlow`, in patches — a crack is lit where the ice behind it is thin) and **rime** on the drum — `SideDustTint`/`SideDustStrength`, the mirror of #535's top dust keyed to how far the geometric normal is from vertical, crusting the vertical faces most of the way to white.

- **The polar sheet**: the glacier cap's fractures carry a cold blue (0.05/0.11/0.18), the drum wears rime at 0.7 over its blue-white.
- **The aurora**: the island was *frosted stone* (#404) and is **lake ice** since #534 — the issue's own words are "ice standing in ice for the polar sheet and the aurora" — a shade darker and greener than the glacier (cap 0.70/0.78/0.84, polish 0.36), with the same fractures, a green-blue light in them, and the same rime. The curtains' colour lands on it through the aurora's own rig as it did on the stone (#462's `GlowTint`), so the cap takes the sky's green the way the snow does.
- **Not done, and why:** the frost *bloom along the top's edges* the issue names is a rim-band on the cap, which neither dust term can address (they are keyed to the normal, not to the radius) — it wants a radial term in the triplanar path, and is the one thing the references show that this pass does not; the drain ring *as a metal fitting sunk into ice* is #404's ruling again (the gold stays in all twenty; a steel fitting bleaches worse under a bright sky, measured there).
- **The drain ring's contrast** (the #404 ring camera, two stations left and right on the band, CIEDE2000 against the cap beside each, mean of the two): **polar 20.5 → 20.8 dE** (unchanged — the fractures run between the stations), **aurora 27.6 → 34.8** (the ice cap is paler than the frosted stone was, and the gold separates from it better). Both well above the 13 the desert failed at.
- **Cost** (Testbed, the polar sheet at the play pose, 3840×1600, `nopost nooverc`, uncapped — the ice sits under the 400 Hz cap like the grid, so `fpscap=400` would read the cap — 20 s windows, alternating pairs against a copy of `main`'s binary after #537): **+0.35 ms** on a 12.25 ms frame (+0.37 / +0.34 over two pairs). It read **+1.1 ms** first, because the joint glow (#535) evaluated the slab groove a *second* time and with the warp that was two more noise reads on every island pixel — the height field now hands the groove out (`SceneSurfaceHeightGroove`, `…CoarseGroove`) and the glow reuses it (+0.5 ms), and the warp's reads went from a 3D noise to a 2D one on XZ (+0.35 ms), photographed identical from both cameras. What is left is the two reads and the rime's lerp, in the two ice scenes only.
- **Photographed** from the #533 camera in both scenes before and after, and from the ring camera, with the references beside them (two prompts, `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\534`, not in the repository): https://claude.ai/artifact/4RcNg1EqgyiTZsXnkVxjvx

### The island dressed: the coastal family (#536)

**Two terms keyed to HEIGHT, on the side faces**, because what marks rock at the sea is where the water reaches, and neither dust term can say that (they are keyed to the normal). `ApplyHeightBands` in `InstancedModel.fx`, called from the two shipping triplanar techniques only:

- **A band** under `BandTopY`, fading out over `BandFade` above it along a line two crossed sines wander, so it is a tide mark and not a ruled one: an albedo ratio (`BandTint`, the dusts' convention) and `BandWet`, how many times over the band mirrors the sky — handed to `ShadePixel` as extra `SurfaceSpecular.Environment`, so a wet face is a mirror and a dry one is untouched to the bit.
- **Bedding planes** (`StrataSpacing`, `StrataStrength`): layers a spacing thick, each a course of its own shade with a dark seam where two meet, the planes dipping and wandering a little across the stack, faded to their mean as a layer shrinks towards a few pixels.

Both sit behind data branches on the uniform **and on the side weight**, so the island's flat top — most of its pixels from any play camera — pays nothing; the first cut ran them on every island pixel with gradient noise for the wanders and measured about a millisecond on the APU (below). `ArenaIsland.DressingFor` states them per scene, reading the water and the sand off the scenes' own configs (`SeaSceneConfig.LevelY`, `TropicalTerrainConfig.LevelY`) rather than restating them, so a tide line cannot drift off the sea it marks. The island's foot is at −13.5: half a unit under the sea's mean level, and exactly on the beach's dry sand.

- **The sea: a sea stack.** The island was the authored pale limestone (#404 left the sea untinted) and is now **grey-brown layered rock** (cap 0.50/0.47/0.42, drum 0.46/0.43/0.38, polish 0.22/0.14 — spray keeps the top damp), its sides **in beds** 0.6 of a unit thick and darkened up to 0.6, with a **tide line** 1.5 units over the sea's mean level: below it the rock is weed-dark (0.09/0.11/0.08) and wet, mirroring four times the sky. The coral shape (#533) cuts no joints, so the top is plain rock.
- **The beach: a weathered foot.** The issue asked for a sand crust round the coral platform's foot, and the first cut drew one and **photographed as nothing**: the beach's own sand (`TropicalSceneConfig.SandColor`, 0.42/0.37/0.29 linear) is the drum's very tone. What reads instead is the grey the old high-water line leaves on coral rock at a beach — a band a unit up from the sand, 0.44/0.42/0.36, a little damp — and a fifth of the top dusted with blown sand. No waterline proper: the island stands on dry sand two units over the lagoon, and a tide mark on its drum would say it stands in water.
- **Not done, and why:** barnacles (a speckle the triplanar path has no channel for — a hash dot field on the band would be one, and the band is dark enough that it did not seem to need it), shells or a coconut against the drum (the #445 dressing already stands round it on the sand), and a notched *profile* at the tide line (geometry — #533's coral shape is the place for it).
- **The drain ring's contrast** (the #404 ring camera `campos=0,-1,24 camtarget=0,-9,0`, 1216×832, `nopost nooverc sceneseed=0`, two stations — the band's right side and its far side, each against the cap beside it — CIEDE2000, mean of the two): **sea 21.0 → 20.9 dE, beach 21.5 → 21.0** — unchanged; the dressing is on the sides and the ring is on the top.
- **Cost** (Testbed on the reference APU, the sea at the play pose `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, 1600×900 ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc nocap`, 25 s windows with the first four readings dropped, three alternating pairs against a copy of `main`'s binary): **+0.07, +0.11 and +0.63 ms** on a 24 ms frame — the first pair's figure against the other two's reads as the run order rather than the build. **The first cut measured +0.94 to +1.41** with the branches taken on every island pixel and gradient noise in both wanders. The meadow, which carries neither term, measured +0.04 and +0.13 against `main` — so the extra code in the shader costs the other scenes nothing measurable.
- **Photographed** from main and the change at one Testbed camera per view (a low one on the lit side of each drum, and a wide one over the sea stack), no references: the Z-Image references are on the desktop and this pass was made on the laptop, from the issue's own words.

### The island dressed: the off-world and arid family (#538)

**The last of the five families.** As merged (1b16d28f) it had four shader mechanisms; **the owner's two rulings of the same day** — joints between tiles never wander, and a crater is a hole, not a texture — took two of them out again within the hour, and the geometry that replaced the second is the part worth reading. What is on `main` now:

- **Craters as geometry** (`IslandMesh.PadCraters`, `LatheMesh`'s new `heightField`): the lunar pad's dish is lathed in rings 0.22 of a unit apart at four times the facets (512), and a height field lifts every vertex — a fixed scatter of up to sixteen bowls between the bore's lip and the floor's arris, many small and a few big (the radius a power of a uniform), none overlapping, placed entirely inside the two rings and faded to nothing over the last half unit besides, so the lip the drain's bead closes on and the arris the physics floor ends at stay exact circles. Each is a parabolic bowl with a raised rim on a Gaussian astride its edge; the depth is capped at 0.20 of a unit, so a ball rolling over the biggest floats by well under half its radius for the frames it takes to cross — the physics floor is the plain dish. The normals come from the positions actually drawn, so a crater shades and silhouettes as a hole. **The first cut drew them in the shader's height field** (one hash per evaluation, a bowl and a rim, on all four height functions) and the owner saw them smeared over the slab's edge like a decal: "a crater is never bent — it is a hole." That code is gone.
- **The top dust blown clear round the axis** (`TopDustClear`: a radius and a fade): #535's top dust weighted to nothing inside a ring round the drain — the lunar pad's powder, swept off where the engines were. The island stands on the world's axis, so the distance is the position's own.
