- ~~**The height band's windward rise**~~ — the desert's drift as a band of tint rising on the windward face. Gone with #550: the drift is geometry now (`SandDriftMesh`, below), and the rise, its uniform and its dial went with it.
- **No dust in the joints, and no bent grids.** The merged cut put sand in the desert top's joints and dust in Mars's, and bent both grids into "fractures" (`SceneRelief`) so the dust would lie in cracks rather than in grout — and that is wavy paving, the first ruling. Both tops now have **no joints at all** (`SceneRelief` sets the slab to 0 for Mars and the desert: rock and sandstone, not a laid floor) and wear their dust and sand as a film (`TopDust`, 0.30 and 0.35); the joint-dust term is deleted from the shader and the renderer, since no scene is left to use it. The owner's own word on the paving that remains on the authored stone: it was never a design — an early shader of a castle no longer in the game left it — so removing it from the stone island too (an outcrop of rock instead) is an open offer, not yet done.

Per scene (`LookFor`, `ShapeFor`, `ReliefFor`, `DressingFor`):

- **The Moon: a landing pad.** The `Pad` shape above; the regolith's pale grey (0.64/0.63/0.62) settled over half the top and blown clear from three units past the funnel's mouth, fading back over three more; craters cut into the dish; no joints.
- **Mars: layered rust rock.** The top a **darker weathered crust** (0.44/0.30/0.22) over a drum of the fresh rock (0.56/0.41/0.31, the old cap colour), the drum **in beds** every half unit (#536's strata, darkened 0.5) like the plain's mesas, and a film of the plain's fine dust (0.72/0.56/0.42, `MarsSceneConfig.RustColorPale`'s tone) over the crust at 0.30; no joints.
- **The outback: a monolith's stub.** The `Monolith` shape and its flutes, no joints on top; the red sandstone unchanged; nothing lies on Uluru.
- **The desert: half-buried.** A film of the dunes' pale sand (0.86/0.70/0.50, `SandColorPale`'s tone) over the top at 0.35, no joints — and the **drift against the drum is a mesh** since #550 (below); #538 had drawn it as a band of tint rising on the windward face, and by the second ruling a shape is geometry.
- **Two cuts, and what the first photographed as.** The craters, one a cell with a little jitter, read as a **lattice of dimples**; Mars's dust in the authored stone's square paving read as **tiles with pale grout**, and the desert's sand in the joints the same; and the desert looked nearly unchanged from the #533 camera, because the drift lies on the windward face, which that camera does not see. The second cut is what is listed above: half the crater cells empty, a third of a cell of jitter and a 3:1 size range; `SceneRelief(scene, relief)` — a per-scene bend on the cap grid over the shape's relief, for the two scenes that stand on the authored stone (Mars a 3.2 cell bent 1.1, the desert 2.8 bent 0.8) — so the dust lies in fractures; and the desert photographed from the windward side as well (`campos=-30,-2,-34`).
- **Not done, and why:** the desert's drift as *geometry* (a mesh of sand against the foot — the tint reads as a sandier lower drum, not as a dune; the follow-up if the owner wants a dune); the Moon's rim of spoil is the `Pad` shape's bank, not a ring of compacted regolith on the *top* (nothing may stand above y = 0 at the rim — a ball would pass through it); the outback's *desert varnish* streaks down the flutes (a directional dark stain the triplanar path has no channel for yet); the Martian rock's strata are #536's shading, not ledges in the profile.
- **The drain ring's contrast** (the #404 ring camera, two stations left and right on the band, CIEDE2000 against the cap beside each, mean of the two): **Moon 21.7 → 21.5 dE, Mars 24.3 → 36.1** (the darker crust separates the gold far better), **outback 25.5 → 26.2, desert 12.6 → 13.4** — the desert is #404's borderline case and stays where that pass left it (13.3 there); the dressing is on the sides and in the joints, and the ring is on the top.
- **Cost** (Testbed at the play pose, 3840×1600, `nopost nooverc`, uncapped, 20 s windows, two alternating pairs against a copy of `main`'s binary): **the Moon +0.01 / +0.03 ms** on a 5.82 ms frame — the craters and the clear ring are as good as free, the pad cuts no joints so its groove is the early return; **the desert +0.28 / +0.28 ms** on a 9.34 ms frame — the cap grid's bend (#534's two 2D noise reads, which the desert did not pay before), the joint dust and the band on the drum. The two ice scenes paid the same bend at +0.35 (#534).
- **Photographed** from the #533 camera in all four scenes before and after, and from the ring camera, with the references beside them (four prompts, one seed each, `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\538`, not in the repository): https://claude.ai/artifact/JYKRNt44B6mtRJe9rvc5EA

### The island without its paving, and the desert's drift as geometry (#550)

**The paving was never a design.** The slab coursing on the authored stone — the top every scene without a shape of its own stands on — came out of an early shader of a castle no longer in the game, modelled by hand on a tablet in 2016, and survived every pass since as if it were a decision; the owner's word on 2026-09-24, after the rulings above, was that nothing about it needed keeping, and that the future is not made by preserving the past. So:

- **The stone island is an outcrop of rock** (`STONE_RELIEF`): no joint grid on the top, and a coarser, deeper relief in its place — three and a half waves a unit at 0.022 against the paving's nine at 0.008 — the undulation a worn rock surface has, which is what breaks the light over a top that no longer has joints to do it. The drum is unchanged. Six scenes change with it: the meadow, the mountains, the forest, the storm, the savanna, the dream; the plinth's expansion joints (the cities) and the machined disc's panel lines (space, the grid) are designed and stay, and `IslandLook.Slab` is read by those two shapes only now. `SceneRelief` (#538's override that took the joints off Mars and the desert) is gone: the stone has none for any scene.
- **The desert's drift is a mesh** (`SandDriftMesh`, under its own `InstancedModelRenderer` in `ArenaIsland`, drawn after the drum in the desert only, in the sky-lit list; it receives the sun's map and does not cast into it — a low sheet under a grazing sun shadowed itself through the map's texel step as a checker of acne): a bank of sand against the face the scene's own wind arrives from (`DesertSceneConfig.Wind`, reversed), 2.6 units up that face, running six and a half out onto the clearing's flat sand in the concave slope a slip face has, over 220° of the drum and tapering to nothing at its ends — one sheet, its inner edge inside the drum's surface so no seam can open whatever the drum's wobble does, its toe sunk a third of a unit so it never floats over a grain of relief, wind ripples across it faint and fading at the toe, normals from the sheet's own tangents. In the dunes' pale sand with the stone's grain at a finer scale, dull, no joints. The tint that stood for it (#538's windward rise of the height band) is deleted with its uniform and its dial.
- **The drain ring's contrast** (the #404 ring camera, CIEDE2000 against the cap beside each of two stations, mean): **meadow 15.8 → 16.7 dE, mountain 18.1 → 18.8, forest 18.7 → 19.8, storm 22.0 → 23.2, savanna 14.6 → 13.9, dream 31.7 → 31.7, desert 14.7 → 13.9** — within a dE of where they were; the savanna and the desert sit where #404's pass left them. The drift on the windward side is dark in the lit frame because that face is in the island's own shadow; with the sun's shadows off it is its own sand colour.
- **Photographed** from the #533 camera and the ring camera in all seven scenes before and after, and the desert from its windward side: https://claude.ai/artifact/HpPe36QRfBEPWyNLC7MM3A

## The drain funnel (Testbed)

Where the recessed square used to be, the centre of the arena is a **glass funnel** the shot balls fall into: they run down the cone to a hole at the bottom and drop through it, below the platform, into the drop — where the kill plane removes them. It is what replaced the flat bath that just let balls pile up.

- **Rendering** is `Prazsky.Core.Render.FunnelMesh` — a single-sided truncated-cone wall (a round rim flush with the stone dish's low inner lip, `ArenaIsland.TOP_Y − DISH_DEPTH`, down to a small hole at `ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_BOTTOM_Y`, which the dish takes nothing off) with inward-and-up normals, and **no collar** now (`squareHalf` 0): the round stone dish meets the round rim directly, so there are no square corners to fill. The rim's height below the stone's arris is `ArenaIsland.DISH_DEPTH`, the one figure the dish added — the drain's mouth, the pit's mouth and the dish's lip stay one plane by construction, carried by the drain family's own world matrix (`_drainWorld`), while the island's mesh keeps the arris plane (`TOP_Y`). It is drawn translucent through its own `InstancedModelRenderer` at `ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_GLASS_ALPHA` (~0.55, more opaque than plain glass, so the funnel reads clearly as a frosted-glass drain). Because it is one open surface it is drawn with `RasterizerState.CullNone` so it reads both looking down into it and up through the hole, and it is one of the four renderers `ArenaIsland` hands the caller's sky-lighting pass (`SkyLitRenderers`/`AppendSkyLitTo`) so its sheen takes the dome. **Its back faces flip their shading normal** (`TwoSidedNormals`, an `InstancedModelRenderer` property mirrored as a `MainPS` uniform, #291): a one-sided wall drawn `CullNone` used to shade its *outside* with the inside's normal, and under the grazing-angle sky sheen the cone read from below — the open-below scenes, and the very shot the drop cinematic's dive films — as a bright, nearly opaque milky sheet instead of the frosted glass it is from above. The uniform is 0 for every other renderer (a closed mesh never shows a back face worth shading), set unconditionally per draw like `Metalness`, and the pit sets it too.
- The rim (radius `ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_TOP_RADIUS`, 14) meets the stone dish's inner lip exactly, and the gold top-rim bead rings that stone-meets-glass junction — `DISH_DEPTH` below the stone's arris now, sunk at the centre of the dish — so the drain reads at a glance where the two materials meet. (The old square-collar machinery that filled the corner gaps between the round funnel and the square pit is gone with the square plaza.)
- **Gold rims, and they lie flat on the surface rather than standing on it (#94).** A polished-gold band runs around **both circles** — the wide top rim and the small bottom hole — which is what makes the glass drain read at a glance (`FunnelRimsMesh`: two bands in one mesh, at the funnel's own local y = 0 and y = -height, so they share the drain family's world matrix — `TOP_Y − DISH_DEPTH`, the glass and the pit under the same one; one gold `InstancedModelRenderer` draws them). Each was a **torus** until #94, a round bead revolved around its own tube as well as around the ring, and the shape was wrong for where it sits: it stood a half-unit proud at the exact junction every released ball is *guaranteed* to cross on its way into the drain, and nothing underneath it agreed — `FunnelPhysics.Build` collides the smooth cone and the smooth dished stone and says so itself, so the bead was a lip a ball rolled straight through. Each band now lies **on the surface that actually meets it there**: the top one on the stone dish, tilted to `ArenaIsland.DISH_GRADE` (derived from `DISH_DEPTH` over the dish's run, so it cannot drift from `FloorHeightAt`), the bottom one on the glass cone at the cone's own grade, which `FunnelRimsMesh` works out from the three figures that already describe it — which is why only the dish's grade is a constructor argument. Each is a **shallow crown with buried edges** since #109: its two edges sink `EDGE_SINK` (0.04) *into* the surfaces they meet and its mid-width rises `SURFACE_LIFT` (0.05, a tenth of a ball radius) above them. The lift exists because coplanar geometry z-fights across the ring's whole width rather than at a fringe — but a whole band *floating* at that lift, which is what #94 first shipped, showed a sliver of the surface under its inner edge from the shallow angle the play camera actually has: a stone/shadow strip between the gold and the glass, at the very junction the gold exists to mark (#109's report). A buried edge cannot be seen under from any angle, the crown keeps the lift so nothing is coplanar, and each ring's normal is taken from the cross-section's own polyline (so a band's shading cannot disagree with the shape it was given, which matters now that the top band turns a crease mid-run) — which incidentally gives back a little of the glint the tori had, the inner and outer halves catching the light differently. **⚠ At the MOUTH that fix bought the very strip it was buying off (#237), and the arithmetic says why.** Burying an edge means the surface is drawn in FRONT of the gold there, so the gold's visible boundary moves inward from the edge to `halfWidth × EDGE_SINK / (EDGE_SINK + SURFACE_LIFT)` — **0.22 units of bare stone** ringing the mouth between the glass and the gold. #109 saw a stone strip under a floating edge and buried the edge; burying it drew a stone strip *over* the edge instead. The two states look the same and neither is the answer: **the stone has to be covered, not hidden behind.** So the top band now **wraps the lip** — past its mouth ring it turns the crease and runs `COLLAR_DROP` (0.35) on down the glass cone, ending sunk into the *glass* rather than into the stone. There is no longer a radius at the mouth where stone is the front-most thing, and the collar's own buried edge sits inside the throat under translucent glass, which shows it as a soft gold under-lip instead of hiding it — the trim piece #237 asked for in as many words. The mouth ring itself is neither buried nor flush but stands `LIP_LIFT` (0.012) off the stone, because the lip is the crease where the dish meets the cone and a ring sitting exactly on it is coplanar with both. **The mechanism was measured before anything was built:** zeroing `EDGE_SINK` made the strip vanish outright, which is what named it. Widths carry the tori's read across (`FUNNEL_RIM_TOP_WIDTH` 1.0 = the old top bead's diameter, `FUNNEL_RIM_HOLE_WIDTH` 0.6), and it is still far less geometry — a few rings of quads against two double-revolved tori. The bead's full roundness stays given up, and rightly — the colour and the metalness are what make the drain legible, not the cross-section. They are **opaque**, so they draw with the opaque scene *before* the glass, and `CullNone` — which is now load-bearing rather than merely convenient: a zero-thickness annulus has one face, and culling off is what lets the top band read from under the island as well as from above it. `ArenaIsland.DrawGlass` puts both draws inside **one** `CullNone` sandwich, restored to `CullCounterClockwise` after, which is image-identical to the Testbed's two separate pairs because the two draws want culling off for different reasons and one setting satisfies both. They are drawn **metallic**, which needed a shader addition: `InstancedModel.fx` now has a `Metalness` uniform (a renderer property, set unconditionally every `Draw` like `SpecularAmbientStrength`, **0 for every other surface** so nothing else changes) that makes the environment-reflection F0 the material's specular color itself rather than the 4 % dielectric `DielectricF0 * tint` — so gold reflects the sky *in gold*. The gold specular (`ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_RIM_SPECULAR`) is passed as a per-draw `BasicEffectParams` override; the gold diffuse (`ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_RIM_COLOR`) keeps the bead visible even under a dark dome. The rims are visual only — no physics; the stone dish and the cone are the floor, which since #94 is what their geometry says too.
- **Physics** is a Bepu triangle-`Mesh` (`Prazsky.BS3D.Physics.FunnelPhysics.Build`, one copy for both executables since #75) — the island's whole physics floor now that the wide grid of ground blocks is gone: the sloped cone wall plus the **dished stone ring** from the rim up to `ArenaIsland.FLOOR_RADIUS`, all **double-sided** — eight triangles a segment, every quad's two added in both windings — deliberately, so a ball collides with the surface regardless of Bepu's one-sided-triangle normal convention rather than depending on getting the winding right. It is handed the very figures `ArenaIsland` draws from, `DISH_DEPTH` included, so the drawn surface and the collided one cannot drift. A ball that lands on the ring **rolls down its dish into the glass** — Bepu spheres carry no rolling resistance (friction resists sliding, not rolling), so the ~6.4° grade is plenty — then within the rim it runs down the cone to the hole (wider than a ball) and falls through; past the ring it drops off the island's edge into the drop and the kill plane takes it. The dish is what made the drain work for big maps at all: on the flat ring a ball parked where it stopped, and the wider the field the larger the share of released balls that never reached the mouth's radius 14. `KILL_PLANE_Y` sits below `ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_BOTTOM_Y` so a ball that drops through falls a visible distance into the drop before `RemoveFallenBalls` culls it. Steep walls (~55°) keep balls running down to the hole rather than resting (a ball that did come to rest would be sleep-culled, i.e. vanish where it sits). Dumping a very large cluster all at once (the `End` debug release of a full map) briefly floods the funnel and spikes the physics until it drains; ordinary matched clusters are small and drain smoothly.
- **Two edges the owner saw as "not smooth" (#423), and neither was the one first suspected.**
  - **The glass drew a fan of light and dark wedges from the hole to the rim.** The vertex normals were exact, but every facet ran the whole wall in one quad: 1.37 units wide at the rim and 0.18 at the hole, a 7.8 : 1 trapezoid split into two triangles, and barycentric interpolation across a trapezoid does not follow the azimuth, so each triangle shaded as if its normal swung at a different rate. `FunnelMesh` now cuts the wall into `ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_GLASS_BANDS` (16) concentric bands spaced geometrically, 1.14 : 1 each, and the wedges are gone from every view.
  - **The gold rim's inner edge was ragged and dark-toothed in every solid-terrain scene, and cleaner in the city.** The gold band's collar turns the lip and runs `COLLAR_DROP` down the glass with its end sunk `EDGE_SINK` (0.04) outside it, and the dark pit sheath hugs the glass there, 0 to 0.01 out: the collar crossed the pit at a slope of about 0.15, and depth precision draws a grazing crossing of two opaque surfaces as teeth. The collar's end sinks `FunnelRimsMesh.COLLAR_SINK` (0.12) now, a slope of 0.38, and the line holds. **Moving the pit out of the collar's way was tried first and rejected in the Game:** with nothing hiding it, the whole collar showed through the glass as a broad pale band along the far rim from the play camera, where there had always been a thin gold line. The deeper sink crosses the pit at the same place, so the rim keeps its width.
  - **The drawn drain is `FUNNEL_DRAWN_SEGMENTS` (256), apart from `FUNNEL_SEGMENTS` (64)**, which the physics builds the collision cone from and which did not change, so nothing a ball does moved. At 64 the gold band's outer edge read as a polygon against the stone. The drawn cone stands at most 0.017 off the collision cone's flats, under a tenth of a ball radius.
  - **Cost:** +0.05 ms looking straight down into the funnel (2.02 against 1.96, `campos=0,2,4 camtarget=0,-8.3,0`) and +0.01 ms from the play level (2.04 against 2.03), meadow, dome 13, three alternating pairs against `main` at 3200×1800.
- **The terrain hole and the dark pit shaft (solid-ground scenes).** In the city there is nothing built below the arena to drain into any more (#275 — see "The city and the glass arena"), and in the sea the funnel drains into the water; but the **solid-terrain** scenes (mountains, meadow, savanna, desert, forest) are a flat clearing at the island's foot, and the flat ground plane sliced across the glass funnel just below its rim — the drain read as a shallow bowl and balls falling through vanished into the ground. The fix is in two halves. First, the terrain shaders `clip()` the island's footprint out of the ground (`IslandHoleRadius`, set from `SceneRenderer.TerrainHoleRadius` = `ArenaIsland.TERRAIN_HOLE_RADIUS`, a couple of units inside the disc's rim so the terrain tucks under the stone edge with no gap; **0 in the map editor**, which draws no island, so its terrain stays whole under the field AABB — and making an island available to a host must not hand the editor one as a side effect). But a hole alone made it worse — the ~55 %-opaque glass then showed the bright sky-haze straight through it, reading as a glass ring on the ground. So second, a **dark pit shaft** (`ArenaIsland`'s own mesh and renderer, drawn by `DrawPit` in the five `SceneRenderer.IsSolidTerrainScene`s only, opaque and `CullNone` before the glass, which composites over it) backs the glass: a near-black `FunnelMesh` that **hugs** the glass funnel — since #291 *literally*: a two-band polyline sharing the funnel's mouth (`ArenaIsland.FUNNEL_TOP_RADIUS`), running `PIT_SHEATH_CLEARANCE` (0.35) outside the glass down to just past its hole, then a narrow throat on down past the kill plane — so a ball runs down the glass into darkness and is culled inside the throat rather than against the bright sky. A wide cone would hide behind the disc's stone ring and leave the bright hole showing through the narrow (radius 14) aperture — the pit **must** stay just outside the funnel at every depth, and the sheath's gap grows linearly 0 → 0.35 from mouth to hole, outside by construction. **⚠ It was one straight cone from the mouth to `PIT_BOTTOM_Y` (−46) until #291**, which — being 18.5 units deeper than the glass on the same mouth — sat *wider* than the glass at every depth (at 18 down the glass has closed to ~1.6 and that cone was still ~7.7) and read, from any vantage under a solid scene's terrain (the free camera, the editor, a terrain dip), as a long dark spike nothing inside the drain accounts for: the owner's "outer shell doesn't match the inside", reproduced and photographed in every solid scene before the fix. Sheathed, the outside reads as the bowl's own underside with a tidy drain pipe below it — the same object as the view down into it, in every map. It is near-matte (`SpecularAmbientStrength` 0.03) so no dome bleaches the well, and the gold beads hide the mouth ring and the knee it shares with the funnel. Purely visual — no physics; the funnel mesh is still the only floor, and `FunnelPhysics.Build` takes the glass's own figures, which did not move.

Windows are not lit once and for all: each keeps its own rhythm off `CityWindowTime` (wall clock, like the balls' pulse — a city's lamps do not stop when the simulation is paused), holding a state for an interval of its own length and then deciding again, with a short fade rather than a cut. `CITY_WINDOW_BRIGHTNESS` stays **under** `GLARE_THRESHOLD`: a window that glares veils the tower behind it, and a thousand of them turn the skyline into one white haze.

Everything about the buildings is procedural and there are no assets. One 1×1×1 `BoxMesh` serves every building — each is that cube under a different instance matrix, so the whole city is a single instanced draw call (the arena floor is no longer part of it; that is the separate `IslandMesh` platform, above) — one call, but fed a frustum-culled array ordered near to far rather than the generator's own, for the reasons in "Drawing the city near to far" in `docs/rendering.md`. Non-uniform scale is safe here because the boxes are axis-aligned in object space: a diagonal scale maps each face normal onto itself, so `normalize` in the pixel shader recovers it without an inverse transpose. The `InstancedCity` technique lays the window grid out **relative to each building**, not on a raw world grid. A raw world grid (what it used to do) put the pattern at a random phase against every tower's edges, so windows landed jammed into corners or clipped — which reads as unreal. Now a dedicated `CityVS` hands the pixel shader the pixel's offset from the building's centre and the building's world size (the box is the 1×1×1 unit cube, so a transformed unit axis is the world size and the transform of the object origin is the centre; all Bone-agnostic), and `CityPS` holds a fixed wall margin (`WindowMargin`, world units) clear of glass at **every** edge and spreads the windows evenly across the interior — so no window touches a corner and every tower carries the same border. The floor/column **count** is still solved from the building's world size against the target pitch (`WindowPitchX/Y`), so a hundred-storey tower still gets more floors than a low one and a wide one more columns; only the phase is now the building's rather than the world's. The per-building cell pitch is the target pitch nudged so a whole number of windows fills the interior exactly (kept near the target, so it still reads natural), and the `buildingId` — for the neon per-tower hue and the lit-window pattern — is taken from the tower's own centre so it is one value across the whole facade. Windows band-limit to their own average coverage against that per-building pitch, so a distant tower is a dim glowing block rather than a moire of floors.

Two things learned tuning it, both worth keeping in mind elsewhere. The **specular ambient is not multiplied by albedo** (a reflection does not care how dark the surface under it is), which is physically right but washes a whole skyline in sky color at grazing angles. Almost every facade of a city seen from inside it, and almost every square metre of a floor beyond your own feet, is at a grazing angle where Fresnel is near 1 — so the city runs the term at 0.07 and the marble floor at 0.4, and both were bleached white before that. And a translucent sheet over a lit city reads as *no floor at all*; the mullions dividing the glass into panels are what make the eye accept there is glass there.

## The shared scene backdrops

### The wind, in one copy (#276)

Three ground scenes show the wind — the meadow, the savanna and the forest — and until #276 each carried its own copy of the same two lines and the same two faults, character for character. The owner reported it from the Testbed as ground that "visibly crawls and does not read as anything in particular".

- **The shading was an infinite plane wave.** `sin(dot(xz, WindDirection) * 0.15 + t * 1.4)`: dead straight, perfectly regular, and at frequency 0.15 its wavelength is 2π/0.15 ≈ **42 world units — wider than the visible field**, so the whole ground brightened and darkened under one straight edge sweeping across it. Nothing outdoors is that regular, which is why it read as an artefact rather than as weather. It is `Noise.fxh`'s **`WindGust`** now: one octave of combed noise, advected downwind, giving irregular patches drawn out along the wind.
- **And the ground's own fine texture translated.** All three sampled their relief at `xz + WindDirection * time * 0.7` — a flat 0.7 world units a second for ever, which at `GrassReliefFrequency` 2 slides the blades' texture across the ground they are rooted in at 1.4 features a second. Measured on two frames 0.6 s apart from a fixed camera, the near field's **mean absolute change was 10.3 of 255 on the meadow and 16.0 on the desert** — essentially every pixel. Grass **leans** by the gust field now (bounded to about an eighth of a feature, so it rocks and springs back); the forest floor, which is litter rather than blades, simply does not move at all. After: meadow **2.7** — the gust and the clouds, which is motion that belongs — and desert **0.26**.
- **⚠ One octave, and that is a measured ceiling.** At two the gust fell off an occupancy cliff in the two dearest shaders: on the reference desktop (6900XT, Testbed, fixed camera, 1600×900 at ssaa 4, `fpscap=400`, median of 17 readings) the savanna went **8.496 → 27.933 ms** and the forest **12.788 → 28.011**, while the meadow — the same addition in a cheaper shader — went 7.746 → 8.097 and merely began dipping to 28 now and then. Back at one octave all three come home: meadow **7.949**, savanna **8.547**, forest **12.953**, i.e. **0.05–0.20 ms** for the whole effect. It is the wall `Forest.fx` documents for its own two extras ("cutting either one ALONE saves NOTHING") read from the other side — these passes are occupancy-bound, so cost steps when the shader crosses a register threshold rather than scaling with work. **Anything added to `WindGust` has to be re-measured on the savanna and the forest, never on the meadow.**

The settings are **shared between the game and the map editor** so a map looks in the editor the way it will play. Twenty `SceneKind`s exist and **every one of them is on the cycle the Testbed's NumPad2 and the editor's V walk** (`SceneRenderer.NextScene`, off the enum itself, so a twenty-first is reachable the moment it is declared — the polar icesheet of #222 was, the aurora of #205 was too, the Grid of #393 too, and none of them needed a key touched). Until #380 the cycle was a seven-long prefix — `CycleLength`, deliberately **not** `SceneCount`, on the argument that it stayed on "the seven scenes a map is authored against" — and the other ten were reachable only by `scene=` on a command line, from the Game's own menu, or in the editor by loading a level that named one. That argument died as the campaign was built: fifty of the hundred and ten shipped levels are authored past the prefix, and the editor could not return such a level to its own backdrop without reloading the file. The split:

- The self-lit backdrops — sea, savanna (with its acacias and birds), desert and the outback (with the same flock of birds), mountains (with its snow), meadow, the forest's floor and hills (its scatter of trees, rocks and stumps is `ForestScatterRenderer`'s beside them since #75, so all three draw the wood too), the tropical beach (with its palms, its waterline's mossy rocks and the same flock again), the volcano (with its lava fountains, its smoke plume and its ash), the polar icesheet (with its sastrugi, its pressure front and its scoured blue ice), **space** and the **aurora** (a second forest floor and hills under its own night sky, planted from its own `ForestScatterRenderer` rather than sharing the daytime forest's — see "The aurora") — live in `Prazsky.Core.Render.SceneRenderer`, which owns their effects, meshes, particle buffers and every tuning constant. Each is a dedicated shader that computes its own lighting from a `SceneFrame` handed over per frame (camera, sun direction, the sky palette in linear radiance, the sun's tinted radiance, the wall-clock time, and an optional cloud-apply hook). `DrawEnvironment` draws the terrain/water (plus the savanna's acacias, the savanna's, desert's, outback's and tropical's birds); `DrawOverlays` draws the foreground weather last — the mountain's snow, the sea's spray, the savanna's campfire flame, the volcano's ash. The Testbed passes `_clouds.ApplyTo` as the hook; the editor passes null, so its scenes get full sun with no cloud shadow (the cloud uniforms stay zero and `CloudSunlight` returns 1). Both build the scene shaders out of the one Testbed content directory. **Space is the odd one in this list, and since #205 it has company** — it replaces the sky rather than the ground (`SceneRenderer.ReplacesSky`, which the dream, the cavern, the Moon, the aurora and the Grid share), so it is one full-screen pass with no grid, no cloud hook and no dome behind it; the six sky-replacing draws are the ones here that touch the depth state (the Moon, the aurora and the Grid each their own way — real ground written first and the sky read depth against it, where the other three draw depth-off over nothing), and each states its own light rig (`TryGetLightRig`) instead of taking the dome's. See "Space (Game)", "The Moon (Game)", "The aurora" and "The Grid".
- The **city** (and its neon relight) is `Prazsky.Core.Render.City` — a seeded generator of one instanced box per building — drawn through the shared `InstancedModel` city technique by an `InstancedModelRenderer` each executable owns and sky-lights like the balls (`CityWindowBrightness > 0` selects the technique; `CityNeon` flips it to neon). The arena floor (marble/glass panels) is **not** shared: it is tied to the physics ground blocks and stays in `Testbed.cs`, which is why the editor draws no platform and marks the field with the AABB instead.

### Every launch is a different city, wood and plain

**Nothing procedural in this project used to be procedural at runtime.** Every generator had a seed written into it as a constant — the city's 20260720 restated in each of the three executables, the wood's 77125, the savanna's 90125, the roofs' 43621, the palms' and the Grid's inside `SceneRenderer` — so the arrangement was fixed at authoring time and a player saw the same city every session. The owner's ruling: **lean into the procedural generation, so it looks different each time.** It was tried with the procedural music and did not survive the ear; a scene is not a tune — there is no "that is out of key" for a skyline, and these are static arrangements rather than something unfolding in time.

**One offset, rolled once per launch, added to every seeded arrangement in the program**: both cities, their rooftop equipment, the forest's wood, the aurora's, the savanna's planting, the beach's palms and the Grid's Life boards.

- **An offset and not a seed.** Each generator keeps its own constant and adds this one, so the savanna's planting and the palms' clumping stay as unlike each other as they were authored to be. One shared seed would have made every scene re-roll from the same number and quietly correlate arrangements that have nothing to do with each other.
- **Rolled once, not per build.** A quality step re-runs the city generator (`ApplyQuality` rebuilds it when the tier changes its radius) and a scene change re-runs it too — both read the offset back and get the *same* city. A skyline that rearranged itself because the player opened Settings would read as a fault rather than as variety.
- ⚠ **`sceneseed=` is not a nicety, it is what keeps the instruments working.** A capture pair or a measured A/B has to be looking at the same city, the same wood and the same planting on both halves, and after the roll it would not be. Both executables take it and both print `[sceneseed] <n>` either way, so a frame filed with its log can be generated again. **`sceneseed=0` is the arrangement everything before this was photographed against**, and that is exact rather than approximate: measured, a pinned run builds **1777 buildings and 7075 pieces of rooftop equipment**, the shipped figures, twice; three rolled runs built 1812/1803/1860 and 7096/7084/7416.
- The **map editor** rolls and has no pin, deliberately: an author is judging whether a *map* reads against a backdrop, and a backdrop that is a different city each session is a better test of that than one that is not. ⚠ **Its wood did not roll until #400's third pass**: the offset reached the editor's cities and roofs, but its forest, aurora wood and fireflies were still built on `ForestScatterRenderer.DEFAULT_SEED` alone — the same planting every session, under a field comment that said the wood rolled too.

### A campfire burns like a fire (#468)

**The savanna's fires were one tapering tongue each — a candle, or a lighter — and the rewrite that gave them three tongues, a turbulent body and rising sparks still left them reading yellow-white.** The owner's brief was *more tongues, more plasma, orange to red tints*, and the last third of it would not come however the colour ramp was tuned. Twice.

- ⚠ **The obstacle was the BLEND, not the ramp.** The flame was drawn **additive**, and additive cannot make a red flame over a bright sky: whatever red is added, the background's own green and blue stay underneath it, so a daylit savanna's fires wash to white by arithmetic. The shader already returned its colour **premultiplied** by the coverage, which is exactly what `BlendState.AlphaBlend` takes (`One` / `InverseSourceAlpha`) — so the change is one word, and the dense body now *replaces* what is behind it while the thin edges still add. It goes on blooming: the colours are linear radiance over 1 and the glare pass reads the scene target, not the blend.
- **And the white core is gated on HEIGHT as well as on heat.** The first rewrite's own comment said the core was "confined to the base" and it was not — `h` alone decided the colour and `h` carries a `(1 - v)` boost, so any heat over about 0.6 reached full core, which is most of the body all the way up the tongues. The gate cuts it off above the bottom third, so the yellow-white is the fire's **seat** and the tongues leaving it cool through orange into red.
- **Checked against a bright dome and a dusk one** (14 and 5), because a blend change is exactly the kind of thing that fixes one sky and breaks the other.

### A path goes round a tree (#476)

**The savanna's trails ran straight through the trees, and structurally had to.** The trails are painted by `Savanna.fx` as the contour lines of one low-frequency noise; the plants are placed on the CPU by `SavannaScatter`. Neither knew about the other, so where the noise and the planting coincided a worn path crossed a trunk — the owner's report, and nonsense on its face: nobody wears a track through a tree.

**`TrailWarpField` is a small CPU-built field of which way to step aside**, handed to the shader as an RG texture and used to **domain-warp the trail's noise sample**. A point near a plant samples the noise as if it stood further out, so the contour that would have crossed the trunk is pushed aside and arrives as a **bend** — which is what a path round an obstacle actually is. Cutting the trail where a tree stands was the other option and was not taken: a path that stops and starts is a different wrong picture.

- ⚠ **The bend is wide and early, and that is the owner's second note rather than a taste**: *people have eyes and see ahead, so they are already going round from a distance.* `TrailAvoidReach` is 34 world units **past the plant's own edge** — many times a trunk's width — and `TrailAvoidOffset` steps the path 16 units aside. A repulsion that began at the bark would read as a kink at the last moment, which is how an ant goes round a tree and not how a person does.
- **Summed, not nearest-only.** Two trees close together push the path round **both**; a nearest-only field would send it into the gap between their trunks, which is the one line a person would not take.
- **Built from what is worth going round**, by footprint radius (`TrailAvoidMinRadius`): grass is walked *through*, a tree, a mound, a kopje and a fallen trunk are not. It is also what keeps the build cheap — the ground cover is most of what is planted. The hearths are in the list too, which is right: nobody walks through a campfire.
- ⚠ **The uniforms are pushed where the field is built, not with the rest of the savanna's dials.** `ApplySavannaParameters` runs *before* the planting does, so a texture pushed there is always null — which cost one capture pair that looked exactly like the feature not working, the paths identical with it on and off because it was off both times.
**And the planting knows about the paths too — the belt and braces behind the bend.** A bend has a limit: in a dense clump, or where a track threads between two trunks, the warp cannot carry it clear, and a plant still stood on a path. So a site is now refused if the ground under it is trodden. `SavannaTrails` is the CPU mirror of the term `Savanna.fx` paints — the same noise, the same zero band, the same warp — less the shader's own two factors, `TrailStrength` (how bare a path is *drawn*; a paler track is in the same place) and the band-limiting fade (a function of pixel size, meaningless away from a camera). `ShaderMath` now holds the one C# copy of `Clouds.fxh`'s `CloudNoise` that both this mirror and `CloudField`'s weather read, rather than a second transcription of the hash.

- **The test is the STEM, not the crown**, at the single point the thing stands on and deliberately not over the spacing footprint, which is the crown's reach. A path running *under* a canopy is what a path does — people walk under trees — and refusing every site whose crown oversails a track would empty the groves along every path on the plain.
- **What a path bends round and what may stand on a path are two questions.** The warp is built from what is big enough to walk round (`TrailAvoidMinRadius`); the refusal applies to **everything planted, grass tufts included** — a tuft is too small for a track to bend round, and is also the very thing a worn path is worn out *of*, so a tuft in the bare middle of one is exactly the artefact.
- ⚠ **The two halves depend on each other, and that decides the shape: the plain is planted three times over** (`SavannaScatter.PLANTING_SWEEPS`). A path bends round what stands on the plain, so *is this site on a path* cannot be answered until the plain is planted — and the answer moves the plants. Bending the paths by whatever happened to be standing already (the obvious way: one sweep, the list growing as it goes) fixed the **ground cover**, which goes in last when nearly everything is already in, and did next to nothing for the **trees**, which go in first when there is nothing for a path to bend round yet: 23 → 12, 22 → 20, 14 → 15 trees on a path over three seeds. The trees are what the report is about. So the first sweep finds out where the tracks will run and the later ones plant clear of them; only the last sweep's instances are kept.
- ⚠ **Each site rolls from its own stream of dice**, not from the planting's. A site that needs a second proposal must not shift the dice for everything planted after it, or each sweep is a *fresh* savanna tested against the paths of a plain that no longer exists, and nothing converges. It also makes a before/after pair of captures readable: with it, only the plants that actually moved differ between the two frames.
- ⚠ **The path is the FIRST ranking key, not a penalty added to the clearance** — this was a priced term until it was measured, and the price lost to elbow room every time. Where no proposal is both clear and off a path, a tree with twenty units of space *on* a track beat one that had to interlace a crown to stand beside it: **16 to 19 plants a seed were put back onto a path that way, against 19 to 25 still standing on one at the end** — nearly all of what was left. And in not one case were all eight proposals on a track: there was always somewhere else to stand. Ranking the path first costs the gap between the roomiest proposal and the roomiest off-path one, and only about one proposal in ten is on a path at all.
- **Measured, six scene seeds, plants left standing on a path** (trees and the like + ground cover, out of some 230 + 255): **19+14, 19+15, 19+17, 12+15, 16+13, 17+9 → 1+0, 5+1, 3+2, 1+0, 0+0, 4+0.** A fifth of the trees, and the ground cover all but gone.
- ⚠ **It does not converge to nothing, and is not meant to.** Each sweep moves a few plants, and moving a plant moves the paths near it, so a sweep both clears offenders and makes the odd new one; past three they roughly balance (4 and 5 sweeps straddle 3 rather than beating it). What is left is a handful of stems at the *fringe* of a track.
- **It did not trade #108's fault back in**, which is the check that mattered: pairs standing inside each other, counted over the finished plain, are **unchanged** at 3, 0 and 1 for the three seeds carrying any, worst margin −2.1, 0.0 and −0.8 either way. At this density a plant turned off a path lands on open ground rather than in its neighbour.
- **Cost**: the planting's arithmetic, three times over — **10 ms for one sweep, 18 for two, 24 for three** on the project's slow machine (Vega 10 APU), at scene load and in the editor's re-plant, which rebuilds twenty-five meshes in the same breath. Nothing per frame: no shader change, and the field the shader samples is the one it already had.

### The sun's shadows, in one copy (#469, #470, #471)

**Eleven of the twenty throw cast shadows**: the savanna, the meadow, the forest, the mountains, the desert, the outback, the beach, the volcano, Mars, the polar ice and the city. One orthographic map is fitted round the camera each frame, the scene's own planting and the host's island, gun and wood are drawn into it, and each terrain shader multiplies its *sun* term by what it reads back — the dome's ambient is untouched, which is what a shadow is. Ten of the eleven ask for one through `Shadows` on their config (`ShadowConfig`: strength, extent, map size); the city — the one backdrop `SceneRenderer` does not own, its config, its towers and its street shader all the host's — hands its own dials and fit over through `SceneRenderer.SetHostShadowScene` instead. A scene that asks for neither gets none.

- **Sea and Storm are out deliberately.** The storm draws no ground at all — `StormClouds.fx` *is* the cloud, and the island in it stands on nothing a shadow could land on — and the sea is water, where a shadow inside the Fresnel, the foam and the subsurface terms is a look decision of its own rather than a line. The six sky-replacing scenes have no sun above the horizon and the gate already skipped them.
- **The map's own box is computed from each scene's terrain figures** rather than authored beside them, so a re-tuned hill height cannot leave a map quietly covering the wrong slab of air. The mountain and the volcano take half their relief on purpose: their peaks are terrain that stands outside the map's 260-unit extent and shades nothing but itself, and a range stretched to reach them would coarsen the bias on the ground at the gun's feet.
- **What it costs is the receiving, not the casting** — the meadow, whose only caster is the island, measured the same +0.19 ms as the forest with 380 of them. The city measured +0.22–0.23 ms with some 1800 towers casting, the same order rather than the outlier the issue worried it might be, because the map's price is set by who reads it back, not by who is drawn into it. The figures, the conditions and the argument are in "Sun shadows" in `docs/rendering.md`.
- **The city's own towers cast and receive; its street does not** — the street shader already darkens a canyon by an occupancy term tuned to read one as shadowed at every sun height (#399), so a shadow-map receiver there changed a paired capture's facades dramatically and its pavement almost not at all. See "The city's shadows" in `docs/rendering.md`.

So a change to any scene reaches both executables at once, exactly as the shared shaders do. The per-scene sections below describe what each one is doing; the tuning dials named in them now live on `SceneRenderer` (or `City`) rather than at the top of `Testbed.cs`.

### The far field (#551)

**Until #551 every camera clipped at 500 units, and the open-ground backdrops were cut off in front of the player twice over.** The owner's words: *"Dohlednost – měla by být větší – např. na Marsu je vidět ořezávání hor na pozadí."* Surveyed from the Game's front end and play pose and from a Testbed sweep of four azimuths per scene, before against a build with the far plane at 4000 and nothing else changed, there were two different faults, and only one of them was the far plane:

- **Where a scene's haze had not finished by 500** — the volcano's plain and the icesheet (haze 900), the sea (700), the storm's cloud band — the far plane sliced the ground or the cloud off with a line. Raising the far plane alone moved those.
- **Where the haze had finished**, the far plane changed nothing at all (Mars: 231 pixels of a 1920×1080 frame, all in the glass in the foreground). There the cut was **the camera grid's own edge**, ±500 round the lens, and it showed because a fully hazed landform is not the sky: the haze ends in the light rig's `HorizonColor`, an average over the dome capture's bottom fifth, which on Mars is **yellow under a pink sky**. The mesas past 460 stood as flat yellow cards with the grid's edge for a back — the "mountains being cut".

So the fix has three parts, and a scene takes all three:

- **The far plane is 2000** (`BasicCamera3D.DEFAULT_FAR_PLANE_DISTANCE`, which `RecoilCamera` takes too). The near plane sets the depth precision near the camera — the 24-bit step at distance *z* is about *z²/(near·2²⁴)* — so the cluster, the island and the cannon lose a few parts in a hundred thousand.
- **The far ring.** After its camera grid, each of the eleven open-ground scenes (sea, polar, desert, outback, tropical and its lagoon, savanna, mountains, meadow, forest, volcano, Mars) draws its terrain a second time over one shared mesh: a polar ring **centred on the arena**, 200 to 1500 out, 432 cells around (0.83° each from the arena) and rings spaced geometrically, each twice as far from the last as a cell is wide, so every cell subtends about the same angle — a camera grid is fine everywhere, and reaching 1500 at today's cells would have been about nine times the vertices. It is the scene's own technique and height function with `OriginXZ` set to zero, because the ring's vertices are already world positions, so the land past the grid is the same land. Fixed to the world, it cannot swim. `FarRingClip` gives every pixel inside the camera grid back to the grid (the ring takes over 6 units inside the grid's edge, so the two tessellations never open a crack), and the rows the grid covers on every side are not drawn at all: rows run outward in the index buffer, so skipping them is a start offset. The ring draws with the scene's `…Reduced` program where it has one — everything those give up is sub-pixel past the grid.
- **The fade to the drawn sky.** `FarFadeToSky` (`FarField.fxh`) carries the last stretch to the colour the dome is **drawn** in behind that pixel — `SkyDome.DrawnLinearAt`, the ramp every dome vertex takes its colour from, handed to the scenes as nine samples over direction.y (`SkyLightRig.FarSky`). It begins where the scene's own haze reaches its end (`HorizonHazeDistance`, read back from the effect) or at 600, whichever is nearer, and is complete at `SceneRenderer.FAR_FADE_END` (1300), inside the ring and the far plane, so nothing about where either ends can be seen. Begun at 900, the icesheet's last few hundred units — all squeezed into a pixel or two at the skyline — kept the haze's teal and drew a line of it under a lilac sky; hence the 600.

**The storm has no ground, and needed the opposite.** Its cloud band reaches 560 across the wind and wraps 780 along it, and the 500-unit far plane was what hid both; `StormClouds.fx` now thins each puff's opacity to nothing over 380–460 from the lens (`STORM_FAR_THIN_*`), so the storm's horizon stands where it stood and only stops being a line.

**Not in it:** the Moon (no air for a fade to go into; its highland crest and curvature hide the grid's edge, see the Moon's section), the aurora and the Grid (their skies are their own passes, not the dome, and their floors fade to their own horizon well inside the grid), space, the dream and the cavern (no far ground), and the two cities (enclosed by their own towers; the far plane going to 2000 only lets a few more towers in).

**The reduced tier draws no ring.** Measured on Mars in the Testbed from a low camera looking out over the mesas (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-5,-400`, dome 19, 1920×1080 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc nocap`, first six readings dropped, three alternating pairs on the desktop): **4.32 → 5.16 ms**, and +0.8 to +0.95 across three earlier sessions of the same pair. Almost none of it is the ring's vertices — a quarter of them (216 around) measured 5.36 against 5.45, and skipping the covered rows bought nothing measurable — and the reduced program did not help either: the cost is the **new pixels**, the band of mesa that used to be sky, shaded by a full terrain program. That is what seeing further costs, and it is the tier ladder's job: at `SceneDetail` 0 the ring is not drawn and the fade instead completes by the largest circle the camera grid covers round the lens (its half-width less the overlap), beginning at 85 % of that — no edge anywhere, a nearer horizon.

### The terrain grids and the billboards, in one copy (#589)

**Every terrain scene's camera-centred grid comes out of one reference-counted cache, `TerrainGridCache`, keyed on (vertices a side, extent)** — `AcquireGridMesh`, on `BackdropServices` since #580 (the renderer's own forwards there). A grid carries nothing scene-specific (every terrain shader moves it by its own `OriginXZ` and lifts it in the vertex shader), and three pairs were asked for three times each: 360 over 1000 (desert, tropical, Mars), 360 over 1200 (mountains, volcano, Moon) and 220 over 1200 (meadow, forest, aurora). Measured through the cache at load: **fourteen grids and 56.1 MB of buffers became eight and 34.0 MB**, and the Testbed's `SceneRenderer` constructor went from 492–559 ms to 462–493 ms over four alternating launches each (desktop, desert, a temporary stopwatch round the call). The cache owns every buffer — a scene never disposes its grid, it gives it back. That matters for the two grids that are not fixed: the volcano's and Mars's step down to 256 a side under the reduced detail program (#540), and each step releases the full grid without taking it from the four scenes still drawing it; at the reduced tier the cache holds ten grids (38.7 MB of buffers), and returning to full frees the two coarse ones again (checked by cycling `detail=` and the two scenes through `alt=`). Eager, not per scene: a scene switch builds nothing, and #589 left it so.

**Every billboard index buffer comes out of one builder, `BuildQuadIndexBuffer` (on `BackdropServices` since #580), and it refuses a count past `MAX_BILLBOARD_QUADS` (16 383)** — past 16 384 quads a 16-bit index wraps and the later quads silently draw the first ones' vertices, the terrain grids' own lesson above. There were five copies of that loop — the particle builder, the snow's and the spray's copies of it, the storm's, and the flame's inline one — and only the volcano's counts were clamped (`MAX_BILLBOARD_PARTICLES`, which stays as the volcano's dial). The snow and the spray now go through `BuildBillboardParticles` with their own seeds, so their sequences are the ones they always were; the flame keeps its own vertices and takes the builder's `mirrored` order, because its quads stand on their base.

**The tropical beach's dressing is uploaded once** since #589: each palm, rock, scrub, tuft and driftwood variant holds a static instance buffer (`StaticInstances`, the savanna's `ScatterBucket` pattern from #451) instead of copying its matrices into one shared dynamic buffer on every draw — some 1,070 instances over about twenty discards a frame, and the palms and rocks again in the shadow pass. The gain measures at nothing — 2.96 and 2.95 ms a frame before, 2.95 and 2.95 after, in two alternating pairs (desktop, `nopost nooverc nocap`, the pinned camera below, 1600×900, shadows on) — so this was never a performance change (BestPractices §9); it is one pattern instead of two. The savanna's hearth stones keep the dynamic path.

Verified against the previous build by capture in all fifteen scenes the change reaches (sea, desert, polar, outback, tropical, volcano, Mars, savanna, mountains, meadow, forest, Moon, aurora, Grid, storm; `sceneseed=3 nopost nooverc`, one pinned camera, 1600×900, a 1.5 px blur and a threshold of 24): after-against-before landed inside the before-against-before noise of the animated scenes in every one (the sea, whose spray and waves move most, 1,884 changed pixels of 1.44 M against 1,947 between its two baseline runs; every other scene at most 369, the volcano, whose baseline runs differed by 380).

### The terrain mirrors, in one copy and checked against the GPU (#590)

**Everything that stands on a terrain is placed by a C# copy of the field the terrain shader displaces its grid by** — the scatters' trees and rocks, the savanna's fires, the volcano's vents and lamps, and since #559 the chapter intro's lenses. Those copies lived in three places until #590: `TerrainMirror` (desert, mountains, outback, polar ice), static methods on `SceneRenderer` (forest, meadow, beach) and instance methods on it (savanna, volcano), with `SmoothStep` and `Frac` written twice, once there and once in `ShaderMath`. **All ten are `TerrainMirror`'s now** — `Desert`, `Mountain`, `Outback`, `Polar`, `Savanna`, `Tropical`, `Meadow`, `Forest` (which the aurora's ground is too, on `AuroraSceneConfig.Terrain`) and `Volcano`, static and config-taking — on `ShaderMath`'s one copy of the helpers; the renderer keeps only the live-config wrappers a host asks through (`SavannaGroundHeight`, `VolcanoGroundHeight`, `PolarGroundHeight`). The move was held to the bit: the bodies are the old lines with their config read through a parameter, and the savanna's and the forest's ramps keep their hand-spelled clamp-then-hermite rather than `ShaderMath.SmoothStep`, which divides by `(R + T) − R` — not `T` to the last bit. Captures before and after (Testbed, `sceneseed=0 nopost nooverc weather=clear`, `shotframe=120`, 1600×900, savanna, beach, forest and volcano each framed out over their planting) differed by no more than two launches of the *same* build differ from each other: at most 1 of 255 on the savanna and the forest, and the beach's water and the volcano's lava changing by the same amounts in the same places between repeat runs of either build.

**`mirrorcheck` is what compares them now** (the Testbed switch, `docs/testbed.md`). Each terrain shader gains a `HeightProbe` technique by defining its height and including `HeightProbe.fxh` last; the Testbed draws one quad through it into a 256×256 `Vector4` target covering ±512 units round the arena, each pixel evaluating the shader's own height function — the one its vertex shader calls — at the world XZ the quad carries, and handing that XZ back beside the height, so the mirror is evaluated at exactly the point the GPU was. No grid, no camera and no interpolation between vertices stand between the two numbers. It prints one line per scene and exits 0 (pass), 1 (fail) or 2 (no mirror); the tolerance is 0.01 world units. No frame of any executable selects the technique.

Measured on the desktop (RX 6900 XT), 65 536 samples a scene, `[build] shaders 32 set 96de29c6`:

| Scene | max \|dh\| | mean \|dh\| | over 0.01 | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| savanna | 0.000033 | 0.0000026 | 0 | pass |
| meadow | 0.000037 | 0.0000033 | 0 | pass |
| forest | 0.000069 | 0.0000058 | 0 | pass |
| aurora (the forest's field on its own terrain) | 0.000069 | 0.0000058 | 0 | pass |
| tropical beach | 0.000634 | 0.000044 | 0 | pass |
| volcano (the massing the mirror copies) | 0.001717 | 0.0000641 | 0 | pass |
| desert | 0.208 | 0.0046 | 8 203 | **fail** |
| polar ice | 3.94 | 0.0030 | 2 395 | **fail** |
| outback | 6.73 | 0.0081 | 2 926 | **fail** |
| mountains | 16.19 | 0.065 | 44 246 | **fail** |

The volcano also prints how far its mirror stands off the ground actually drawn, which adds the scoria the mirror leaves out by design: **1.35 at most, 0.24 on average** — the clearance a path over the cone has to allow, and less than the "three units" the mirror's comment had guessed.

**Seen to fail (BestPractices §10).** Before any of those passes was trusted, the forest mirror's second octave was changed from 0.26 to 0.27 — one term, 1 % of the hills — and the check was run again: forest and aurora both **fail at 0.290 max, 0.174 mean, 61 795 of 65 536 samples over tolerance**, exit 1. The change was not committed.

**⚠ The four failures are real, and they are all one cause: the noise's hash is ill-conditioned in float.** The six mirrors built of sines and hermite ramps agree with their shaders to under a thousandth, which is the GPU's `sin` against `MathF.Sin` and nothing else. The four built on `ShaderMath.Noise` — the #559 mirrors — do not, and the old comment's "may differ from the GPU's by a rounding" was the wrong estimate: a lens pulled several units off `TerrainMirror.Mountain`'s answer can be sixteen units inside the drawn range. `NoiseHash22` takes `frac((x + y) · z)` of a product near 20 000, where a float carries the fraction to about 1/500, so any difference in how the steps are rounded moves the hash by whole quanta, and a ridged field raised to the third power magnifies that into units. The shader compiler rounds differently from the C# in two places, and both were **measured to be the cause, not guessed**:
- **It fuses the hash's `dot` into multiply-adds.** A standalone copy of the mountain mirror with the dot written as `FusedMultiplyAdd(z, x + 33.33, FusedMultiplyAdd(y, z + 33.33, x · (y + 33.33)))` differs from the unfused one by **16.19416 max, 0.0647203 mean, 44 246 samples over 0.01** on the same grid — the GPU's figures to the fifth digit. With that one line changed in `ShaderMath.Hash22`, `mirrorcheck` passes the desert (0.000232), the mountains (0.000748) and the ice (0.000992).
- **It folds the outback's constant offsets** — `cellId + seed + 23.7` with `seed` a literal becomes `cellId + 37.4` — where the C# adds left to right. With the fused hash alone the outback still failed (6.73 max, 562 samples, every one at a boulder's or a monolith's wall, the formation's centre rolled a few units elsewhere); writing the mirror's rolls as `cellX + (seed + 23.7f)` as well brought it to **0.000293 max**.

Neither fix is in #590, deliberately: the first changes `ShaderMath.Hash22` under every CPU user of the noise — `CloudField`'s weather mirror and `SavannaTrails` as well as the terrain — and so is not the bit-for-bit move this change is, and both assume a GPU that fuses and folds the way this one's driver and `fxc` do; whether the laptop's integrated Radeon agrees is unmeasured. A durable fix is more likely a hash that is well-conditioned in float on both sides (smaller products before the `frac`), which changes what the four scenes, the clouds and the trails look like and wants its own issue and its own captures.

### The backdrop classes and the scene registry (#580)

**`SceneRenderer` is being taken apart one scene at a time, and the first three steps have landed.** It was 8,157 lines organised by *layer* (all the fields, then one constructor building all twenty scenes, then every `Apply*`, then every `Draw*`), so one scene was spread over a dozen stretches of the file and every per-scene question was a switch with twenty arms. The target is one `Backdrop` class per scene in `Render/Scenes/`, with the renderer left as a dispatcher over them plus the services several scenes share. Step 1 moved three scenes and the registry; the renderer was **7,578 lines** after it. Step 2 moved the storm, the Grid and the Moon, one commit each (6,985, 6,295 and **6,066 lines** after them). Step 3 gave the scenes that shared an effect a clone each (6,074), moved the aurora (5,725), made the far field a service and added `TerrainPass` (5,425), moved the icesheet (5,349), made the flock a service (5,122) and moved the desert and the outback (5,053 and **4,952 lines**), again one commit each.

- **`SceneCatalog` is the one table of facts about each `SceneKind`** (`Render/SceneCatalog.cs`, with the enum itself): the display name, the parse key a level file writes and `scene=` takes, its aliases (`ice`, `tron`), and the three classifications — `ReplacesSky`, `IsSolidTerrainScene` (and `OpenBelow`, its negation) and `AnimatesLightRig`. It replaced five parallel tables on the renderer: the positional `SCENE_NAMES` (which mislabelled every scene after a reordered member without a word), the twenty-arm `TryParseScene` switch, the level converter's own copy of the one irregular spelling (`"neon"`), and three hand-kept membership lists. **The type refuses to initialise unless every enum member has exactly one row at its own index and no two spellings collide**, so an executable with a missing row does not start; `Tests/BS3D.Tests/SceneCatalogTests.cs` feeds the check a table with a row missing, two rows swapped, one spelling naming two kinds and an uppercase key and sees each refused, and pins every name, key and flag to what the old tables answered. It has no GPU type in it, so `SceneNameJsonConverter` reads and writes scene names off it alone: **the level format no longer depends on the renderer.** `SceneRenderer`'s old static members forward to it, so no caller changed.
- **`Backdrop`** (`Render/Scenes/Backdrop.cs`, internal) is one scene's share: its config, effects and buffers, its push, its `Draw`, and the questions only it can answer (`DrawOverlays`, `DrawsAtDisplayResolution`, `TryGetLightRig`, `TryGetViewpoint`, `TryGetSceneEvent` since the storm, `TryGetSunDirection` since the Moon, `TryGetTerrainProbe` since the aurora, `ShadowReceivers` and `TryShadowFit` since the icesheet, `OnDetailChanged`, `Dispose`). **It has only the hooks the migrated scenes need**, and every default is the answer the renderer's old `default:` arm gave. `BackdropServices` carries what several scenes share: the device, the full-screen quad and the supersampling factor; since step 2 also the seed offset (the constructor's `seedOffset`, which every seeded arrangement adds to its own constant), the billboard index builder (`BuildQuadIndexBuffer`, `CheckBillboardQuads`, `MAX_BILLBOARD_QUADS` — #589's one copy; the flame and every particle buffer still on the renderer build through it too), the terrain grids (`AcquireGridMesh` over the renderer's `TerrainGridCache`, which the renderer still owns and disposes; its own `AcquireGridMesh` forwards) and `TerrainHoleRadius`; since step 3 the `Snowfall` (the flake buffer the mountain and the aurora share, and its draw), the `FarField` and the `Birds`.
- **The renderer dispatches first and falls back second.** `_backdrops` is indexed by `SceneKind` and null for a scene not yet migrated; `DrawEnvironment`, `DrawOverlays`, `TryGetLightRig`, `TryGetViewpoint`, `TryGetSceneEvent`, `TryGetSunDirection`, `TryGetTerrainProbe`, `TryShadowFit`, `GetSceneConfig` and `SceneDetail` ask the backdrop when there is one and run their old switch arms otherwise. The scene-specific public queries (`DreamSolidCenter`, `CavernCrystalCenter`, `TryGetSpacePlanetshine`, `StormFlash`, `TryGetStormFlash`, `StormCellCount`, `StormCell`, `GridSolids`, `TryGetGridRing`, `TryGetMoonEarthshine`, `AuroraGlowColor`, `PolarGroundHeight`, `PolarCrevasse`, `PolarRidgeAt`) stay on the renderer as one-line forwarders with their public docs, because the backdrop classes are internal; the backdrop's own method takes the short name (`StormBackdrop.Flash`, `GridBackdrop.Solids`, `MoonBackdrop.TryGetEarthshine`, `AuroraBackdrop.GlowColor`).
- **Space, the dream and the cavern went first, and they were picked because they touch nothing else.** They are the three pure sky-replacing passes: no terrain grid, no far ring, no shadow map, no scatter, no birds, no shared mutable effect, no scene event or ground glow. What they did share was exactly two things, and both became services rather than moving: the full-screen quad (Space built it, and the Moon's sky, the aurora's sky, Mars's moons and the Grid's void all draw over it — `_fullScreenQuad` now, built at the top of the constructor) and the dream's and cavern's display-resolution target, which stays with the renderer because it is drawn *around* a backdrop (the backdrop only says `DrawsAtDisplayResolution`). Their code moved verbatim, comments with it; each backdrop is still built at the point in the constructor where its code stood.

- **Step 2 moved the storm, the Grid and the Moon**, each line for line with its comments by a script that checked an anchor on every line it cut, and each checked afterwards by a line-multiset comparison (every removed renderer line reappears verbatim in the new files, apart from the listed renames and forwarders). The storm brought the first scene event — its strike schedule, flash, lamp and cells — and needed the billboard builder and `Hash01` (now `internal static`, shared with the volcano's eruption). The Grid is the first backdrop on a terrain grid, so the grid cache and the hole radius became services; its solids, Life boards, landmark ring and the shapes the chapter intro frames moved whole. The Moon brought `TryGetSunDirection` and the earthshine, and reads `SupersampleFactor` through `Services` at draw time, as space does. The dream's and the cavern's display-resolution target (`_backdropTarget`, `_backdropBatch`) had been declared inside the Moon's field region by accident of history; it stayed with the renderer, in the backdrops' region.
- **Step 3 began with the two shared effects** (the issue's own step 2). The mountain and the aurora each draw the snow through their own `Effect.Clone()` of `Snow.fx`, and the sea and the tropical lagoon each through their own of `Sea.fx`; each clone's config is pushed once at load and its owner disposes it. `DrawSea` and `DrawTropicalWater` had re-pushed 23 water values a frame, and `DrawSnow` eleven, only so that a shared effect's slots held the scene being drawn. A clone copies the parameters and the techniques and shares the compiled shaders (a clone's `Dispose` leaves them to the original), so nothing is compiled twice. The captures did not move.
- **Then the aurora.** It is the Moon's shape (a terrain grid under a sky pass on the shared quad, the terrain first), and it brought three things with it. The animated light rig (`TryGetLightRig` is the one that reads the wall clock) and its helpers `AuroraLightMix`, `AuroraHueShift` and `TowardsHue` moved whole, and the public `AuroraGlowColor` the three hosts call every frame forwards to `AuroraBackdrop.GlowColor`. `mirrorcheck` reads the aurora's ground through the new `TryGetTerrainProbe` hook, and gives the same line as before. And the snow: the flake buffer the mountain and the aurora share became the **`Snowfall` service** (`Render/Scenes/Snowfall.cs`: the buffer, its capacity, `ApplyParameters` and `Draw`), built by the renderer where the snow stood in the constructor, still sized off the mountain's `FlakeCount`, and handed to the backdrops through `BackdropServices.Snowfall` before the aurora is built. The aurora's own clone of `Snow.fx` moved into its backdrop, which loads, pushes and disposes it; the mountain's stays on the renderer with the mountain. `BuildSnowBuffers` went: it was a "(re)build" nothing called twice.
- **Then the far field became a service and the terrain skeleton a helper**, before any terrain scene moved. `FarField` (`Render/Scenes/FarField.cs`) is #551's region of the renderer whole — the ring mesh, the fade's constants, the per-effect slot and technique caches, `Begin` (was `BeginFarField`) and `DrawRing` (was `DrawFarRing`) — built before `BackdropServices` and handed to it (`Services.FarField`), since the backdrops that draw a terrain need it as much as the scenes still on the renderer do; the renderer's twelve terrain draws call it the same way. It reads the tier through its own `SceneDetail`, which the renderer's setter forwards on every change (the ring's `FarRingDrawn` read `_sceneDetail` directly before); `SceneRenderer.FAR_FADE_END`, which `BasicCamera3D`'s doc names, stays public as a forwarding constant. **`TerrainPass`** (`Render/Scenes/TerrainPass.cs`) is the issue's own helper: one effect, its grid from the cache, its extent, the name of its time uniform and ten per-frame parameters resolved once, and one `Draw(frame, holeRadius)` that snaps, pushes, lets the clouds at the effect, states the far fade, draws, draws the ring and restores. The polar icesheet, the desert and the outback — whose draws were the same thirty lines with three names changed — each drew through one from that commit on, still on the renderer. The other eight terrain scenes run the same skeleton with more in it (a depth state, point lights, a second pass) and join it as they move, when it can be seen what each one needs.
- **Then the polar icesheet**, the first backdrop on a `TerrainPass` and the one that brought the shadow hooks. `ShadowReceivers` names the effects a scene reads the sun's map through, gathered once at load: `RegisterShadowReceivers`' list is now the renderer's remaining scenes plus the union of the backdrops', so a migrated scene states its receivers beside its fit rather than in a list two screens away. `TryShadowFit` states the ground's height and how far the box has to reach below and above it; the renderer still centres the box on the camera and adds the island's headroom and the margin, and asks the backdrop before its own switch. `DrawShadowCasters` did not come: the icesheet, the desert and the outback cast nothing of their own (the island and the gun are the host's casters), so that hook waits for the savanna or the beach, which have casters to state. The polar queries the chapter intro flies over the ice with (`PolarGroundHeight`, `PolarCrevasse`, `PolarRidgeAt`) forward to `PolarBackdrop.GroundHeight`, `Crevasse` and `RidgeAt`.
- **The flock became a service before the desert and the outback**, which draw it (`BirdFlock`, `Render/Scenes/BirdFlock.cs`, handed to the backdrops as `Services.Birds`): the effect and its cached parameters, the rest-pose mesh, the per-bird state, `SeedBirdFlock`, `Draw` (was `DrawBirds`) and `ResolveFlap` moved whole. It is still seeded once and sized to the largest count of the four scenes that draw it — the renderer works that number out and hands it to the constructor, so the trap its doc records (a desert flock silently capped to the savanna's count) cannot come back by a scene moving.
- **Then the desert and the outback**, one commit each, by the same script as the icesheet: each is its config, its effect and `TerrainPass`, its push, a `Draw` that is the pass and then the flock (`Services.Birds.Draw` with its own `BirdsConfig`), its viewpoint, its shadow receiver and fit, and its probe. Each exposes its `Birds` config so the renderer can still size the shared flock off the largest of the four counts. That leaves `RegisterShadowReceivers`' hand-kept list with the renderer's seven remaining terrain scenes and the two scatters, and the three backdrops' effects joined to it at load; `TryShadowFit`'s switch has the same seven.

**Traps, for whoever takes the next scene:**

- ⚠ **A scene's reduced technique is picked only when `SceneDetail` changes, never at load.** `OnDetailChanged` is `SelectDetailTechniques`' timing, and the renderer starts at detail 1, so until a host lowers it the dream and the cavern draw with their effect's *default* technique — the first one the `.fx` declares, which is the authored one. Selecting in a backdrop's constructor would be harmless today and wrong the day a shader declares its reduced technique first.
- **`SupersampleFactor` is written after the constructor** (every host sets it in an object initializer), so it lives in `BackdropServices` and the renderer's property forwards there. A backdrop reads it at draw time; one that copied it in its constructor would draw every scene at a factor of 1.
- **Shared helpers become `internal static` on the renderer, not copies**: `SafeNormal`, `AtBearing`, `DirectionFromElevationAzimuth` and `Hash01` are used by migrated and unmigrated scenes alike.
- **`TerrainHoleRadius` is written by the host after the constructor and whenever the island changes**, so it is `SupersampleFactor`'s case again: it lives in `BackdropServices` and the renderer's public property forwards there; a backdrop reads `Services.TerrainHoleRadius` at draw time and never copies it.
- **A backdrop takes its terrain grid from the cache and never disposes it** — the cache is shared by every scene asking for the same (vertices, extent) pair (#589), and the renderer disposes the cache. A backdrop's `Dispose` frees only what it built itself (the storm's puff and bolt buffers, the Grid's tower buffers and Life textures; the Moon has none). **An effect loaded from the content manager is the manager's and is never disposed; a clone of one is its owner's and is** — the aurora's snow clone is the first a backdrop's `Dispose` frees.
- **Disposal order moved**: every backdrop is disposed first in the renderer's `Dispose` now, where the storm's and the Grid's buffers used to go last. Nothing depends on the order; it is noted because it is a visible change in the list.
- **A service the renderer builds mid-constructor has to exist before the first backdrop that uses it.** `Snowfall` and `Birds` are set on `BackdropServices` where the snow and the flock stood in the constructor (the far field is built before the services and passed in), and a backdrop reads them from `Services` — so a scene built earlier in the constructor than the snow or the flock cannot use them in its own constructor, only at draw time. The aurora and the desert/outback are fine because only their draws reach them; the flock's size is the one thing that runs the other way (the renderer reads each migrated scene's `Birds` config), which is why the desert and the outback are built before it.
- **The far ring's tier is `FarField.SceneDetail`**, which the renderer's `SceneDetail` setter forwards on every change. Nothing but that setter may write it; a backdrop with a detail-driven technique still takes `OnDetailChanged`.
- **A loaded machine widens the noise past what two old-build runs show.** During the flock's commit another session was loading the desktop, and scenes the commit could not touch (the dream at 50,000 pixels, the city, the cavern) were flagged along with the ones it could. Re-running a flag on both builds still settles it, and when a flag survives that — the beach at `detail=0` came out 40,000 apart across builds against 1,000 within each — the tightest test is the same scene on **the previous commit's build**, which cannot differ by the old build's noise: 260 pixels.
- **Pixel identity cannot be judged frame against frame, because the Testbed's clock is not the frame's.** It runs a variable time step, so frame *n* of two runs of the *same* build lands at a slightly different wall-clock second, and every animated scene differs: between two runs of the old build the sea differed in 117,532 pixels, the aurora in 9,399, the mountain's snow in 7,548 (blurred 5×5, a channel moving more than 8 of 255). The verdict is therefore **a new-build capture set against the spread of two old-build sets** — and a single outlier is a third old-looking run away from being noise: the first new-build run put the dream at `ssaa=1` 17,505 pixels off, a second new-build run 138 off, and the two new-build runs differed from each other by 15,720. The rig is `sceneseed=0 nopost nooverc weather=clear`, `campos=0,6,60 camtarget=0,-8,0`, `at=0:F5,0:F12`, `shotframe=150` at 1280×720, launched minimized without focus; all twenty scenes at the default `ssaa=2`, the six sky-replacing ones again at `ssaa=1` (the dream's and cavern's direct path) and five scenes at `detail=0` (the reduced techniques).
