It must be a bend of the *position* and never of the frequency: scaling the frequency by a field puts `coordinate × field` in the phase, and the coordinate here is a world position running to ±500, so the phase gradient goes as hundreds of cycles per unit while the band-limit is still computed from the nominal frequency and has no idea. The near sand came out as a violent rainbow moiré across the entire frame.
  - And `SandPattern` has **no early exit** once the ripples are unresolvable, tempting as one is: the value feeds `ddx`/`ddy` through `PerturbNormalFromHeight` and the resolvable factor varies per pixel, so a branch on it would diverge inside a quad along the very line where the ripples fade — and a quad whose lanes took different paths has undefined derivatives. A `lerp` does the same job for a handful of ALU. The sharpening of the line crests fades **quadratically faster** than their amplitude, because the shaping is a nonlinearity and generates harmonics well above the fundamental: fading the amplitude alone would leave the crests as the last thing to go and the first thing to crawl. Drawn opaque, first in the scene block where the city would be, `CullNone` (the winding is moot on a heightfield); the dune, ripple, dust and sand dials (`DUNE_AMPLITUDE`, `DESERT_LEVEL_Y`, `DESERT_RIPPLE_*`, `DESERT_DUST_*`, `SAND_COLOR`, `SAND_COLOR_PALE`, `SHEEN_STRENGTH`) sit on the shared `SceneRenderer`. Shader Model 5.0, drawn in both executables. It carries **no point lights** (no campfire), so unlike the savanna its draw sets `SceneLightCount` to nothing, and it forces **no default dome** — like the mountains and the meadow, it keeps whatever dome is up. It shares the savanna's [flock of birds](#the-savanna-testbed) circling overhead (drawn from the one mesh in all three scenes), the one moving creature over the empty sand.

**The dunes are cut by the wind, the sand is orange, and the haze is dust** (a pass with no issue: a survey of all twenty scenes' menu frames put the desert last). Under a noon dome the crests tonemapped to near white and the erg read as snow; under the purple and teal domes the far dunes faded cyan; and every dune was a symmetric rounded lump, repeated to the horizon as a lumpy wall. Redrawn against references rendered locally (a Sahara erg from eye height, a single crest in side light):
- **A dune is a sawtooth along the wind** (`DuneProfile`): the height climbs a long gentle windward slope over `DUNE_WINDWARD` (0.75) of each `DUNE_SPACING` (64 units) period, breaks over a corner of a crest and falls down a short steep slip face — about 35° at full height — into a flat interdune trough (`h^1.5`). From any low vantage an erg reads by exactly that: a bright face and a dark face meeting on a sharp line. The crests are made **sinuous** by warping the along-wind coordinate with slow noise across the wind; their **height wanders** along the crest; a **second set** 35° off the wind at a little under half the spacing takes over where it stands taller (a `max`, which keeps both sets' crests sharp where a sum rounds them into each other); and a long low **swell** carries the rows over rises. The wind direction is `DesertSceneConfig.Wind`, the dust veil's own.
- **Low dunes are let go, not kept small.** A dune a fraction of a unit high still has its period's full slip face, and turned away from a low sun that face is a line of shade a few pixels wide — the first build drew them across the interdune flats as thin dark cracks. The height and the second set's presence go through a smoothstep, so a dune is either substantial or absent.
- **The normal is analytic** (`GradientNoise2Grad`, the shared gradient noise with its derivative written out — the same hash and quintic fade, so its value is `GradientNoise2`'s to the bit and the vertex shader and the pixel shader describe one surface). With five noises in the field, the three finite-difference taps the normal used to take cost **+1.60 ms** looking across the dunes and +1.53 from above; one evaluation with its gradient costs +0.35 and +0.27. Checked against finite differences of a numpy copy of the field over 3 000 points: a median error of 1e-11. It also draws the crest as the corner it is rather than smearing it over the taps' 1.5 units.
- **The sand is orange-tan:** `SandColor` (0.48, 0.24, 0.085) and `SandColorPale` (0.68, 0.42, 0.2), the references' trough and crest tones linearised; they were 0.55/0.38/0.18 and 0.82/0.68/0.44. `SandBounce` (0.22) lights the faces the sun does not reach with sunlight thrown back off the lit sand, which is why a slip face reads warm brown in every reference rather than the sky's blue.
- **The haze fades into dust.** It went straight to `HorizonColor` on the argument that matching it hides the grid's edge, and under dome 13 that uniform is teal while the dome draws its horizon lilac-grey, in the Game and the Testbed alike: the far dunes stood against the sky as a teal band that matched nothing. They fade now into a murk of the horizon's brightness in the sand's hue (`HazeWarmth` 0.85), and so does the dust veil, which used to add the horizon colour itself.
- **Cost:** against `main`, three alternating pairs each, dome 13, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (3200×1800), `nocap`: **−0.02 ms** from the play camera (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-2,-60`, the flat clearing), **+0.35 ms** looking across the dunes (2.11 against 1.76, `campos=35,-8,45 camtarget=70,-14,110`), **+0.27 ms** from above (2.45 against 2.18, `campos=0,25,60 camtarget=30,-14,140`). Checked under domes 1, 3 and 13 in the Testbed and on the Game's menu.

## The mountains (Testbed)

The fifth setting in the NumPad2 cycle (or `scene=mountain`). A snow basin the arena sits in, ringed by tall snow-capped peaks that rise with distance and fade into an alpine haze — the city's clearing-among-towers rebuilt in rock and snow, the platform standing in the basin as it stands anywhere.

The terrain is the desert's machinery with a different field: a camera-centred grid (they share `AcquireGridMesh`), snapped to its cell on the CPU so it does not swim, displaced by a *ridged fractal* heightfield — `Noise.fxh`'s `RidgedFbm2`, five octaves at a 130-unit first spacing — rather than the dunes' smooth sines. It is low and gentle around the arena (world origin) and rises into peaks with distance (`ClearingRadius`/`ClearingTransition`), so the play surface sits in a clearing the way it does in the city, and the basin stays below the platform glass. Its base normal is taken by finite differences — three field taps per vertex — because the ridge folds and the distance ramp would make a closed-form gradient more trouble than it saves; a fine `RockRelief` (band-limited against the footprint) then perturbs it per pixel so the near rock reads as rough stone. Snow lies where the surface faces up (a `smoothstep` on `normal.y`) *and* above an irregular, noise-broken altitude snowline, so the range reads as rock with snow on it rather than one white blanket; rock varies between a dark and a lighter grey-brown in patches; the blue zenith fills the shadows so the snow reads cold where the sun misses it, and the range dissolves into the skyline over `MOUNTAIN_HORIZON_HAZE_DISTANCE` — the strong aerial perspective that says a lot of cold air. **And since #208 the snow carries its own two surfaces**, which it had none of: a wind-streaked drift relief (`SnowRelief` — combed fbm on its own grain, crossed to the rock's, stretched harder and half its strength, tilting the normal only where the snow mask says snow lies) and a sparse sun-facing **sparkle** — a glint in the top 0.75 % of a third-of-a-metre cell lattice, added as a specular-weighted glint after lighting and gated on the snow mask. **The cell says only WHERE a glint is; its size comes from the footprint** (about a pixel and a half, jittered off its cell's centre by whatever the radius leaves over so it can never be cut off flat against the boundary). That was #278: a cell is fixed in *world* units, so the same third of a metre that is the intended 3–4 pixels at the mid slopes' ~0.08 world/pixel is **thirty** pixels on the basin floor beside the arena — and lighting the whole of it there scattered hard white parallelograms over the ground the player stands closest to, on rock rather than snow, since the glint's own comment said "on the lit snow" and the line did not multiply by the mask. (The threshold takes 0.75 % rather than the ~1.5 % this said until #278, because `NoiseHash22` answers in [-1, 1]; the sparser reading is the one that was tuned by eye, so the number stayed and the arithmetic is written down beside it.) Both band-limited against the footprint like everything else in this shader; measured, the pair costs nothing visible at the frame rate (326-372 FPS at 1600×900 ssaa2 uncapped, against the scene's own noise). ****|⚠  THE DRIFT RELIEF WAS THEN CUT OUTRIGHT (#296), at `High` and everywhere else.** The owner asked for something to be given up on the top tier, and this was the menu, measured on the reference desktop at 3840x1600, `ssaa` 2, against a 10.99 ms frame: the **sparkle 0.20 ms**, the **rock's fourth octave 0.31**, the **drift relief 1.08** - close to a tenth of the mountain's whole frame, and photographed on and off from a mid-range camera over the snowfields the two frames cannot be told apart. Best saving, least seen; the mountain's `High` is **10.99 -> 9.85 ms** for it. The cut is all-or-nothing on purpose: three octaves to two saved only **0.27** and to one only **0.63** - the occupancy signature again, partial cuts buying proportionally less and only removing the call crossing back over the threshold. One octave is the 0.63 ms option if the character is ever wanted back. **What #208 was actually complaining about survives, and this is lighter than the state it complained about rather than a return to it**: the snowfields keep the **sparkle** - the half a photograph of snow is recognised by - and they still take their 40 % share of the rock's relief. **`MountainReduced` (#298) then needed a new partner for the sparkle**, half its old pair having been promoted into the authored scene; the rock's fourth octave is it, so `Low` drops the glint and shades the rock on three octaves. Weaker than the pair it replaces - **0.18 ms at `ssaa` 1 and 0.58 at `ssaa` 2**, against 0.40 / 1.36 - which is the honest price of the good half having gone to everyone.

**The peaks read as low-poly for a long time, and the tessellation was never the reason (#86).** The field was four octaves of `1 - |sin(dot(p, direction))|`, and both of that construction's failures were on the screen at once. Plane waves keep their planes: every face carried the same long straight corrugations, which read as wind-rippled sand rather than rock. And its finest octave's ridges ran about **thirty units** apart, so an 82-unit peak had three features from base to summit and its outline against the sky was a smooth simple cone — the "1998" read is a field with no high frequencies in it, not a mesh with visible facets. Measured against the grid, the case closes: the cell is **3.34 units**, so the vertex grid was oversampling the field about ninefold and a denser one would have changed nothing. `Noise.fxh`'s opening calls this exact construction out — "a sum of plane-wave sines keeps its planes however many terms it has, and the eye reads it instantly as the plasma effect of a 1994 demo" — and the library exists to replace it; the mountain was simply the scene that never got converted.

Converting it needed one non-obvious step. `RidgedFbm2` **cannot be dropped in raw**: sampled over the annulus the peaks occupy it means 0.70 with a standard deviation of 0.13, against the plane-wave field's 0.36 and 0.17 — it sits high and narrow, so it lifts the whole basin and leaves shallow dips in it, which draws a white blanket rather than a range (it did, first try). Dropping a 0.24 floor out and **cubing** what is left puts the terrain back on the height distribution the rest of the scene was tuned against — median 0.30 and p95 0.67 against the old field's 0.35 and 0.66, so `Height` 82 and the snowline both stand unchanged — and the curve is itself the ridged look: valleys flatten, crests keep their height. Measured slope says the same, the median falling from 0.77 to 0.67 while the p99 rises from 2.48 to 3.72. That steepening is why `SnowSlope` moved 0.95 → 0.78: the old band wanted a face within 18° of flat, which on the new massing left the snow only in the gullies and a bare rock range in a scene it snows in. Cost, measured on the integrated Radeon at `High`: **~25.0 ms against ~24.2**, about 1 ms for five gradient-noise octaves evaluated three times per vertex (the finite-difference normal) over 129 600 vertices.

**And the same construction was still in the scene's other field until #170 — the fine `RockRelief`, which laid a plaid over the snow.** #86 converted the *massing* and left the per-pixel relief alone; #140 then added a **fourth sine onto it** rather than replacing it, which is how the defect outlived its own diagnosis twice. Confirmed by capture before anything was touched: a regular cross-hatch, plus broad diagonal bands, in the **interior** of the snowfields — which is also what identified the culprit, since the report's other candidate (`snowNoise`, one un-fractalled octave on the snowline altitude) would wobble the snow's *edge* and cannot lay a lattice across its middle. At `RockReliefFrequency` 0.6 the four periods are 2π/f and its halves — about 10.5, 5.5, 2.9 and 1.5 world units — and they interfere by construction. It reaches the snow at all because the slope term that scales the relief floors at **0.4 rather than 0**, so flat, up-facing ground — exactly where the snow lies — still takes 40 % of a field that feeds the *normal*.

- **Four octaves of `Fbm2Combed`**, so there is no shared axis to read as a lattice and the relief still has a **grain** (`ROCK_GRAIN` is the dominant sine's own direction, `ROCK_STRETCH` 2.4) — the savanna's #117 lesson, that isotropic noise reads as gravel. Four and not the two or three the report suggested: #140 added the finest sine to reach crag scale, and the fourth octave is what still reaches it (10.5 world units down to ~1.3, the old spectrum's own span).
- **The frequency is converted, not reinterpreted.** The config's figure is a sine's *angular* frequency (period 2π/f) where a noise field wants a domain scale (a feature is ~1/scale), so it goes in as `f/2π` and the coarsest feature stays where it was authored; passing it straight in would make every feature 6.3× finer and take the broad undulation out of the range. The comment on that property said "features per world unit", which was wrong for the sines too, and now says what it is.
- **The amplitude is bought back by a shader gain (`ROCK_FBM_GAIN` — derived here as 3.0, doubled to 6.0 since #504 by the ⚠ below), not by moving `RockReliefStrength`** — #117's choice, and this scene has the stronger reason for it: both mountain levels bake that figure into their JSON (see the snow dials below), so a new default would not reach the shipping game until `LevelGen` was re-run, and a hand-built level pinning 0.5 would quietly go flat. The three is derived and then checked: the four sines' weights put their RMS near 0.41 where four `Fbm2BandLimited` octaves come to about 0.20, and for equal amplitude and period a sine is half again as steep — which matters because it is the field's *slope* that tilts the normal. Confirmed by eye from a fixed camera against the old field: the relief reads as strongly as it did, and irregularly.
- **Measured: no cost.** Game front end, mountain, dome 13, `quality=medium`, `nocap`, 30 s runs, first six samples discarded: sines **49.9 mean / 51.8 median FPS**, noise **53.3 / 54.8**. Four rotated gradient-noise octaves against four sines came out a touch *faster* — plausibly because each old octave carried a `sin` and a `saturate` guard of its own — but the run-to-run spread is 38–61 FPS on this machine, so the honest claim is that the swap costs nothing measurable rather than that it wins anything. This is the one place the mountain differs from the savanna's own conversion, which measured +0.11 ms for going from two sines to three noise octaves.
- **`snowNoise` was left alone**, deliberately. It is the report's secondary candidate and nothing in the capture points at it; changing it would move where the snow lies, which is a look decision with no fault behind it.

`MOUNTAIN_GRID_N` was raised from 240 to 360 for craggier detail, and **that is what forced the grid builder (`CreateGridMesh` then, `TerrainGridCache` since #589) onto a 32-bit index buffer**: a grid over ~255 a side has more than 65 536 vertices, and the old 16-bit index silently wrapped, so far triangles referenced the wrong vertices and stretched into garbage. On the near-flat sea (380) and savanna (400) that garbage stayed down at the surface and never showed — but the mountain's tall peaks stretched it into strong, regular *dark horizontal bands across the whole sky*, which read exactly like a glare/shading artifact and cost a long hunt down the wrong path (glare threshold, snow brightness, normal band-limiting — none of it). The lesson is in the code: **any terrain grid over 255 a side must use 32-bit indices**; the sea and savanna were silently over the line already and are now correct too.

And it snows. `Snow.fx` is a static buffer of billboard flakes — one quad each, its base a fixed point in a unit cube — animated entirely in the vertex shader: each flake falls, drifts on the wind, sways and tumbles, wrapping with `frac` inside a box that follows the camera, so the snowfall is endless and always around you. Following the camera rather than the world trades a little translational parallax for never popping at a box edge, the right trade for a uniform veil of small flakes. They draw last, alpha-blended and depth-read (the terrain and the cluster hide the flakes behind them) writing no depth — the mountain's own scene, and since #205 the aurora's too, each through its own `SnowConfig` on the one shared flake buffer (see "The aurora"'s own note on why `DrawSnow` had to stop reading `_mountainConfig.Snow` directly to get there). Since #580 each scene draws it through its **own clone** of `Snow.fx`, its look pushed once at load; until then the two shared one effect and re-pushed eleven values every frame. Their color's luminance (~0.76) actually sits *over* `GLARE_THRESHOLD` (0.55); what keeps a near flake from blooming into a glowing orb — which reads as a bug rather than a snowflake — is the feathered speck alpha and the quarter-resolution glare downsample diluting its few pixels. Dim the colour, not the opacity, if flakes ever glow.

**For a long time a flake was a snowball (#85)**, and the reason is worth keeping: a feathered white circle is exactly what a lit sphere looks like. The old speck was a disc feathered only over its outer half at opacity 0.9, and at `FlakeSize` 0.13 one eighteen units out still covered 23 pixels — against a ball a single unit wide, that is a snowball falling past the camera, not weather. Three things separate a flake from a ball, and the shader now carries all three. **Shape**: the silhouette is a six-armed crystal, `cos(6θ)` built from the direction cosines by the double-angle identity — a handful of ALU, and unlike `atan2` no branch cut to seam on — normalised by `1 + lobe` so the arms always reach the quad's inscribed circle and a spikier flake loses the area between them rather than growing out of the quad that carries it. The per-flake lobe depth is decorrelated from the size random and never zero: a flake with no arms is the disc the whole shape exists to escape. **Tumble**: the quad turns in its own plane while the mask stays in flake-local space, so the crystal itself rotates, and a twinkle at twice the spin rate glints it broadside-on. **Focus**: the box is centred on the camera, so `length(boxPosition)` *is* the distance from the lens — a flake nearer than a quarter of `NearFade` is dropped entirely, because real snow that close to a lens is a blur and drawing it crisply is what filled the screen with a white coin. Softening the mask alone would not have done it: wherever a flake crosses something dark the tonemap crushes the feathered rim back to white, which is why `Opacity` came down to 0.6 as well.

The dials — count, size, shape, fall speed, wind — sit in `MountainSceneConfig.Snow` (`SnowConfig`), and `SceneRenderer` rebuilds the flake buffer when the count changes, so the map editor's property grid retunes the snowfall live. They used to be baked into the mountain levels' JSON as well, so a changed default did not reach the shipping game until the generator was re-run; since the format-2 refactor a level names only its scene and **a change to these defaults reaches every mountain level directly**. Retuning for #85 halved the flake and dropped its opacity a third, which cost the veil most of its coverage — hence `FlakeCount` 1400 → 2800.

### Redrawn from references (#504)

Six rounds of fixes had each been settled by looking at a *defect* (a plaid, a lattice, a snowball), and none against a mountain. #504 drew 30 references locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\504`, not in this repository): a range from a valley at dusk, high peaks under a violet sky (the Tower's own dome 8 is a violet twilight), a rock face with talus, a broken snowline, distant ranges in haze, snowfall in a basin, wind-carved snow, a cirque — three seeds each — and six img2img redraws over the Testbed's own frames. What they said, and what changed:

- **Snow and rock are two materials with a crisp edge**, where the scene drew one lilac film. The facing band `RockSlope`→`SnowSlope` was read *linearly*, so every mid-steep face — most of a range — was a 40/60 blend of white and near-black, and under dome 8 that blend is the uniform lilac the scene was. Both the facing band and the altitude snowline are now read through a second, narrow smoothstep (`SNOW_EDGE` 0.16 either side of the middle), and the noise already in them breaks the edge into patches.
- **The rock is near black** (`RockColor` 0.035, `RockColorLight` 0.095, from 0.08 and 0.20 grey-brown): every range the references drew is dark rock against bright snow, and the old rock took the dome's lilac about as strongly as the snow beside it.
- **Steep faces are FLUTED** — dark ribs and white couloirs running straight down, which every face in the references shows. A field varying fast along the face and slowly down it shifts the snow's facing threshold (`FluteSnow`): one gradient noise over (x, y) and one over (z, y), both stretched down world Y, blended by which way the face looks.
  - **⚠ Not by combing the rock relief down the fall line**, the first cut: a combed domain is rotated about the world's origin, so wherever the direction changes — at every facet of the grid — the sampled coordinate jumps by the distance from the origin times the angle, and the faces came out covered in zig-zag chevrons following the mesh.
  - **⚠ Per VERTEX, because per pixel it cost 0.9 ms** of a 10.0 ms frame (23 Mpix, the reference desktop) — and still 0.3 behind a data branch on steep ground, an occupancy-bound pass keeping the registers the branch skips. A couloir is coarse enough for the grid: at `FLUTE_SPACING` 10 the 3.34-unit cell puts three vertices across a period and the field is interpolated to the pixel, where the sharp edge is drawn. Measured free.
- **The basin is snow.** The scene always called itself a snow basin and every cirque in the references has a white floor, but the floor lies below the snowline and took only the 0.15 shoulder — bare rock round the island, which the darker rock turned near black. Inside the clearing's own ramp the floor is snow now. Since that snow faces straight up, **the hemisphere ambient had to change**: up-facing took the zenith *alone*, and dome 8's intensely saturated violet zenith turned the floor into a blue-violet sea. A surface facing up still sees the dome down to the skyline, so it takes 75 % zenith and 25 % horizon now (`0.25 + 0.5 · (n.y·0.5 + 0.5)`), and the shadowed snow is the greyed violet the references draw.
- **Alpenglow.** At a low sun the basin and the lower slopes lie in the range's shadow and only the heights take the light — pink summits over blue snow in every dusk reference. The sun's share climbs from a third at `AlpenglowLow` (5) to all of it at `AlpenglowHigh` (45), scaled by how low the sun is, so a high sun lights everything as before. Not a cast shadow (the map covers the basin, not the range); the floor keeps the basin from going to the ambient alone, since the island in it is lit by the rig's sun.
- **Massifs.** The ridged field alone drew a skyline of crests all of one height, a saw round the basin; every reference has dominant massifs, lower sections and saddles. One octave of noise at `MASSIF_SPACING` 300 scales the field's height between 0.35 and 1.6 — vertex work, three taps a vertex, and nothing on the CPU mirrors this height.
- **⚠ The rock relief had been applied TWICE since #208.** That change split the normal's perturbation in two — `rockNormal` for the snow's decision, then `Perturb(rockNormal, relief + snowRelief)` for the lighting — so the lit normal took the rock relief twice; #296 cut the snow's half and left the second call standing. `ROCK_FBM_GAIN` had been matched by eye with one application in #170. The doubled relief is what had been on the screen for a month and the references ask for more rock detail rather than less, so it is kept as a doubled gain (6.0) and the second `PerturbNormalFromHeight` a pixel is gone.
- **What was left alone**, deliberately: the falling snow's six-armed flakes (#85's answer to a flake reading as a snowball against the balls; the references draw snowfall as soft round dots, which is exactly what #85 rejected), the massing's ridged character (#86), and the violet dome itself, which is the Tower chapter's.
- **Measured against `main`** (desktop, Testbed, 1600×900 at ssaa 4 = 23 Mpix, dome 8, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, 18 s runs, medians, alternating builds; shader sets `b04447bf` against `a4a26456`): the play pin (`campos=0,2,45 camtarget=0,-4,0`) **10.01 → 9.97 ms**, the range from the basin (`campos=0,2,70 camtarget=0,25,-300`) **9.30 → 9.28**, `MountainReduced` 9.15 → 9.09 and 8.45 → 8.49. **Cost-neutral** — the second perturbation it dropped pays for the rest — which is what this scene, 12.35 ms fullscreen against 13.3 at 75 Hz, can afford.

### The "38 FPS mountain" was not the mountain (#270)

**A playtest reported this scene at ~38 FPS fullscreen on a 6900 XT, an order of magnitude under the anchors above. It is not this scene, and it is not a cost at all** — the finding is kept here because here is where it will be looked for. Measured on the reporting machine itself (Ryzen 9 5900X / RX 6900 XT, **3840×1600 panel at 75 Hz**), windowed throughout, `fpscap=` rather than `nocap`:

- **The frame is cheap.** In the Game, `level=Column` (540 balls) under this scene at `quality=high ssaa=2`, windowed 1600×900: **200 FPS under `fpscap=200`** — under 5 ms. The same run under vsync presents at **37.5**. It also holds `fpscap=150`, `100`, **and 76** exactly, and 76 paces the frame the same way a 75 Hz vsync does, with the same idle. So it is not frame pacing, not a GPU clock ramp, and not a frame sitting just over the budget.
- **Paired repeats, the same build and the same level, alternating:** vsync **37.5 / 37.5**, `fpscap=75` **75.0 / 75.0**. Same work, same target rate, twice the frame rate. What costs the frames is the *presentation*.
- **It is not monotonic in supersampling** — ssaa 1 → 75, **2 → 37.5**, 3 → 31.8, 4 → 75 — and no cost curve can be V-shaped with its minimum in the middle. That alone rules out "the scene costs this much" without any further argument.
- **What it takes is a heavy cluster, not this backdrop.** The mountain *alone* never collapses: the Game's front end reads 73 and the Testbed holds a flat 75.0 at ssaa 1, 2 **and** 4, and under this scene the small `level=One` holds 75.0 while `level=Column` halves. (This bullet said until 2026-08-27 that the same level "drawn in the meadow collapses identically", and that was **wrong** — see the correction below. The three bullets above are all measured under the mountain and do not depend on it.)
- **The Testbed shows a milder form of the same thing** (a heavy map at ssaa 2: vsync **71.0** against `fpscap=75` **75.0**, the frame itself 5.03 ms), so this is the shared present path and the Game amplifies it. `PresentationInterval` is `One` in both, not `Two`; the mechanism inside DXGI/MonoGame was **not** pinned down and nothing here should be read as saying it was.

**~38 FPS is 75/2**, and this project has met that number before: `BS3DGame.Quality.cs` records #121/#209's Onion clearing the menu at High and then playing the whole level at "37.5 FPS on a 75 Hz panel, exactly half refresh".

One figure here *is* about cost, and it is the one that matters for a fullscreen player. It has now been measured **fullscreen at the reporting resolution itself** rather than extrapolated from a window: `level=Column` at **3840×1600, `quality=high`** (ssaa 2, so 24.6 Mpix shaded), `nocap` —

| mountain, `level=Column`, dome 8 | | |
|---|---|---|
| launch 1 | 81.0 FPS | **12.35 ms** |
| launch 2 | 80.8 FPS | **12.38 ms** |

**12.35 ms against the 13.3 ms a 75 Hz panel allows** — about a millisecond of headroom, so fullscreen `High` here is genuinely marginal, which is why the half-refresh present above had something to catch. On a slower machine there is no headroom at all.

> **Correction (2026-08-27).** This table was published as *mountain 81.0 against **meadow** 80.8, 0.03 ms apart*, and read as settling the issue's premise that the mountain is "far under every other scene's class". **It was the same scene measured twice.** The second run was launched with `scene=meadow`, but a `Level` file carries its own scene, its own dome and its own weather, and `GameplayScreen.Session.cs` applies all three over the command line — so `level=Column` put the mountain back and the run drew it. The startup line `[game] scene Meadow` printed what was *asked for*, which is what let it stand; the `[fps]` line said `Mountain` every second of both runs and nobody read it. Four other runs in this investigation had the same fault. What the two rows are actually worth is a **repeatability check**, and a good one — two independent fullscreen launches 0.03 ms apart.
>
> **So the cross-scene comparison was never made, and the issue's framing is not answered by one.** It does not need to be: the 38 FPS was measured to be the present path and not a cost at all (the four bullets above, all under the mountain), so "far under every other scene's class" is answered by there being no class difference to explain. The mountain's own fullscreen cost, 12.35 ms, stands — it is the figure the two launches agree on. Comparing scenes properly means either the front end, where no level overrides `scene=`, or levels that genuinely name the scenes, whose maps and domes then differ too. The trap is now trap 11 in `.claude/skills/benchmark/SKILL.md`.

The windowed stand-in this was estimated from first held up, which is worth knowing because it is how the *other* machine can measure a 4K load: ssaa 4 at 1600×900 (23.0 Mpix, within 7 % of the above) predicted **12.0 ms** against the 12.35 measured here. The empty scene at that load is **10.5 ms** (Testbed, fixed camera at `campos=0,2,45 camtarget=0,-4,0`, dome 8), so the cluster and the gun are about 1.9 ms of it.

> **⚠ That stand-in does NOT hold for the cavern, the dream, space or the Moon**, and #209 caught it doing so. Those four *replace the sky*, and since #155 they shade a target the size of the **back buffer** and scale it up — so raising `ssaa` in a small window grows only the resolve over them and not the pass itself, while going fullscreen grows the pass by the whole back-buffer ratio. Measured on the cavern with `level=Onion`: windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 4 reads **4.00 ms**, fullscreen 3840×1600 at ssaa 2 reads **5.96** — the stand-in is a third light, where on the mountain's real geometry it predicted 12.0 against 12.35. Use it for the terrain scenes; measure the sky-replacing four at the size they will be played at.

The instrument this needed did not exist: the Game got the Testbed's **`fpscap=N`** for it, because a vsync-capped level can only ever report "dearer than one refresh" and the whole question was how much dearer. The answer was "not dearer at all", which no vsync-capped run could have said.

**The fix was to stop vsyncing.** The game presents immediately in every mode now and holds the rate on the CPU in `Game/Platform/FrameLimiter.cs` — which is safe precisely because the game is only ever windowed or *borderless* fullscreen (`HardwareModeSwitch = false`, #157), so DWM owns the flip and composites at the panel's rate whatever the app asks for; there is no scanout to race and nothing tears. Verified on the case that was reported, at the default settings and with nothing pinned but the level (which brings its own scene and dome with it): **37.5 → 78.0 FPS, flat** — windowed first, and then **fullscreen at 3840×1600, which is where the report came from**, also 78.0 flat with the back buffer confirmed on the run's own `[fps]` line. (78 and not 75 because the limiter aims a few percent over the refresh — a limiter never pays back an overrun, so one aimed exactly at the refresh drifts slower than the compositor and periodically leaves it nothing new to show.) The limiter sleeps the bulk of each period and spins only the last two milliseconds, unlike the Testbed's benchmark-only idle which spins the lot; see its class doc for why that distinction matters on a laptop.

**What is still open:** the 12.35 ms above is real, and a 75 Hz panel allows 13.3. Fullscreen `High` therefore has about a millisecond of headroom on this machine and none at all on a slower one, so a genuine cost reduction here — or a tier that drops supersampling at 4K — is still worth having. It is now an ordinary "this is a bit dear" question rather than the ten-fold mystery it was filed as.

### The same signature explained four more reports (#209, #167, #166, #165)

**All four were filed from this machine as "does not hold 75 FPS at `High`", and none of them reproduces.** They were re-measured together once #270's retraction made it clear that every one of them carried the vsync signature rather than a cost — #209 reported *~35 FPS*, which is half of the refresh and not a number a frame time produces by accident. Measured in the Game at the condition each was reported at — **fullscreen 3840×1600, `quality=high` (so `ssaa 2x`), the level loaded and playing, `fpscap=400`** so the cap never binds and nothing quantizes — as the median of ~20 one-second readings, each level bringing its own sky as it does in play:

| Issue | Scene | Level | | | Against the 13.3 ms a 75 Hz panel allows |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| #209 | cavern | Onion (`Eleven.json`) | 167.7 FPS | **5.96 ms** | 2.2× inside |
| #165 | savanna | Heart | 120.5 FPS | **8.30 ms** | 1.6× inside |
| #166 | desert | Basket | 106.9 FPS | **9.35 ms** | 1.4× inside |
| #167 | dream | *front end, `preview=Onion`* | 138.9 FPS | **7.20 ms** | 1.7× inside, worst arc 7.70 |

- **#167 could not be measured as filed, and that is itself the answer**: it reports the dream *with a level loaded*, and when it was filed **no shipped level named the dream** — the campaign's ninety entries used nine scenes and that was not one of them. Where the dream actually appeared was the front end, so that is where it was measured, with a map hanging (`preview=`) so it is not the empty backdrop #103 was criticised for measuring. The front-end camera orbits, so that row is a median over 34 s with its worst arc quoted beside it. **The dream is a played scene since #325**: the Mirage, the eleventh block, hangs all ten of its levels here — so the campaign's 105 entries now use eleven scenes, one per chapter, and this row can be re-measured off a level whenever anyone wants the figure the issue actually asked for.
- **The player-facing figure is better still**: run with no `fpscap` at all, Onion sits flat on the limiter at **78.0 FPS** (this panel reports 78 Hz, not 75), and the adaptive probe draws **no verdict** — it leaves the level on `High`. See "The quality tier" in `docs/game-shell.md`, where the retracted step to `Medium` is recorded.
- **The remaining marginal scene is the mountain, not any of these four** — 12.35 ms above, against these 5.96–9.35. Which is worth stating plainly, because the four issues were each opened in the belief that their own scene was the expensive one.
- **And #296 went looking for its cost. It is PIXEL work, and there is no look-identical reduction left in it.** Split by the standard test — the same pin at two supersample factors — on the reference desktop, `Full.json`, fixed camera, windowed 3840×1600: **`ssaa` 1 (no MSAA) 3.19 ms, `ssaa` 2 10.99 ms**, which solves to a **fixed 0.59 ms and 2.60 ms per unit of shaded pixels**. So the vertex side is not the target however it looks on paper: `TerrainHeight` is three taps of a five-octave ridged fbm per *vertex*, fifteen octaves, and it is inside that 0.59.
  - **Two look-identical optimisations were tried and both measure at NOTHING**, which is the part worth keeping so nobody spends the afternoon again. (1) An early **break** out of `Fbm2BandLimited` once its own fade has reached zero — bit-identical by construction, since the weight only falls — measured **11.10 ms against 10.99**, i.e. slightly *worse*: forcing a real `[loop]` costs more than the skipped octaves save, this project's "a runtime branch keeps the registers that cost it" arriving a third time. (2) Hoisting the two `ddx`/`ddy` of the world position shared by the pixel's two `PerturbNormalFromHeight` calls — **11.03 and 11.07**, i.e. noise: the compiler was already common-subexpressing them.
  - **What is left all costs look, and the biggest piece is now priced**: the snow's own two surfaces (`SnowRelief` + `SnowSparkle`, #208's pair) are **1.36 ms at `ssaa` 2 — 12.4 % of the frame** (0.40 at `ssaa` 1). They are not given up at `High`; #298 wired exactly that pair to `MountainReduced` and spends it at `Low`. Which is also #296's own second suggestion arriving: "a tier that drops supersampling at 4K" is what `Medium` already is.

## The meadow (Testbed)

The sixth setting in the NumPad2 cycle (or `scene=meadow`). The Windows XP *Bliss* hill: vivid green rolling to a crisp horizon under a blue cloudy sky, a flat flowering clearing where the arena stands rolling up into hills with distance. It is the desert's and mountains' displaced-grid machinery again (shared `AcquireGridMesh`), but the field is smooth rolling sines rather than dunes or ridges, low around the arena (world origin) and rising with distance, its normal by finite differences. The grass is matte, varied in broad patches so it is not one flat green, and the whole field takes the mood of the dome like everything else.

The motion is the wind and the weather, with no particle system: bands of wind comb through the grass — bright and dark travelling downwind — while the shared cloud shadows drift across the hills, and a fine wind-animated relief tilts the grass normal so the light plays over it. And it is in bloom: wildflowers are scattered through it, little rosettes rather than dots — a ring of petals around a warm golden eye, `|cos(N * angle / 2)|` cutting N rounded lobes (even in the angle, so it survives the `atan2` seam), with a per-cell petal count, size, rotation and colour (white daisies, yellow buttercups, the odd pink one). **A rosette has a shape of its own since #127, not just an albedo** — it used to be a pure colour swap under the ground's own shading, which read as a flat decal printed on the grass. Each petal is now a dome (full mid-petal, dying at the scalloped rim, dipping in the gaps) with the eye a raised boss over them, summed **into the very height field the grass relief feeds `PerturbNormalFromHeight`** — one surface, one perturbation, so a flower catches the sun and the sky hemisphere on its own form; the height is zero by construction at the cell border, so the per-cell hashes cannot tear the field where cells meet. **And the grass's own share of that field is faded out under the rosette (#283)**, which #127 did not do: summed in flat, the flower wore the ground's fine texture and whatever the gust was doing at that point, with its dome merely added on top — so the petals came out washed and blotchy instead of reading as five domes. The colour had always isolated itself correctly (`lerp(grass, flowerColor, flowerMask)` replaces outright); this is the normal being given the same treatment, and the comment above the line had claimed it for some time. **⚠ The weight is the smooth `petalProfile` and deliberately NOT `flowerMask`**: the mask is antialiased over a pixel and a half, and this weight goes into a height field that is *differentiated*, so a mask-shaped falloff puts a pixel-wide step in the derivative and lights a hard ring around every rosette — a new artefact in place of the old one. The profile is linear in radius, so its derivative is bounded by `1/petalEdge` and the crossover is invisible; it is also the same profile the dome is built on, which is what makes the two complementary — where the flower stands tallest the grass texture is gone entirely, and they trade off at the rim. It carries `resolvable` with it, so the grass's texture comes back by itself as the flowers fade out with distance. On top of the normal, a cheap occlusion gradient shades the rim of each petal against its face and brightens the eye's centre (the ambient half a hemisphere term is too broad to give), and a narrow **contact ring** darkens the grass just outside the petals, which is what makes the rosette stand *over* the grass instead of lying in it. Each flower's centre is held within `[size, 1 - size]` of its grid cell so the whole rosette fits inside and no petal is clipped by the cell edge, and the lot — heights included — fades against the pixel footprint so the distant meadow stays clean green rather than a shimmer of dots. The rosettes' form constants (`FLOWER_PETAL_RELIEF`/`FLOWER_EYE_RELIEF`/`FLOWER_CONTACT_SHADOW`, fractions of a flower's own size) live in `Meadow.fx`; the mood dials (density, spacing, size) stay on the config. Grass, hill, wind and flower dials sit at the top of `Testbed.cs`.

**The grass has a material of its own since #281**, because it read as green stone. The issue named the cause: the relief-and-matte recipe is the one the mountain's rock and the island's stone use, so a green bumpy surface and a grey one were the same kind of thing. What was added comes from real meadows, from references rendered locally for the issue (a low Bliss-like field, a close-up, a backlit slope, rocks in grass and wind on hills). Every term has its own dial on `MeadowSceneConfig`:
- **Clumps** (`GrassClumpSize` 0.55, `GrassClumpStrength`): the zero-crossings of a gradient noise one clump across darken the seams between tufts, and a noise at the same scale gives each tuft its own shade and warmth. The whole term fades out against the footprint, so the far field keeps its mean instead of shimmering. The seams were a `Voronoi2` field first; the ridged noise draws the same web of thin lines at one noise instead of nine hashes, and measured 0.43 → 0.36 ms added at the near camera.
- **Tips and hollows** (`GrassTipColor`, `GrassTipStrength`), read off **the very relief that tilts the normal**: where the combed field stands high the lighter, drier tip colour shows, where it dips the green darkens. The relief is normalised to its own amplitude, so this means the same at any `GrassReliefStrength`. It needs no band limit of its own, because the relief's octaves already fade.
- **Blades seen from the side:** a two-octave noise stretched along the ground direction **towards the camera**. Nothing drawn on a ground plane is a vertical stroke, but a stretch along the view direction projects to exactly that: lines radiating from the vanishing point, upright in front of the lens. It is what turned the near field from brushed fur into blades, and it is band-limited to the near field.
- **Dry patches** (`GrassDryPatchStrength`): broad yellow-green islands a few tens of metres across, squared so most of the field stays lush.
- **The velvet** (`GrassSheenStrength` 0.35): the field gives back light where it is seen edge-on, off the terrain's base normal (the blade relief is too fine to decide how a slope is seen). It is what makes a far slope read as a soft carpet; at 0.55 the far hills went pastel.
- **The glow** (`GrassTranslucency`): looking towards the sun the blades transmit it yellow-green, strongest edge-on and gone in the cloud shadows.
- **Both light terms are kept off the flowers**, which are petals and not blades.
- **It costs, and `Low` has a reduced program for it.**
  - **Setup:** measured against unmodified `main` in the Testbed at pinned cameras, dome 13, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (3200×1800), `nocap`, three alternating pairs each.
  - **The authored field adds 0.36 ms** looking down at the near grass (`campos=0,-7,45 camtarget=0,-13,60`) and **0.19 ms** from the play level (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,6,-60`).
  - **`Meadow.fx` now has two techniques over one body**, the forest's pattern (`MeadowField(input, detail)`). `MeadowReduced` drops the two terms that cost, the clump seams and the blade strokes, and keeps what is arithmetic on values already computed: the tips, the dry patches, the velvet and the glow.
  - **The reduced program adds 0.11 and 0.06 ms**, three quarters of the cost back.
  - **Selection:** `SceneRenderer.SceneDetail` selects it, and the Game's `Low` tier takes it. It was checked to draw at all, for the forest's reason, by photographing `detail=0` and seeing the strokes and seams gone.

## The neon city (Testbed)

The last of the seven, at the end of the NumPad2 cycle (or `scene=neon`). Not a new place but the same procedural city relit: the `CityNeon` switch on `InstancedModelRenderer` flips `InstancedModel.fx`'s city technique from warm-and-cool lamps to a neon night — near-black facades and windows glowing pink and blue, the magenta and cyan of a neon street, one dominant hue per tower with the odd off-colour accent, about one window in six sparking the opposite hue, big sign bands wrapping some towers in the contrast colour, and a fraction of it all buzzing. The window brightness is driven well over `GLARE_THRESHOLD` (`NEON_WINDOW_BRIGHTNESS`), so every lit pane blooms into a sign; the facades stay dark under any dome, so the neon carries whatever the sky, but it reads best under a dark or dusk one. The `#if !OPENGL` guard the neon path used to sit behind is gone now that both executables build the file for Shader Model 5.0 — the map editor draws the city, and its neon, through the same shared technique, so V reaches the neon scene there too. It is the heaviest scene — the whole city plus the extra sign/flicker work and the bloom it feeds — so it runs the lowest frame rate. **The campaign's eighth block, the Arcade, plays here** under dome 16, the darkest of the twenty (a near-black zenith over a warm horizon), which is the pairing the facades want: they stay dark under any dome, so a bright one only fights the neon. Measured with a level loaded, the heaviest of the five (588 balls) holds a flat 60.0 FPS at High — ssaa 2×, 1600×900, vsync on, 932 of 1788 towers drawn — on the weakest machine here; what that does *not* say is how much headroom is left, because measuring a frame's real cost needs the presentation cap lifted, and that had been reported crashing the owner's machine (the journal, #228). #250 then found it is not about `nocap` at all: the desktop hard-reset twice in one afternoon under a **capped** run - `Kernel-Power 41` with no bugcheck, no WHEA and no display-driver reset, against ten unexpected shutdowns in thirty days. The instrument for the day the machine is trusted again is `fpscap=N` (`TestOptions.FpsCap`).

## The forest

The eighth `SceneKind`, a temperate woodland glade: a mossy needle-strewn clearing whose hills dress in a dark conifer canopy, scattered with procedural trees, rocks and stumps. The terrain is `Forest.fx` on the shared `SceneRenderer` (the meadow's displaced-grid machinery, cool and floorless of blooms), and **the wood standing on it is shared too** — `ForestScatter` for where every piece stands and `Prazsky.Core.Render.ForestScatterRenderer` for everything it is drawn with, both in the library since #75, drawn through the shared instanced effect. Until then the scatter lived in the Game alone, which is exactly why the Testbed and the editor used to show a bare clearing: the terrain under it was always the shared `SceneRenderer`'s, so all three had the glade and only one of them had the trees in it. Nothing about it was ever the Game's by nature; the split was an accident of where the scene was built. Like Space the scene sat past the end of the NumPad2/V cycle until #380 widened it to the whole enum, so `scene=forest` and loading a forest **level** were how the Testbed and the editor reached it; both keys walk to it now. The game also has it on the scene menu and in the random launch pick. Where in the frame the wood goes stays the caller's, and so does the scene gate — `ForestScatterRenderer.Draw` touches no GPU state, assumes the opaque scene state the callers already set, and draws whenever it is called, so each keeps its own `if (scene == SceneKind.Forest)` and places the draw after the terrain the trees stand on and before the island.

Two tree species split by `ConiferFraction` — a **conifer**, a spruce built as **tiers of branch whorls** (each layer a drooping `LatheMesh` skirt with a shadowed tuck beneath it — the sawtooth silhouette is what says "spruce", where the first build's smooth wobbled cone said "toy"), and a **broadleaf**, a crown of several overlapping **leaf lobes** over a base noise field (a deciduous crown is a cluster of masses; a single noise-bulged sphere still read as a ball, because gradient noise has no structure at the one scale a crown does). `RockMesh` and `StumpMesh` are lathes with strong and modest irregularity. Each kind is built at **several sets of proportions AND its own structural seed** (six spruces, four broadleaves, three boulders, two stumps — and since #462 three snags and two logs, which the daytime forest plants none of): the seed rolls what proportions cannot — the spruce's tier count, taper, droop and pinch; the broadleaf's lobe count, directions and weights; and every part's wobble phase (`LatheMesh` grew an `irregularityPhase` for exactly this — without it every variant undulated identically, and **two lathes that share an edge must share the phase** or the seam opens). `ForestScatter` splits its instances between the variants, because a uniform scale and a yaw do not change a silhouette and the silhouette is what the eye counts — proportions alone made copies of one tree at three sizes, and the eye caught it. So a kind's instances come back as `ModelInstance[][]`, one array per variant, each its own instanced draw (and two per tree, since bark and foliage are two materials and the tint is per-draw) — and each foliage variant takes its own **tint multiplier on the config's linear colour** (`ForestScatterRenderer.CONIFER_TINTS`/`BROADLEAF_TINTS`, means near 1 so the config stays the authored average, encoded to sRGB only after the multiply), because a real stand is never one green. **All of those tints — twenty-five then, thirty since #462 — are encoded once at build time**, which is the one thing the hoist changed rather than moved: the Game re-derived every one of them on every frame the forest was on screen — a `GetSceneConfig`, six `ToVector3` and a `LinearToSrgb` per variant — for values only a config edit can change, so the draw is six indexed loops with no arithmetic in them now. The cost of that is one rule to know: a caller that mutates a config **in place** has to call `ForestScatterRenderer.Replant`, or it goes on drawing the colours the old config asked for (which was why the map editor's live grid re-planted rather than re-scattered, until #522 removed the grid and the `Replant` with it — the config is read once at build now, see "The scene-config panel (MapEditor)" in `docs/formats-and-tools.md`). And the tint tables must be at least as long as their variant arrays, which nothing checked: a seventh spruce used to throw from *inside the draw*, in the one scene where the forest is on screen at all, so it would have been a crash nobody met until they picked the forest. It fails at construction now, naming both lengths. The variety is in the **mesh** rather than in a per-instance stretch: the shader transforms normals by the world matrix with no inverse transpose, so a non-uniform scale would shade a squashed tree as the shape it was authored at. A **rotation** is safe, which is why every instance also gets a small lean (a boulder a much larger tumble). Near-tip lathe rings get their wobble **capped at a share of their own radius**, or the displacement crosses the axis and turns the ring inside out. The scatter is the savanna's clumped generator, planted on the floor through `TerrainMirror.Forest` — a CPU mirror of the shader's height field, and **`MathHelper.SmoothStep` is not GLSL `smoothstep`** (value-interpolate vs clamp-then-hermite); that misuse scattered the first forest six thousand units into the sky, and the same misuse inverted the first crown's bottom tuck.

**Neither scatter kept its plants out of each other until #108**, and the forest and the savanna had the bug for the same reason: positions were drawn independently, with **82 %** of them clumped around a cluster centre at a density that *rises* inwards (`off² * ClusterSpread`), and nothing anywhere compared a candidate against what was already standing. Two landing on top of each other was therefore the expected case rather than bad luck — measured on the shipped forest config (240 trees, 20 clusters, spread 34), **67 overlapping pairs, 35 of them badly**. `ScatterSpacing` is the rule in one copy for both: a packing fraction (0.72 — crowns in a real stand interlace, and a wood whose every crown clears every other reads as an orchard), a retry budget, and a signed clearance test so a position that cannot be made good is at least made *least bad*. Retries measured against that same config: 1 → 67 pairs, 2 → 11, **4 → 0**, and 8/16/32 also 0; the shipped budget is 8 as headroom for a denser config, and it costs nothing because the loop stops at the first position that clears. Nothing is ever dropped for want of room — a forest that quietly plants 180 of the 240 trees it was asked for would be the worse bug. Each caller keeps only what genuinely differs: the forest reads its radii off the crown radii the meshes are built at, the savanna off the very width `Acacia.fx` sizes the billboard with (so a bush is held to a third of a tree's room rather than all of it, which needed the tree/bush roll moved *before* the position). Two consequences to know: the trees' two species share one occupancy list (they are scattered separately for the groves, so a per-call list would have stopped each species colliding only with itself), while rocks and stumps keep their own — a boulder at the foot of a tree is what a wood looks like. And **a scatter re-rolls at an unchanged seed**, because the number of rng draws now varies with how crowded the ground is.

**The wood did not take the dome's mood, and the lighting was not what was wrong with it (#108).** Every scatter renderer is enrolled for the sky rig in all three executables, the rig tints the key light and the hemisphere ambient, and both reach the trees — proved by making the pigment a neutral grey for one run, whereupon the very same trees took a sunset dome fully, salmon-pink, with not a line of the lighting path changed. The trouble is the **pigment**: `FoliageColor` is `(0.042, 0.115, 0.026)` in linear radiance and `ConiferColor` `(0.028, 0.075, 0.026)`, whose red and blue channels are near zero — a coloured light has almost nothing to multiply there, so the crowns stayed the same green under every sky while the ground around them turned. A saturated pigment not taking a coloured light is correct physics; it is simply not the picture this scene wants, so the pigment is what moves. `ForestScatterRenderer.ApplySkyTint` carries every tint a third of the way (`SKY_TINT_STRENGTH` 0.34) towards the dome's hue — the same idea and nearly the same figure as `SkyLightRig.SKY_TINT_STRENGTH`, which lerps the key light half of the way to the horizon rather than all of it, and for the same reason: far enough short of 1 that a spruce is still a spruce. **Hue only** — the dome's tint is rescaled to the pigment's own luminance before the blend, so a dark dome cannot darken the wood nor a bright one light it. Called from the same pass that applies the rig (all three executables have one) and guarded on the value, so re-encoding the tints happens on a dome switch and never on the frames between, which is what the tint cache exists for.

The first build's trees rendered as **black eggs sitting on the ground**, and every cause is a trap worth keeping:

- **The crown used its height as a semi-axis**, spanning `[baseY − h, baseY + h]` — the bottom pole a unit under the ground and the whole trunk buried inside the egg. A crown spans `[trunkHeight, trunkHeight + crownHeight]`, so the trunk stays visible under it.
- **Every lathe profile was traced bottom-up**, the reverse of the direction `LatheMesh` documents (top → outside → underside), so trunk, rock and stump were all **inside out** — the sphere-winding trap in its lathe form: nothing disappears, the far side is drawn with inward normals, and a shallow inside-out dome even reads *lit* (you are looking at the interior of its bottom disc, whose normal faces the sun).
- **The config colours are linear radiance, but the material-diffuse uniform is sRGB**: the instanced shader decodes `DiffuseColor` with `SrgbToLinear` at the tap (ball tints are display values), so linear-authored greens landed near black — 0.16 decoded to 0.02. The scatter draws encode once at the boundary (`ColorSpace.LinearToSrgb`); the terrain shader's own colours stay linear like every scene shader's. **And a tinted draw is not a pass-through**: `Draw` reduces the material diffuse to its luminance and multiplies by **1.25**, a boost calibrated for models whose brightest material is 0.8 — over a white material that lands every encoded colour half again too bright, so the scatter meshes are built at 0.8 (`ForestScatterRenderer.SCATTER_MATERIAL_DIFFUSE`) and the two cancel.
- **A procedural-mesh renderer defaults to a white specular** (`(1,1,1)` power 16 — right for vinyl and glass), which on a dark crown reads as wet obsidian. The scatter passes matte per-draw `BasicEffectParams` — three of them, built once in `ForestScatterRenderer` (foliage, bark and weathered stone) out of the scene's flat ambient fill, which is the only thing about the scene that component is told — and a **zero** specular there means "no override, use the white default", so matte is a dim non-zero colour, never zero.
- **A `LatheMesh` span whose first ring sits on the axis got a `+Y` face normal for the whole span.** The ring tangent was taken from the span's first ring, and a ring at radius 0 has all its segments at one point — so the cross product collapsed and the fallback fired. That is *right* for a flat top (a disc's first ring is its centre) and wrong for anything that comes to a point: the fix takes the tangent from whichever of the span's two rings is off the axis, without which the top third of every conifer shaded as though it faced the sky.
- The forest belongs in **`SceneRenderer.IsSolidTerrainScene`** — its clearing is flat ground at the island's foot like the meadow's, so the terrain shader cuts the island's footprint out and the dark pit shaft has to be drawn behind the drain's glass, or the funnel reads as a glass ring over bright sky haze. It was omitted from the hand-kept list in the game **and** from the Testbed's own, which is the exact failure a duplicated classification invites and the reason there is one shared list now (#75, above). The five are mountains, meadow, savanna, desert and forest.

The terrain took a second pass after the first build read as **blurred, low-res mounds**, and each fix names the cause:

- **The hills' sine octaves are domain-warped** before they are summed (two long sines bending the sampling plane — exact to mirror on the CPU), because summed plane waves keep their planes however many there are, and straight wavefronts are what made the hills a *row* of smooth mounds. Five octaves rather than three, the two new ones filling the gap between hill and lump scales — the band a "smooth blob with bumps on it" is missing — with amplitudes summing to 1 so `HillHeight` keeps its meaning.
- **The floor lumps run under a broad mask** and gained a third wave: unmasked, two sines interfere into an even weave across the whole floor, which reads as a pattern rather than as ground; masked, the roughness comes in patches the way roots and hollows do.
- **The base normal is per pixel now** — the savanna's fix, three `TerrainHeight` taps in the pixel shader — because interpolating the coarse displaced grid's per-vertex normal leaves a Mach band at every cell edge, and on this scene it read as the whole floor being slightly out of focus.
- **The needle relief was four octaves, band-limited per octave** until #281 made it noise (below) (the ball relief's rule — one global fade flattens the lot at arm's length), and a **needle-scale colour grain** joins it near the camera: the relief tilts the light, but a floor with no fine *albedo* change still looks airbrushed however it is lit. Both fade to nothing before they can shimmer.
- **The near slopes' canopy gained a third, crown-top mottle scale** under its own band limit — the two broad scales alone read soft-focus exactly where the hills fill the frame.
- Measured on the APU (front end, 1600×900, `quality=high`, dome 13, 16 s runs): **17.2 → 20.0 ms**. The +2.8 ms is mostly the per-pixel normal's three field taps; the forest stays inside the terrain-scene band (the mountain is 19.1) and above the adaptive probe's floor on this machine.
- **The floor's second pass — FBM mottle, triplanar normal variation, slope-driven colour and procedural tree shadows — was the most expensive thing on this scene, and it was not free** (the mottle and the normal variation are gone since #281, below). Measured on the desktop GPU (front end, 1600×900, `quality=High`, dome 13, `nocap`, two 18 s runs each): **540 → 371 FPS**, i.e. 1.85 → 2.70 ms, **+0.84 ms and −31 %**. That is at windowed 1600×900; the work is per-pixel on the floor, so it scales with resolution and 4K is the stated base.
- **What that cost actually is: occupancy, not work — and it took a full attribution sweep to find out (#118).** Removing any *one* piece saves **nothing at all**: without the triplanar normal variation 2.71 ms, without the tree shadows 2.72, with the floor's FBM cut from four octaves to two 2.71, against the full 2.69. Removing *pairs* saves a great deal — normal variation and shadows together 2.09, normal variation and the mottle's FBM 1.97, all three 1.85. A cost with that shape is not per-feature work being done; it is the pass sitting below an occupancy threshold that only a pair of removals climbs back over. **So a per-feature quality dial would have bought nothing**, and neither does a runtime branch: a `float FloorDetail` uniform with two `[branch]`es on it measured **2.72 against the full look's 2.70**, because the register footprint is fixed when the shader is compiled — the branch skips the work and keeps the registers that were the actual limit.
- **Hence a second compiled program.** `Forest.fx` has two techniques over one body (`ForestFloor(input, detail)`, called with a literal from `ForestPS` and `ForestReducedPS` — MGFX cannot parse a uniform argument in a `compile` statement, so it is two entry points rather than the tidier `compile … ForestPS(true)`). `SceneRenderer.SceneDetail` picks between them and the Game sets it from the tier: **`Low` drops both extras and `High` and `Medium` keep the authored floor** (it was everything below `High` until #298 — see "The quality tier" in `docs/game-shell.md`). Measured with supersampling pinned at 2× so only the technique differs: **High 2.69 ms, Medium 2.10** — the reduced program recovers 0.59 of the 0.84 the pass added, and `High` is untouched. The Testbed and the map editor leave `SceneDetail` at its default 1, since they are where the scene is looked at and tuned.
- A trap this set on the way: `SceneDetail` began as a plain auto-property, and `ApplyForestParameters` — which runs from the constructor and on a config change — had already chosen the technique by the time any host set it, so the reduced program was compiled, shipped and **never once drawn**, at every tier, while looking perfectly correct. It measured as "the technique switch saves nothing", which is the same reading as the uniform branch above and would have been believed. Caught by making the reduced technique output flat red for one run: the floor stayed green. The property re-selects on write now.
- **The floor colour answers the form**: `slope` (from `baseNormal.y`) keeps the moss and bilberry on the flats and washes the banks to litter and bare earth (since #281; it washed flats to moss green before that). Without it the floor reads as one material however it is lit, because the colour never answers the shape it sits on. The four-octave `ForestFbm` replaces the single `CloudNoise` tap the patches and litter used to read off — one octave reads as smooth blobs where four sum to broken ground.
- **The tree shadows are cast by trees that do not exist.** The terrain shader does not know where the scatter's ~240 instances stand, and passing them in would be a limit and a cost; `ForestShadow` plants a *virtual* tree per hash-grid cell instead and marches the sun ray across the cells. The two never argue about who shadows whom because the shadow's density rides `canopyRamp` — 0 inside the clearing, where the real trees stand and only their own shadow should show.

**The floor is litter, not lawn, since #281.** The owner's playtest said the forest floor read as "lines and waves" and that a real forest floor is not that green, and both were true of what was drawn: the fine relief was four plane-wave sines, and the flats were washed to moss green. It was redrawn from references rendered locally for the issue (a clearing seen across, the floor seen from above, a glade with the forest around it):
- **The relief is band-limited noise** (`Fbm2BandLimited`, three octaves, in `NeedleRelief`). Summed plane waves keep their planes however many there are, and that is exactly the "lines and waves" of the report.
- **The base is a carpet of rusty needle litter** (`LitterColor`, `LitterColorDark`) with bare earth (`EarthColor`) where its mottle is lowest. Everything green lies on top of it: **moss cushions** (`ForestColor` and `ForestColorDark` are their lit and shaded greens now, `MossCoverage` 0.4) with ragged edges, a darker contact rim in the litter and a low rise in the relief (`MossHeight`); **bilberry** patches (`UndergrowthColor`); and **dry grass** where the clearing stands open (`DryGrassColor`). Near the lens the litter carries **needle strands**, noise combed along a direction that wanders with the mottle.
- **Three fields draw all of it, and that is the cost story.** The first build gave every layer a field of its own and cost **+0.64 to +1.04 ms** against the green floor (three pairs, 3200×1800). With one field for how damp the ground is (moss and bilberry where it is high, dry grass where it is low, which is also how a clearing sorts them), one for the litter's mottle (and the earth where it is lowest) and one fine field (cushion edges, bilberry clumps, moss tone), the picture is the same. **`VaryNormal` and `ForestFbm` are gone**: sixteen noise taps a pixel of triplanar normal variation, added over the sine relief to break it up, and redundant once the relief is noise.
- **The reduced program's pair is now the needle strands and the tree shadows.** Photographed at `detail=0` to prove it draws.
- **Cost:** **cheaper than the green floor.** Against `main`, three alternating pairs each, dome 13, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (a 3200×1800 target), `nocap`: **−0.27 ms** from the play camera (2.43 against 2.69, `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-2,-60`), **−0.33 ms** looking across the floor (2.42 against 2.75, `campos=35,-8,45 camtarget=70,-14,110`), **−0.44 ms** from above the clearing (2.85 against 3.29, `campos=0,25,60 camtarget=30,-14,140`). The reduced program never had the normal variation to give back, so it is the one that pays for the new layers: **+0.12 ms** across the floor (2.23 against 2.11) and **+0.16 ms** from above (2.56 against 2.40); against the new full program it still saves 0.19 and 0.29.
- The hills' canopy, the tree shadows, the wind's shade and the haze are unchanged. `Aurora.fx` keeps its own copy of the old sine relief under its night sky and was not touched.

The wooded hills are **colour, not geometry**: past the clearing the floor dresses in a dark canopy mottle (`TreelineColor`, grove- and crown-scale noise) with its **own ramp, nearer and tighter than the hills'** — the slopes that fill the frame are the transition band itself, and a canopy keyed to the full hill ramp only arrives where the haze already owns the colour. Its edge rides the grove noise so the woods begin on a ragged line. The horizon haze is **two-stage**: distant forested ridges turn *blue* (the air scatters skylight), so the hills recede first into a zenith-derived murk and only the last stretch melts into `HorizonColor` — reaching it exactly at `HorizonHazeDistance`, which must stay at or inside the terrain grid's half-extent or the mesh edge shows against the dome. And **`CloudNoise` is gradient noise, which clusters hard around zero**: measured over four million samples, one sigma is 0.18, the 5–95 band is ±0.30 and only the extremes approach ±0.8 — so the usual `* 0.5 + 0.5` spans about 0.35–0.65 and the ACES curve flattens that into one tone. Spread it by ~1.6 (and saturate) before using it as a mottle, here and anywhere else a noise has to *read* on screen.

## Space (Game)

The ninth `SceneKind`: the island floats in deep space under a long-exposure astrophoto sky — a dense starfield, the Milky Way with its dust lanes, three coloured emission nebulae, scattered distant galaxies, one large banded planet, and a **volume the island is inside of**. Physically an eye in orbit would see mostly black; this is deliberately the *picture* of space, which is what the eye recognises as space. `Space.fx` on the shared `SceneRenderer`, so all three executables compile it; only the Game can select it (`ScenePage`, the random pick, `scene=space`), and the Testbed reaches it with `scene=space` too — the Testbed's NumPad2 and the editor's V both walk to it since #380, as they do to the forest.

**It was the first backdrop to replace the SKY rather than the ground — the dream and the cavern followed it, then the Moon (which alone keeps a ground under its replaced sky), and `SceneRenderer.ReplacesSky` is now the four of them — and every difference below follows from that one fact.** The other eight leave the dome standing over their ground; space, the dream and the cavern have no ground, no horizon and no weather.

- **One full-screen pass, not a displaced grid.** A four-corner quad already in normalized device coordinates, the view ray recovered per pixel from an `InverseViewProjection` uniform (`Matrix.Invert(View * Projection)` — row vectors, as everywhere here), drawn `DepthStencilState.None` so it writes no depth and the island, cluster and gun simply cover it. It covers the **whole** frame rather than a hemisphere, which is why the clear colour goes to black in this scene and the wide-aspect corner band the other scenes clear the horizon for cannot happen. No `OriginXZ` (nothing on the ground to swim), no `IslandHoleRadius` (nothing drawn under the island), no cloud hook.
- **One layer of it is marched through, and every other one is not — which is what the scene was missing.** Everything above `StarNestVolume` in `Space.fx` is a function of the view **direction** alone; the camera's position enters the shader only to build the ray. So the whole sky was a painted dome: move the camera and not one pixel changed, turn it and the picture slid rigidly, near and far together. A starfield is entitled to that (real stars have no parallax worth drawing) but it left the scene with no depth of its own to be inside of, and it read as a texture rather than as a place. The volume is stepped along the ray **from the camera's own position**, so structure one step out crosses the frame faster than structure eight steps out — parallax, from real depth. Measured directly: two frames taken along the *same* view direction from positions 45 units apart differ by a mean **|ΔRGB| of 39.8** over 14 000 samples, and since every other layer is direction-only by construction that difference is entirely the volume. Before it, the same test is exactly zero.
  - The field is Pablo Roman Andrioli's **Star Nest** (MIT, so usable rather than merely admirable). Space is folded into mirrored cells by the triangle wave `2T·|frac(p/2T) − 0.5|`, which repeats the volume for free and mirrors at every wall so no seam shows — **spelled with `frac` since #147, not as the original's GLSL `abs(T − mod(p, 2T))`**: GLSL `mod` is floor-based where HLSL `fmod` truncates toward zero, so the straight port stopped mirroring below zero and **jumped by the full 2T period at every `p = −2T·k`, k ≥ 1**. The drift walks `p.z` down through those planes — the first crossing is derived to enter the marched span between 483 and 772 s at the shipped ~33-unit camera, a circle per march step sweeping the sky over its window — and it was photographed for #147 with `SpaceTime` temporarily pinned to 661 s, where all eight circles land within half a degree of one great circle (derived) and stack into one hard, frame-crossing brightness step. The fix leaves the look the layer was tuned at untouched (a pinned before/after pair at t = 30 s differs by no more than one display code anywhere in the sky) and at 900/1800 s keeps the authored statistics — the sky's darkest percentile within two codes, its mean within 9 % (0.5 % at 900 s, 8.2 % at 1800 s) — so `VOLUME_HAZE`, `Strength`, `Opacity` and the tint were re-judged against the void and deliberately kept. Cost, measured at the repro vantage (1920×1080, `ssaa 2`, `nocap`, the desktop RX 6900 XT, two interleaved runs each, 18 readings per run): **442.2/442.4 FPS with `frac` against 436.1/437.1 with `fmod`** — the correct fold is also marginally the cheaper one (the march kernel compiles to 123 instruction slots against 124, d3dcompiler ps_5_0 /O3: `fmod` pays a sign-select for its truncation). Inside a cell an iterated map `p = abs(p)/dot(p,p) − FORMULA` runs — a sphere inversion, a fold into the positive octant, a translate — whose attractor is a filigree of sheets. What is drawn is not where the point lands but **how far it moved** at each iteration, summed: points near the attractor move little, so the sum is an inside-out distance field and the filaments come out as dark tracery inside a glowing web. Colour comes from `(s, s², s⁴)` along the ray, so the near web is cool and the far one warm, which is a cheap stand-in for scattering and most of why the field reads as having a front and a back.
  - **Four things had to change for it to belong to this scene rather than replace it.** It is entered at the camera (above). It is rotated into a basis of its own, or the fold's cell walls read as a grid squared up with the island and the gun. The world is scaled down hard on the way in (`Volume.Scale`, the parallax dial) — the cells are 0.85 units wide in the field's space against an island 26 across and a camera 33 out, so raw world coordinates would fly the eye through thousands of cells and the whole thing would boil. And it carries an **extinction**, which the original needs none of because it draws nothing behind itself; here a bright web that lets every star shine through its densest knots reads as a decal over the sky. The extinction comes off the emission's own luminance rather than a second accumulator, so it cannot disagree with what is drawn, and it is a reciprocal so it can never go negative however hard the strength is turned up.
  - **The flat haze term is the one number that decides whether this is an addition or a takeover.** The original's `v += fade` adds a constant lift to every pixel — the series sums to about 3.7 — and at full weight it took the void from the 3–4 display codes this scene is authored around to a uniform grey that swallowed the Milky Way's band and both bright nebulae whole. Star Nest is free to do that because nothing else is in its frame. Here it is kept at `VOLUME_HAZE` = **0.12** (0.22 until #505 found it was laying a wash over every pixel of the frame — see "Redrawn from references" below), and its job is the thin stuff *between* the filaments and nothing more.
  - **One iteration may move the point at most `VOLUME_JUMP_CAP` (8), and on the first step `VOLUME_JUMP_CAP_EYE` (30) — the fix for #401's "disco strobe".** The inversion divides by `dot(p, p)`, so an iterate that lands near the origin is thrown out by the reciprocal, and summed and cubed that one jump lit a disc of the web far over the glare threshold: bright orange-white points with a bloom halo that came and went within a third of a second, because the drift alone carries a near-singular iterate off the step it was sampled on. It was the volume and **not the star lattice** the issue first suspected — a pinned Testbed camera shows them appear and vanish with time alone. A CPU copy of the march over 24 cameras and times puts an ordinary iteration's move at 4.4 (99th percentile) and 7.8 (99.9th), with the singular ones reaching 219 and the lit discs radiances up to 141; the cap sits just above the 99.9th percentile, so only that tail is touched.
    - **The disc stays; only its brightness is the cap's to set.** A throw leaves the next iterate at about `−FORMULA` on every axis whichever pixel it came from, so every pixel inside the disc runs the same orbit afterwards, and that orbit's own moves are 4.7, 4.2 and then a second throw of 17 and 16. The disc is a flat patch of one shared sum — the fractal's structure, not a sampling fault — and clipped at 8 it is a faint knot in the web rather than a flash.
