So the mare is a basin with a rim (`HighlandHeight` 40, `HighlandInnerRadius` 190 → `HighlandCrestRadius` 310): the skyline is now the crest, ~4.5° over the lens, and past it the curvature takes back over — quadratic drop against a saturated rise, so elevation falls monotonically outward and the grid's edge stays hidden behind the crest. `HighlandSaddleFloor` (0.5) is the reason the belt never dips to the plain: a saddle that did would be a notch the eye looks straight through, onto the ground the crest exists to occlude, and at a shallow enough angle that is the clip plane.
- **The belt's shape is `MareBase`'s noise, reused rather than its own — and that reuse is the whole reason it is affordable.** `MoonHeight` is evaluated **four times per pixel** (the vertex tap plus the normal's three), so a private two-octave massif field cost eight extra gradient noises a pixel and measured **2.0 ms of an 18.9 ms frame** at ssaa 2 on the reference APU — 12 % of the frame for a silhouette the plain's own undulation carries for nothing. Driving the belt off the already-computed `MareBase` value put the frame at **17.7 ms against 16.2 ms with no belt at all**: ~1.5 ms for the feature, of which about half is simply the extra terrain now covering pixels that used to be black sky. It buys a correlation — where a massif stands high the mare undulation runs high too — which is invisible and can stay that way: `MareBase` moves the plain by about two units against the belt's forty, and the belt only exists 190 units out. It also reads *better* than the private field did, its 91/32-unit octaves giving a craggier crater-rim skyline where the hand-picked 160/65 gave a smooth swell.
- **The sky is the shared starfield plus an analytic Earth, and the Earth is deliberately small.** The star lattice moved out of `Space.fx` **verbatim** into `Stars.fxh` (uniforms included, so one C# push routine serves both effects) rather than being copied — the two no-atmosphere skies want an identical field, and a second copy would be the duplicated-classification mistake in shader form. No Milky Way, no nebulae, no galaxies, no volume: an Apollo sky is stars on black and the Earth, tuned sparser than space's through the same `SpaceStarsConfig` shape. The Earth reuses the space planet's machinery (ray-sphere at the distance that gives the configured angular radius, `smoothstep` terminator — narrower than the gas giant's, Earth's atmosphere being thin — `NightAmbient` floor) with continents instead of bands: a 3-octave `Fbm3` thresholded at the real ~30 % land fraction, arid belts off a second coarser field, fractal-broken polar ice, and swirled weather whose mask band is deliberately high and narrow — the first build's wide band handed back a white planet with blue flecks, and the marble must stay blue with white on it. **Its angular radius ships at the true 0.95° (1.9° across, ~30 px at the default window), which is an expectation-setting decision, not a bug**: far smaller than the mental image cropped Apollo photos leave, and saying so up front is why the dial is in the config. Being that small its lit body — clouds included — stays **under** `GLARE_THRESHOLD`, unlike the space planet's limb: a 30-pixel disc is no long coherent arc, and a small bright thing the glare's sparse grid samples stochastically flickers. Only the thin atmosphere rim carries a touch of bloom, through a saturated blue whose luminance stays modest, and the rim is lit **per point of the ring** off the sun's side of the disc — one global phase factor drew a complete blue circle around a gibbous Earth, night limb included, which reads as a ring around the planet rather than as its air. Continent/cloud octave counts are chosen against the disc's ~30 on-screen pixels: a fourth octave contributed speckle, not coastline.
- **The light rig is the one rig whose ground half outshines its sky half** (`MoonLightingConfig`): the sky is black and the sunlit regolith below is the only diffuse source there is — Apollo photographs fill their shadows from the ground, not the sky. The terrain's own sun (`MoonTerrainConfig.SunColor`) is the config's and never the frame's dome-derived `SunColor`, the `TryGetLightRig` argument painted onto the ground: there is no dome, and a dome-derived sun on a domeless ground would be a lie. The terrain's fill is a tiny non-zero `AmbientColor` (at zero the shadowed wall of every crater reads as a hole cut in the ground — the space scene's island lesson at terrain scale) plus a directional **earthshine** term off the Earth's own direction; the same figures feed `TryGetMoonEarthshine`, the scene point light (the planetshine pattern — a real light rather than more ambient, so the metallic drain beads get a highlight out of it), so the ground and the island cannot disagree about how bright the Earth is. The ambience bed is the quietest in the set, deliberately — near-static sub-bass under the faintest slow hiss, no wind (wind is the one thing this scene cannot have); it also unseated `ProceduralAmbience.BakeScene`'s cavern-as-`default:` arm, which is exactly how a thirteenth scene would have silently played cave drips.
- **Measured** (reference APU, 1600×900, `nocap`): Game front end **~17.7 ms at `High`** (ssaa 2) — desert-class, cheaper than the city — of which the highland belt is ~1.5 ms. No reduced program: the scene sits comfortably inside the terrain-scene band, and the figures above are why (the single-cell crater field and the depth-read sky are each worth an order of magnitude against the first build, and the belt's borrowed noise a further 1.2 ms).

### Redrawn from references (#508)

The scene names its target in its own first line — the Apollo-photo look — and nobody had put an Apollo photograph beside it. #508 drew 29 references locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\508`, not in this repository): a surface photograph at eye height, the terminator at a very low sun, a highland skyline, Earthrise, a fresh crater from the ground, the Earth over the horizon, an oblique orbital view — three seeds each — and six img2img redraws over the Testbed's own frames. Three things came back from every one of them, and the scene had none of them.

- **The sun is LOW.** Every surface photograph is long black shadows off every rock and a black bite out of every crater, which is a sun a handful of degrees to a couple of dozen up — the landings were timed for it. This scene stood on the 35° that every domeless scene shares (`SkyLightRig.DOMELESS_SUN_DIRECTION`), where a crater's shadow is a sliver. It has its own now (`MoonLightingConfig.SunElevationDegrees` 16, `SunAzimuthDegrees` 40), asked for through `SceneRenderer.TryGetSunDirection` after the rig has chosen between the dome's sun and the domeless one — so the terrain, the island, the cluster and the Earth's phase all answer one direction. **It is a constant of the scene and not of any dome**, which is what keeps #220's reason for holding these scenes off the level's sky byte intact: nothing swings it but this config.
- **A crater is a bowl with a shadow in it, and the shadow is ANALYTIC.** The field shaded its bowls by facing angle alone, which draws a crater as a soft dimple lit on one side — a plain of them reads as a golf ball. `CraterLayer` now solves its own shadow: a point in the bowl is dark when the lip between it and the sun stands higher than the sun's ray climbs on the way to it. The distance to that lip is the ray–circle exit in the crater's own radii; the heights are the profile the layer already has. **No march and no extra taps** — the crater knows everything about itself — and the gradient taps' copies are dropped by the compiler, the way the ejecta output already was.
  - **The edge never narrows under a pixel** (`pixelCells · shadowSlope`), or a far crater's shadow crawls as the lens moves — the same rule everything else in this shader is band-limited by.
  - **And the craters are mostly SHALLOW now** (`depth = lerp(0.25, 1.0, roll²)`, from a flat 0.55–1.0): a real field is mostly old craters worn down to dishes with a few fresh deep ones, and once every bowl cast a shadow the old spread filled every one of them with black and the mare read as a sponge. The same roll drives the ejecta, so the few deep ones are also the ones with bright fresh rims — young is deep *and* bright.
- **⚠ Boulders were tried and rejected, and the reason generalises.** Every Apollo surface photograph is full of rocks with hard shadows lying off them — the strongest "this is the Moon" cue there is at eye height — so a painted rock went in: a disc on a single-cell lattice lit on its sunward side, its shadow the capsule the disc sweeps away from the sun, both held inside the cell by a margin. It read as **scattered black dashes and holes**. This plain is seen at a grazing angle from everywhere the game looks at it, and a disc painted *on* the ground foreshortens to a sliver there while the rock it stands for stands *up* out of it — so what stayed visible was the shadow without its rock. **A rock needs geometry**, and the Moon has no CPU mirror of its height field to plant one on (the crater lattice is hashes all the way down). It would be a scatter ring in the flat clearing, where the height is known — and from the play camera the deck occludes exactly that ground (see the highland belt's own note), so it would be for the front end and the chapter intro only.
- **The Earth is bluer.** The references' marble is white swirl over deep blue with brown-green land showing through; this one wore yellow blotches and a halo. `CloudAmount` 0.55 → 0.8, `LandColorArid` from a saturated tan to a dull brown, `RimStrength` 0.5 → 0.3 (the references' air is a thin bright edge, not a glow round a lamp).
- **Measured against `main`, and it came out CHEAPER** (desktop, Testbed, 1600×900 at ssaa 4 = 23 Mpix, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, 18 s runs, medians, alternating builds; shader sets `a4a26456` against `11a1d7ac`): the play pin (`campos=0,-4,45 camtarget=0,-9,0`) **6.45 → 5.94 ms**, the plain (`campos=40,-9,120 camtarget=70,-14,160`) **5.60 → 5.42**. Three pairs each, every pair the same way round. **No mechanism is claimed** — the shadow is a dozen instructions on a tap the shader already made, and nothing was removed but the boulders that never shipped — so the honest reading is that the pass did not get dearer, and that its register allocation seems to have landed better.

## The outback

The thirteenth `SceneKind` (#112): weathered red-orange monoliths standing on a wide, near-flat spinifex plain, the island in a clearing among them — Uluru and Kata Tjuta rather than a Sahara. `Outback.fx` + `OutbackSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Terrain, Surface, Air, Birds — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=outback` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like the forest and the sky-replacing scenes it sits past the end of the NumPad2/V cycle, and the editor reaches it by loading a level whose scene names it.

**It is the desert's arid sibling and deliberately shares nothing of its look.** The Sahara is golden dunes whose only silhouette is their own crests, with a braided ripple surface; this is red ground under **rock** — a skyline of bornhardts with the gullies water has cut down their flanks, and a cellular spinifex ground rather than a wave one. It is a `IsSolidTerrainScene` (flat clearing at the island's foot, the island's footprint `clip()`ed out, the dark pit shaft behind the drain's glass, and therefore no dive for the drop cinematic), it takes the dome like every terrain scene, and it shares the savanna's and desert's [flock of birds](#the-savanna-testbed) — the one flock's per-bird state is sized from all three configs' counts.

- **The monoliths are geometry, on a jittered single-cell lattice** — the Moon's crater trick, the space starfield's and the meadow's wildflowers': a formation is held inside its own cell by its own reach, so only the pixel's own cell is ever read (three to five hashes) rather than the nine a neighbourhood walk costs. That matters more here than anywhere, because the field is evaluated **four times per pixel** (the vertex tap plus the normal's three). Each formation is two lobes at its own bearing, elongation and squareness (a bornhardt comes in shoulders — Kata Tjuta is thirty-six domes leaning on each other, and a field of identical single domes is the plane-wave-sine failure in dome form), with a talus apron at the foot. A second, rotated lattice carries boulder-scale outcrops, so the two grids share no lines.
- **Redrawn against locally generated references (2026-09-17), designed on the CPU and then checked in the game.** The references show what a monolith on its plain actually is, and the scene was not it. A monolith is a **loaf**: walls near vertical to a rounded crown, where the scene drew a whaleback leaning back all the way down. Its walls are hung with dense **black water streaks** from the crown's edge down, pale where the skin has **flaked**, with **hollows** at the foot; the scene had a mottle and a few stained gullies. And the ground is a **vivid red** sand with **separate grey-green tussocks** on it, where the scene had an olive mottle over brown. All four are drawn now, and the far haze no longer ends in the dome's teal (below).
  - **How it was checked without the GPU**, since the desktop was resetting under the image generator and the owner asked for no graphics load. `Outback.fx`'s height field and pixel shader were ported line by line to numpy, and drawn as a heightfield ray-march: heights sampled on the mesh's own 2.5-unit grid and bilinearly interpolated for the silhouette, the field read at the hit point for everything else, and screen derivatives taken from neighbouring pixels. The port's geometry was first matched against a GPU capture of the shipped scene. Its lighting is **not** the game's (the dome palettes are averages the runtime computes; approximations stood in), so it settled shapes, patterns and artefacts, and **no colour was final until the scene had been seen in the game**. Several of the traps below were found in that port and not by eye. Its shapes held up on the GPU exactly; the lighting is where it was wrong.
  - **What the game changed**, in the Testbed under domes 1, 3 and 13 (four pinned views, `nopost=1` so grain and aberration did not disguise the shader) and in the Game's front end and a level played in the scene. The shaded walls went near black under a low sun, which is what brought in **`SoilBounce`** (below). The streaks came out lighter than a shaded wall, not darker, which made `VarnishColor` a warm near-black. The glint still greyed them at 0.12. The hollows' band was a black skirt at 0.6. And the tussocks read as **beans**: a flat plateau shades only its rim, so a field of them came out as dark seeds under dome 13 and pale pills under dome 1.
  - **Cost, measured against main** (desktop GPU, Testbed, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 2 — target 3200×1800 — dome 13, `nocap`, 18 s runs, three alternating pairs per camera, every pair within 0.01 ms of the others): the play camera **2.45 → 2.62 ms (+0.16)**, across the plain to a near monolith **2.91 → 3.21 (+0.30)**, from above **3.07 → 3.30 (+0.23)**. Of the new work, the streaks' `GradientNoise3` sits behind a `[branch]` on `wallGate * rockMask`: both of its uses are multiplied by `wallGate` anyway, and measured against the same build without it, the branch saved **0.05 ms from the play camera, 0.08 across, 0.10 from above**, two pairs each. It is a data branch, not the occupancy-bound case this project's full-screen passes are; a wall is a small part of any frame. The rest is the loaf's `pow` and `sqrt` in every tap of the height field, the tussocks' frayed outline (one `GradientNoise2`) and the bounce. Everything the three normal taps do not read is dropped by the compiler once the function is inlined.
  - **The clearing gates the rock per FORMATION, not per pixel** — off the formation's own centre. Applied per pixel a radial ramp slices a monolith with a gradient and draws it half sunk; taken from the centre a formation is either standing whole or is not there. The plain's own swell is not ramped at all, so the trap the desert's `DuneSum` carries a trailing constant to avoid — a field with a mean times a radial ramp is a bowl with the island at the bottom — never arises: the one field with a big mean is the rock, and it is never partially raised.
  - **The margin bites, and it is honest about it.** A formation's margin inside its cell is its *full* reach — radius × apron × gully depth × elongation — so the largest, most elongated one is left a jitter box of `1 − 2 × 0.44`, i.e. **0.116 of a cell** (0.16 × 1.3 × 1.18 × 1.8 = 0.4419, against the 0.45 the clamp allows). It still reads as random because the visible irregularity is in **world** units: 0.116 of the 270-unit cell is **31 units of wander** for a rock some 80 units across. The empty cells (`RockChance`) are what break the lattice for the rest. (This read "a quarter of a cell … ninety units" against the old 370-unit cell until #171; the fraction was the loose half of that sentence, and it is the one the arithmetic above pins.)
- **The lattice was one skyline too sparse for the camera the game is played from, and the camera is not what #171 thought it was.** The issue was filed on the arithmetic that `GAME_FOV`'s half-angle (21.43°) against a view axis pitched ~25° puts the frustum's **lower edge above the horizon**, cropping the plain away. Measured instead of estimated, on `Pyramid_Small`: the axis is pitched **+15.52°**, not 25 — the 25 in `GameCameraFit`'s remarks is the angle to the hanging *cluster*, while the axis aims at the field's centre (`CameraTargetY` 1.89) — so the lower edge sits at **−5.91°**, *below* horizontal, and the horizon is in frame at 85 % of the frame's height. (A 20×20×20 field reads +17.41° and −4.02°, the same answer.) Nothing about the framing was cropping anything.
  - **The control settles what it is instead.** Through that identical frustum, sampled at eight bearings from the play camera's own geometry: the **desert** fills its band with dunes at every bearing and the **savanna** with acacias at many distances at every bearing, while the outback held **one** formation at seven bearings of eight and bare horizon either side of it. So it is this scene's own density, and a camera change would have moved all fifteen settings to fix one.
  - **Why it was that sparse.** The play frustum is 70° wide horizontally and the haze has taken the ground by about 350 units (`HorizonHazeDistance`'s quartic bites in the last fifth of 480), so a player's wedge held some two lattice cells of 370 — times `RockChance` 0.62, about one formation. **`RockSpacing` is 270 and `RockChance` 0.70 since #171**, which roughly doubles what stands in that wedge; every bearing of the eight now carries a formation and most carry a second at depth.
  - **The cell shrank rather than the formations growing, and that is forced.** A formation's radius is a fraction of its *own* cell (0.10–0.16), and its margin already stands at 0.4419 against the 0.45 clamp — raising the fraction to hold the world size would push every formation over its cell edge and break the single-cell read the whole design is built on. So the monoliths came in with the cell: 27–43 units of radius against an unchanged `RockHeight`, still twice as wide as they are tall, which reads as a steeper bornhardt and not as a spire. The wide free camera the scene was authored at is unharmed — it gains a second rank of formations at depth and keeps its red plain.
  - **What it does not do, and the report's other half.** At the gun's own *rest* bearing the before and after are nearly indistinguishable, because that wedge already held the near monolith; the gain is across bearings, which is what a player traversing actually walks through. And **the red plain is still a sliver**, which is geometry rather than density: the lens sits 0.6 above the island's deck (see "The city and the glass arena") and the plain is only about half a unit below it, so the ground is seen at grazing incidence — the rows between the island's rim and the horizon span 50 to 500 units, and the far end of that is what the haze owns. Nothing short of moving the camera changes that, and the camera is aimed at a hanging cluster.
  - **Measured cost**: two whole builds run alternately on the reference desktop, windowed 1920×1080 at ssaa 4 through the *wide* free camera — far more rock on screen than the play camera holds, so the harshest reading available — **+0.183 ms of 12.0**, slower in three pairs of three. `RockLayer` early-outs on a cell with no formation, so what this buys is paid for in pixels that now take the full rock path.
  - **Note the outback is not in any shipped level** (a level's own `scene` wins over the player's pick — `GameplayScreen.Session`), so today it is reached in the Game through the scene menu and the front end, and in the Testbed and the editor. That is worth knowing before weighing what a play-camera framing costs.
- **The gullies are geometry too, and that is not where they started.** They were a per-pixel field combed along the terrain's own downhill direction (`Fbm2Combed`) driving the normal through `PerturbNormalFromHeight` — and the rock drew in **horizontal terraces**, one band per row of grid triangles. Both of that path's inputs are screen derivatives, and a screen derivative is constant across a triangle and jumps at every facet edge; on the gentle terrain of every other scene here adjacent facets very nearly agree and nothing shows, on a flank falling forty-four units over eight cells they do not. So the ribs moved into the height field, where the terrain normal's own finite differences pick them up and no screen derivative is involved — and they buy the **silhouette** as well, which a normal never could. They are radial by construction (a gully runs downhill, and downhill on a dome is radial), sampled on the **unit circle** of the formation's own frame: a closed curve in the noise's domain, so it comes back round to itself with no seam and no pole, which is what a rib count taken off an `atan2` bearing cannot do (the failure the cavern's mineral veins shipped with once). The gully value is handed back **out of the height field** so the desert-varnish streaks cannot land anywhere but in the gullies they belong to.
  - **The terraces were not the ribs' fault, and finding that out is what the two-stage debugging cost.** They were the **heat shimmer**: its noise was keyed to `worldPosition.y`, which on a near-vertical flank is a set of horizontal stripes one world unit apart, strongest exactly where the haze fade is half done. The distortion belongs to the air the ray passes *through*, not to the surface behind it, so its domain is the **view ray's own direction** now — features of a fixed angular size, sliding upward against the clock, sticking to nothing. The rock's fine relief went back to `PerturbNormalFromHeight` afterwards and no terrace returned.
- **The near monolith was an airbrushed orange dome, and every field was present when it was.** From the play camera the closest formation fills a third of the frame, and the colour selector — `broad + gullies + surface field` — was summed at weights whose worst case ran well past 1, so the whole face pinned to `RockColorBright`: three procedural fields in the shader and not one of them reaching the screen. The weights are cut to land inside 0…1 for all but the extremes. The surface field itself folds **world height** into its domain, because a field of the XZ plane alone is constant down a vertical face and would draw the flank as one smeared vertical streak.
- **The spinifex is cellular, and a raw Voronoi is a honeycomb.** Hummocks of spiky grass space themselves out the way plants competing for the same water do, which is a cellular field by nature — `Voronoi2`'s low values are closed round cells with bare red sand between them, where an fBm mottle only gives patches with no edge. But its sites are one per cell jittered by 0.4, so the first build tiled the plain on a near-perfect hexagonal packing with the bare ground reading as a connected web: the plane-wave-sine failure in cellular form. Two things break it and both are needed — the domain is **warped** by a field running at about a third of the hummock spacing, which slides neighbouring sites off their lattice rows, and the same field jitters the **threshold**, so hummocks vary in size instead of tiling at one radius. One noise evaluation does both. The **rim** is what is band-limited, not the hummock: a cellular field's finest feature is the width of the band its threshold cuts (0.3 of a cell), so a fade keyed to the cell leaves the rim aliasing for the whole distance between, and a fade keyed to the rim takes the tussocks off the plain within a hundred units. Widening the threshold band by the pixel keeps a distant hummock as a soft blob of the right average brightness; the field itself only fades once the *cells* approach pixel size, and finishes before they reach it (the desert's grain rule).
  - **Separate tussocks, since 2026-09-17.** The field used to cut a soft band 0.3 of a cell wide and then **scale** the result by the cover and the patch mask, so a sparse stand drew faint tussocks rather than fewer of them. Now the cover and the patch mask set each tussock's **radius**: a thin stand has small tussocks and then none, and each one has a narrow edge, a lighter crown and a ring of shade on the sand round its foot. The ring is not a cast shadow, which would take a second Voronoi at an offset point, nine more hashes. The distance fade goes to the field's **average cover** (a disc's area in a unit cell), not to bare sand, so the far plain does not redden where the tussocks give out. Drawn large enough to be unmistakable, the first cut read as **polka dots**, the savanna's tuft-disc failure again, so they stay small and patchy.
  - **In the game they read as beans, and three things fixed that.** The relief was the colour mask itself, flat on top with a cliff at the rim, so the normal turned only in a thin ring. Each tussock became a flat pill with a dark crescent: seeds under dome 13, pills under dome 1. Now the relief is a **dome**, `(1 − x²)²`, round over the top and meeting the sand with no crease, and it is kept **low** (`SpinifexRelief` 0.06; at 0.35 each one had a bright side and a black one). The outline is **frayed** by a fine noise (one `GradientNoise2`, faded with the pixel), because an exact disc of spines is a pebble. And the colour is a **dry straw-green lighter than the sand**: the darker grey-green, lit by the red-heavy low sun, came out darker than the sand around it, and dark dots on red read as pits.
- **The outcrops carry no gullies at all, and their radius is held near their own height.** At a fifth of their 110-unit cell they came out twenty-five units across and five tall, and a disc at that aspect with gullies cut round it is not a rock but a **starfish** — five radiating spines lying flat on the sand, which is exactly what it drew. A boulder is a rounded thing (water never ran far enough down one to cut it), and at that size the ribs' world width falls under the grid cell anyway. Their talus apron is taken off the **unribbed** radius for the same reason: the apron is wide and only a tenth of the height, so on a small formation it is most of what can be seen, and scree fills a gully in rather than following it out.
- **The distance fade is in TWO stages, the forest's arrangement and for the forest's reason: one colour cannot do both jobs.** The mid-distance has to keep the scene's own red, or a dome with a teal horizon (13, the benchmark one) paints the far plain and the shadowed flank of every monolith green — aerial perspective behaving correctly and still the wrong picture. The last stretch has to arrive at the dome's exact `HorizonColor`, or the terrain's own edge shows as a seam against a sky it does not match — which is what a single fade to a dust colour drew, a red plain butting straight onto a green sky. So the dust builds up quadratically and a murk takes over on a quartic that only bites in the final fifth.
  - **The murk is no longer `HorizonColor`**, the desert's fix of the same day carried over (`HazeWarmth`, 0.85). Fading the last stretch to the uniform was meant to match the dome, and it does not: dome 13's `HorizonColor` is a teal that the dome never draws above the skyline, where its horizon is lilac-grey. So the far plain stood against the sky as a **teal band** that matched nothing. The murk is the horizon's *brightness* in the dust's own *hue* (`HazeTint`'s), leaned towards by `HazeWarmth`, so the plain now goes into its own red haze.
  - **The dust takes the sky's brightness in full and only half its hue**, which is `ForestScatterRenderer.ApplySkyTint` run the other way round. Lit by the sky's colour outright — `HazeTint * skyLight`, the obvious spelling — the product keeps whichever channel the dome happens to be strongest in, so a teal-horizoned dome handed back a green dust however hard `DustStrength` was pushed: a multiply cannot be out-weighted by a blend whose other end carries the same hue.
- **The grid is 400 over 1000** (a 2.5-unit cell) rather than the desert's 360 over 1000, because this terrain carries a real silhouette and not only a shaded surface — and since the loaf, that silhouette is as steep as the mesh allows. **`Bornhardt`** is a superellipse crown, `sqrt(1 − t^p)` (the squareness roll `p` running 2.2–5.0, dome to block), multiplied down to nothing over the last **0.30** of the radius. On a 27–43-unit radius that wall falls over 8–13 units, three to five cells. Narrower does not hold, and the second lobe proved it. Its wall was first cut to its own smaller radius, two cells, and the mesh's facets wandered back and forth across the crease where it leans on the main body. Everything the pixel shader reads off the height field there drew **horizontal ripples, one per row of the grid**. So the lobe's wall is widened by its own scale to the same world width. The **outcrops keep the whaleback**: their wall and squareness range (0.475, 1.5–5.5) are the least-squares fit to the profile they shipped with, within a tenth of their height at every radius and roll, because a 0.30 wall on a 5–9-unit boulder would be one cell wide. This is the desert's crest-sharpening rule at monolith scale. Over 255 a side, so the grid cache's 32-bit index buffer is load-bearing here (the mountain's lesson).
- **The desert varnish hangs down the whole wall, and it is the one glossy thing in the scene.** The near-black mineral skin water leaves where it has run repeatedly down one line is, on a monolith, dense streaks from the crown's edge down. It is a threshold of `GradientNoise3` over (bearing, height), gated on how steep the rock is and kept off the lowest reach of the wall, where the references show flaking and hollows. The gullies and the broad field lower the threshold, so the streaks crowd where water gathers.
  - **The streaks run on the formation's BEARING**, the direction the gullies are already sampled on, which `RockLayer` hands back. A field of world position cannot draw them, however tall it is stretched: down a wall that leans back, a point moves outward across the plain as well as down, and every streak broke into a column of blobs. The bearing does not change down a wall at all. The lobe's walls use the main body's bearing too; a second bearing for them looked no better, and switching between the two would have left a seam.
  - **A monolith's body always owns the fields it hands back.** A boulder can sit on a monolith's lower wall, where the monolith's coverage is under 1 and the boulder's centre is 1. With the larger coverage deciding, the streaks took the boulder's bearing there and fanned out of its centre as a starburst of hairlines.
  - **The gully field had the wrong sign in every shading term.** `rib` cuts the radius, so a *high* value pulls the edge in, which makes a gully. Measured round a flank, height and `rib` correlate at −0.68. The shipped varnish took `−rib` and stained the spurs, and the ambient occlusion lit the gullies. Both are turned round, and both are lighter than they were: at the old weights each gully drew a dirty smear down the wall.
  - **Band-limited off the view ray, not off the surface.** `fwidth` of the world position is constant across a facet and jumps at its edge; on a wall three cells tall that steps once per grid row, and a band limit keyed to it draws the rows. The ray through a pixel is the same whichever facet it lands on, so the streaks take `dist × fwidth(towardsEye)`. As a streak narrows towards a pixel, the field fades to its own **average coverage**, `1 − smoothstep(−0.4, 0.4, threshold)`, which matches the noise to within 0.02 across the thresholds the gullies reach. A far monolith keeps the darker face its streaks give it.
  - **The rock's surface field folds in the height field's height, not the mesh's.** The two differ between vertices by up to a few units on a wall this steep, differently on every facet, and folding in the interpolated Y drew chevrons down the walls.
  - **The glint came down from 0.5 to 0.04** (`VarnishGloss`). On a few gully lines it was a highlight. Across a whole wall it painted every streak on a sunlit face white, the opposite of a water stain, and 0.12 still drew them pale grey in the game. Its Blinn lobe stays gated on the varnish *and* the rock mask, because a glossy plain reads as wet ground, which is the one thing the outback is not.
  - **The varnish is a warm near-black, not a grey.** Under the lilac and blue domes, a neutral grey reflected more of the sky's blue than the red rock beside it, so the streaks on a shaded wall came out *lighter* than the wall.
  - **Flaking and hollows.** `FlakeColor`/`FlakeStrength` are pale, washed-out orange patches off the surface field the rock already pays for: on the walls only, mostly low, never under a streak. `CaveShade` darkens a band along a wall's foot whose top wanders with the broad field (no hollow where that field runs low) and is ragged with the surface field. Both are albedo only. `CaveShade` is 0.35, because at 0.6 the band was a black skirt round every shaded wall.
- **The walls take light bounced off the sand** (`SoilBounce`, 0.35), the desert's `SandBounce` weighted for a loaf. A near-vertical wall sees half the ground and half the sky. Under a low sun the sky half left a shaded wall close to black, streaks and all, where every reference shows it a deep red. The bounce is the sun's light off `SoilColorPale`, following the sun's height, strongest where the sun does not reach, and weighted by `1 − normal.y` so it comes from the ground. The flat plain faces the sky, takes none of it, and keeps exactly the colour it had.
- **Measured before the 2026-09-17 redraw** — the redraw's own cost is above, measured as a difference against main in the Testbed rather than repeated here (desktop GPU — Ryzen 9 5900X / Radeon RX 6900 XT — front end, windowed 1600×900, dome 13, `quality=high` i.e. ssaa 2, `nocap`, 18 s runs with the first four readings dropped): **outback 443.2 FPS / 2.26 ms**, against **desert 511.9 / 1.95** and **mountain 531.6 / 1.88** on the same machine in the same pass. So it is the dearest of the three terrain scenes here, at **+0.30 ms and +15 %** over the sibling it is closest to in construction — three noise octaves on the rock's surface field and the rock lattice read four times per pixel, against the desert's four sines. No reduced program: it is well inside the terrain band and nowhere near the occupancy-bound class the forest floor, the dream and the cavern are in. **No figure is claimed for the reference APU**, because these three do not keep their order across machines — on the APU the mountain is the *dearest* terrain scene (19.1 ms against the desert's 16.9), the reverse of what this machine reads — so extrapolating from here would be inventing a number.
- **It forces no dome**, like the mountains, the meadow and the desert — it keeps whatever is up, and the two-stage haze is what lets it keep its own colour under any of the twenty. Its ambience bed is a thin dry wind under the shrill of cicadas, which is what heat sounds like; giving it one also turned `ProceduralAmbience.BakeScene`'s `default:` arm into a deliberately near-silent neutral, so the *next* scene is audibly missing a bed rather than plausibly playing somewhere else's (that arm has now silently handed the wrong atmosphere to two scenes running — cave drips to the Moon, then vacuum to the outback).

## The tropical beach

The fourteenth `SceneKind` (#244, the owner's own playtest request): the island stands in the middle of a tropical island's lagoon. A ring of white-gold sand at the island's foot carries the palms and the mossy rocks; the sand slopes through a wiggling waterline into calm turquoise water; and all around, some three hundred units out, the island's own green shore ridge rises out of the lagoon and closes the horizon, with one channel through it where the open sea reaches the skyline. `Tropical.fx` + `TropicalSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Terrain, Water, Palms, Rocks, Birds — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=tropical` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like the forest and the other scenes that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reached it only by loading a level naming it until #380 put every scene on V.

**It is the first scene with terrain AND water in the one frame, and it takes the Moon's lesson: state the order, don't inherit it.** `DrawEnvironment`'s tropical case draws the land first — an ordinary depth-writing opaque draw — then the lagoon depth-READ over the bed the terrain just wrote, then the palms, the rocks and the flock. Two consequences worth knowing:

- **The lagoon is `Sea.fx` itself, unchanged.** `DrawTropicalWater` pushes the tropical config's water values into the very same effect and grid the open sea draws with — calmer swell, turquoise body colours — so the two scenes cannot drift apart in how water is *drawn*, only in what it is. The clip radius is the innermost the wiggling waterline ever reaches, less the shoulder of `Sea.fx`'s `CALM_BAND`, so the swell dies approaching the shore and the surf laps onto the sand rather than breaking against a circle; `FunnelPoolRadius` is 0 (the drain's standing pool is the sea scene's own arrangement). **Each water draw has its own clone of the effect since #580** (`_seaEffect`, `_lagoonEffect`, both `Effect.Clone()` of the one loaded `Sea.fx`, disposed by the renderer), its config-static water pushed once at load. Until then the two scenes shared one effect instance, and both draws pushed their whole uniform set every frame, config-static values included: a NumPad2/V switch applies no config, so pushing only the per-frame half would have left whichever scene was switched to drawing the other's water — the shared flock's sizing trap, in shader-parameter form. A clone shares the compiled shaders and copies the parameter values, so the per-frame half is all either draw pushes now.
- **The actual waterline is the depth test's, not the clip's.** Between the clip radius and the coast the water plane extends under sand that is still above it, and the opaque depth-writing sand rejects it from every vantage — so the waterline the eye sees is exactly where the beach profile crosses the water level, wiggle included, with no shader doing anything about it. The clip only keeps the calm band anchored and stops the two surfaces grazing at the line itself.

**The land is one radial profile keyed on the distance to the WATERLINE, not the island** — flat dry sand at the island's foot, a hermite down through the water level exactly at the coast radius, the lagoon bed under opaque water, then the far shore's own rise. Two hermites meet at the coast so the profile crosses the water level *by construction* — the waterline and the coast are the same line and cannot drift apart, which is what lets the wet-sand band, the far shore's sand fringe and the lagoon's clip all read one figure. The waterline is a mean radius wobbled by sine octaves of **bearing** (integer multipliers, continuous round the circle), so the coast breaks into bays and headlands; the far shore wobbles on its own octaves so the two coasts share no lines, and its ridge carries **one channel** carved by a cosine bump of the bearing — a closed ring reads as a crater lake, and the channel is what says this lagoon belongs to an island in an ocean.

- **The height field is sines and hermite ramps ONLY, no gradient noise** — because `TerrainMirror.Tropical` mirrors it on the CPU to plant the palms and the rocks on the ground the shader draws, the contract the savanna's mirror has held since the acacias. Everything noisy (sand patches, the shell grain, the canopy mottle, the wind bands) lives in the pixel shader, where no plant ever asks for it. The far ridge's hills are domain-warped sines — the forest's trick, and warp-by-a-sine is still a sine, so the mirror covers them too.
- **The surface answers the two coasts differently.** The near beach is sand all the way down to its waterline, with a **wet band** darkening the last unit above the water (keyed on height above water, so it follows the wiggle exactly) and a fine wind-combed ripple. The far shore is jungle: the same ridge ramp that raises it gates a **green mask**, over a sand **fringe** just above the far waterline (every coast keeps a band of bare sand — it is what a shore is), with two band-limited mottle scales on the green (the forest floor's lesson: one scale reads as camouflage), travelling wind bands through it (the savanna's grass trick, mild at this distance) and a canopy relief tilting the normal. One relief field, lerped by the green mask, through one `PerturbNormalFromHeight` — the outback's single-perturbation rule.
- **The haze is the outback's two-stage arrangement** — mid-distance keeps the scene's own colour under any dome, the last stretch arrives at the dome's exact `HorizonColor` — with a weaker, near-neutral tint: clear marine air, not dust.

**The palms are real 3D geometry on the acacia's path (#202), and they sway.** `PalmMesh` is two meshes over one instance — the solids (a slim trunk bowed on a rolled curve — a swept tube, not a lathe: a lathe is strictly about the Y axis — ending in a boot of old frond bases with a bunch of coconuts under it) and the leaves: eight to eleven live fronds radiating from the trunk's tip, each a midrib carrying two rows of leaflets, over the skirt of dead fronds hanging under the crown (a live crown over a bare trunk reads as a lollipop; the skirt is half of what says "palm"). The leaves are **single-sided and drawn with `CullNone`**, `Palm.fx` turning each pixel's normal to the face the camera sees — see "Real palms, not paper (#557)" below for why they stopped being the double-sided strips they were until then. Four variants at rolled proportions and structural seeds; the variety is in the mesh and never in a per-instance stretch, for the shader's inverse-transpose reason. `Palm.fx` is `Acacia.fx` plus **the sway**: each vertex carries a weight in its UV.x — zero along the trunk, rising along each frond — so the wind moves the crown and never the trunk (a palm whose whole body waves reads as a kelp), phased off the instance's own world position so a grove never beats in unison, off the wall clock like the clouds and the sea.

- **⚠ That weight lives in `TEXCOORD0`, an ordinary texture coordinate, and it is only a sway weight because `PalmMesh` chooses to bake one there — so `SwayStrength` is a per-DRAW argument and must never be a per-frame one.** `SwayStrength` was set once in `ApplyPalmFrame`, which `DrawTropicalRocks` also calls, so the palms' own strength reached the waterline boulders. Both a `RockMesh` and its moss cap **are** `LatheMesh`es (`RockMesh` hands out `_lathe`'s own buffers), and `LatheMesh` writes `s / segments` there — 0 to 1 **around the circumference**. So one side of every ring was displaced at full frond-tip weight while the other side stood still: the stones did not merely drift downwind, they **sheared open**, and they did it off the wall clock so it read as motion. Reported by the owner in as many words — "the rocks on the beach move with the wind, which is nonsense". `DrawPalmPart` takes `swayStrength` beside `dappleStrength` now, so no mesh can inherit a sway nobody meant it to have, and both the shader and the draw carry the warning. **Verified as a paired diff of two frames at different wall-clock times from one fixed camera**: over two boxes holding rocks, *not one pixel* changed by more than 24 (mean |d| 0.00 and 0.07), while the palm crowns in the same pair moved 18.7 % of their pixels past that threshold at a max of 198 — which is what says the test can see geometry that moves, rather than that nothing in the frame does. **The palms are planted only on dry sand** — a height test against the water level, which follows the wiggling waterline — clumped into groves with `ScatterSpacing`, sunk a fraction into the sand (the forest's own figure; planted at the exact surface a flared root reads as standing on a pinhead). **And since #408 the ring starts at 52 rather than 36, for the front end's sake**: the menu camera's wide leg stands 34 to 45 units from the axis depending on the window's shape (see "The menu camera" in `docs/game-shell.md`), at a height a tall palm's crown reaches, so a ring beginning at 36 planted palms *inside* that orbit and the lens flew through their crowns whenever the scene page chose this scene; the owner's "the same thing in some scene previews" (#408). **Since #555 the root's ring is not what keeps the orbit clear — the crown is tested itself** (`PalmConfig.OrbitClearance`, 46: the widest orbit plus a unit of air). At the heights the palms stand at now, the trunk's bow and the lean carry a crown several units off its root, so a spot is only a candidate if the crown's own world position less the furthest a frond reaches (`PalmMesh.Crown`, `PalmMesh.FrondReach`) stays outside it. The forest's and the savanna's rings (44 and 42) clear the orbit at every aspect because their trees top out below the lens.

**The rocks with green tops are two meshes over one instance, not a shader trick.** Each waterline rock is a `RockMesh` (the forest's own boulder) plus a moss cap — a low lathe dome whose rim is buried in the stone's upper flank, on its own `irregularityPhase`, so where the green emerges over the grey is the two wobbles disagreeing and no two rocks share the line. The cap is drawn at 0.84 of the stone's radius with its rim well under the stone's surface *at that radius* — the stone is a dome, and a cap as wide as the stone itself would float over its flank. The rocks are planted in the **band straddling the waterline** (a height test again), so some stand half in the water — which is what the issue asked for and what a shore looks like — with a tumble a washed boulder has earned, on their own occupancy list (a boulder at a palm's foot is fine; the forest's split).

**It is an `IsSolidTerrainScene` — the first member of that family with water in it.** Under the island there is sand: the terrain cuts the island's footprint as every solid-terrain scene does, the dark pit shaft backs the drain's glass, and there is no dive for the drop cinematic (`OpenBelow` stays the complement). The lagoon's water starts past the beach, well outside the hole, so nothing about the shaft's reasoning changes. It takes the dome like every terrain scene, and **it forces the brightest one**: entering the tropical scene defaults to dome 1 (clear blue over a warm horizon) by the sea's and savanna's own mechanism, overridable as always — white sand and turquoise water are the postcard, and only read as one under a sunny sky. The flock circles the lagoon on the scene's own `BirdsConfig` (the shared state is sized from all four scenes that draw it now). Its ambience bed is the sea's wash at a calm remove — smaller swells, the foam hiss still a quarter turn behind — under the lightest warm breeze of the set.

- **Measured** (desktop GPU — Ryzen 9 5900X / Radeon RX 6900 XT — the Game's front end, windowed 1600×900, dome 13 pinned, `nocap`, ssaa 2, 14 s runs with the first four readings discarded): **tropical 501.4 FPS / 2.0 ms**, against **sea 598.4 / 1.7** on the same pass — i.e. desert-class (the desert measured 511.9 / 1.95 on this machine for #112) and inside the terrain-scene band, which is what a gentle-sloped grid plus the sea's own water plus ~110 palms and ~30 rocks in instanced draws should cost. No reduced program, for the same reason as the outback's: nowhere near the occupancy-bound class. No APU figure is claimed (the outback's caveat — the terrain scenes do not keep their order across machines).
- **Verified in captures, not by eye at the desk**: the play vantage shows the sand ring, 15–20 palms reading as palms and the turquoise water starting beyond the beach with no artifacts; a shore vantage along the waterline shows the mossy rocks strung through the surf line and the wet band on the sand; a high vantage outward shows the beach falling into the lagoon and the green ridge closing the horizon. The one fault the captures caught — palms planted at the exact surface reading as floating — was fixed by the forest's sink before anything was committed.


**#268 rebuilt how it READS, after looking at it from the play camera rather than from the vantages it was built against.** Four findings, three fixed, one disproved — and the disproved one is the most useful thing on this page.

- **The far ridge showed the lagoon its sandy flank.** The vegetation mask was `ring * fringe`, where `ring` is the ridge's own *rise* — the same 95-unit `RingWidth` ramp that lifts it in the height field — so greenness grew **outward**: zero at the ridge's inner edge and full only most of a hundred units further out, i.e. over the crest and beyond. The near flank, which is the whole of what the lagoon and the play camera look at, was sand by construction. Measured across the ridge band before the fix: **(204, 199, 168)**, red over green, with no green in it anywhere. It is two questions now, answered separately: a **short radial gate** says which landmass this is, and the ridge's own **height above the water** says how vegetated it is (`SHORE_SAND_FRINGE` 1.1, `JUNGLE_RISE` 4.0 — form constants, the meadow's rosette rule). The ridge stands 15–28 units out of the water, so all but its own beach comes up green.
- **And the haze was eating what the mask then delivered.** The final stage was `haze⁴` to the dome's exact horizon colour — the outback's arrangement, borrowed with its exponent. That is right for the outback, whose far field is empty plain; here the horizon **is** a feature, and the ridge stands at 300–420 units against a haze distance pinned at 480 by the grid's own half-extent, so at the fourth power its crest was already 48 % of the way to the sky and the jungle came out beige anyway. It is `haze⁸` in this scene, which still reaches 1 at the grid's edge — the seam it exists to hide is still hidden — and stops eating the ridge.
- **The lagoon was grey, and `WaterShallow` was never the fault.** `Sea.fx` gives its pale colour only to the up-facing faces of the swell, caps that at half and biases a calm patch darker still — right for water with nothing under it, wrong for a lagoon, so the basin was drawn mostly in `WaterDeep`, a dark navy. Measured across the water band: **(140, 146, 133)**, blue *below* red, against a turquoise sitting in the config the whole time. `Sea.fx` gained one uniform, **`ShallowBias`**, lifting the floor of that mix; the lagoon runs it at 0.78 and the open sea at 0, where `lerp(x, 1, 0)` is bit-exactly `x`. The lagoon's own body colours went up with it (a white-sand basin is far brighter than open water: `WaterDeep` 0.012→0.030 in green, `WaterShallow` 0.24→0.52), which is what actually delivers the turquoise — the bias alone moved it barely at all, because at these viewing angles the Fresnel sky reflection is most of what you see and only a much brighter body can compete with it.
  - **The open sea is unchanged, and that was checked rather than asserted** — a shared shader deserves it. A per-pixel diff of two runs is worthless here (the swell and the cloud deck both run off the wall clock, so two launches catch different phases: 45 % of pixels differ between two builds that are the same). The regional means over 62 400 water pixels are the honest instrument: **R 69.55 / G 88.77 / B 123.29 before against R 69.30 / G 88.49 / B 123.08 after**, i.e. a third of a level out of seventy — wave phase, not a shift.
- **The sand's ripple existed only as a tilt of the normal**, where `Desert.fx` learned to darken its own troughs as well — "with the normal tilt alone a dune's shadowed flank goes back to being a flat patch of colour", which on flat beach sand under a high sun is the whole story. It shades now (`RIPPLE_SHADE`). And that alone was not enough: the ripple's domain scale of 0.85 made a feature about **1.2 world units** across against the desert's ~3.9, so `Fbm2BandLimited`'s own contract faded it out at a third of the distance and the beach was smooth everywhere the eye looks. Coarsened to 0.28 at four octaves. Measured, sand-pixel luminance sd from the same vantage under the same dome: **4.42 original → 4.70 shaded → 4.92 coarsened**, against the desert's **5.98**.
- **The lagoon cannot be put into the play frame by moving the waterline, and #268 established that the expensive way — by trying it.** The island's stone cap stands 5 units over the beach and the play camera looks out *across* that cap; its far rim cuts the sight line and everything at the sand's own level behind it is gone. Proved with `arena=none`: the identical play-camera frame shows the lagoon plainly with the island hidden and none of it with the island drawn. Then `ShoreRadius` was brought 100 → 66 with the palm ring following it — **still no water**, because a nearer waterline is a nearer thing to hide — and the test was repeated on a small map in case the stand-off was the variable. Also none. All of it reverted. **What follows is worth keeping: from the play camera the only things visible past the island are things that STAND UP** — the palms, the far ridge, the sky. A flat beach and a flat lagoon are equally invisible whatever their radii, so the lagoon belongs to the raised vantages (the front end's orbit, the drop cinematic, the editor) and the far ridge is what the play camera actually gets. Which is why the ridge got the work.
- **Measured** (desktop — Ryzen 9 5900X / Radeon RX 6900 XT — Testbed, fixed camera, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 4, dome 1, `fpscap=150`, 16 s runs, first four readings discarded, **paired** on the one pin): **119.8 FPS / 8.3 ms before against 117.2 / 8.5 after**, i.e. about **0.2 ms** for one more fBm octave and a handful of ALU on a full-frame terrain. It stays desert-class on the same pin (the desert reads 118.2 / 8.5, the sea 141.9 / 7.0). No APU figure is claimed, the outback's caveat holding.

### Detail and variety, from references (#445)

The owner, from play: **the beach needs an upgrade, designed from generative-AI references**. 18 rendered locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\445`, not in this repository): the beach from an elevated three-quarter view, a waterline close up, a palm-variety concept sheet, a lagoon and reef from the air, a beach-prop sheet and the far shore — three seeds each. The issue's own summary of what was here was exact — *"a beach ring, one tree species in four variants, a rock string and a lagoon; nothing else grows, nothing lies on the sand"* — and the references say the same thing four ways.

- **⚠ The palms had four crown variants, a 0.62–1.47 size span and still read as a plantation, because the silhouette a palm is recognised by is its LEAN and they had none.** The instance took `0.05f * rng` radians in a **random** direction: at most 2.9°, and as likely inland as out. Every reference leans, most of them 20–45°, and they lean *seaward*, because that is where the light is over the water and a coconut palm grows towards it. The lean is now `0.08 + 0.55 · r₁ · r₂` (a product of two rolls, so most palms are moderate and a few lie right over) about an axis biased to the outward bearing, scattered ±40°. The old comment's caution is what the bias is for and it still stands: *a leaning palm reads as wind-shaped, a tilted one as felled*.
  - Tilting +Y towards the outward unit (uₓ, u_z) means rotating about **(u_z, 0, −uₓ)**: for a right-handed rotation about A the velocity of Y is A × Y, which for a horizontal A is (−A_z, 0, Aₓ). Worth writing down because it is exactly the kind of thing that ships backwards — the first capture was taken to check the palms lean *out* and not *in*.
  - ⚠ **And from #445 until #555 it did not happen, because the yaw came after the lean (#555).** The instance matrix was `Scale · Lean · RotationY(yaw) · Translation`, and row-vector matrices apply left to right: the palm was leaned seaward and then the *leaning* palm was spun about the world's Y axis by a random angle, which threw the bias away — palms leaned in over the arena as often as out over the water, plainly visible from an elevated camera. `SceneRenderer.PalmWorld` yaws first now, so the turn only spins the mesh's own bow about the trunk and the lean is the one stated above.
- **Three kinds of dressing, and not one new mesh.** The savanna's scrub foliage, its grass tuft and its fallen log are a beach's bushes, sea grass and driftwood at another size and colour, which is what a game-agnostic mesh library is for. `TropicalDressingConfig` plants 250 bushes and 520 tufts clumped around the palms' **own** cluster centres (so the green gathers where the shade is), on dry sand only, and 26 pieces of driftwood in the band the sea throws a log onto — lying **along** the waterline more often than across it, since the yaw is the tangent scattered by ±50°.
  - ⚠ They are scattered **after** the palms and the rocks, and that ordering is load-bearing: everything here draws from one rng stream, so anything inserted earlier would re-roll the whole beach behind it. The aurora's snags record the same trap.
  - ⚠ **None of it sways.** `Palm.fx` reads `TEXCOORD0.x` as its sway weight, which `PalmMesh` bakes as a deliberate trunk-to-tip ramp; every other mesh in the library puts something else there, and at the palms' strength the rocks sheared open. A still tuft beside a swaying palm is a smaller fault than a tuft that tears itself apart.
  - ⚠ **Scrub size is the dial that decides whether it reads as a thicket or as green boulders.** A `FoliageMesh` is a smooth lobed dome with no detail inside its own silhouette, so at 1.7 units a few big ones photographed as beanbags dropped on the sand. The count carries the coverage and the size carries the reading: 1.05 and 250 of them. Their height went the other way for the same reason — at the savanna's proportions (a little over half the radius) a beach bush seen from the elevated three-quarter view this scene is framed in is a flat green *puddle*, so it is near its own width in height.
- **The foam line, which every waterline reference has and this beach had none of.** It is on the **land** and not on the water: `Sea.fx` draws the lagoon and its whitecaps, and a lagoon this calm makes none at its own edge, so the swash's residue has to be sand shading. Keyed on height above the water like the wet band it sits in, so it follows both coasts' wiggling waterlines exactly and needs no new field; centred a little *above* the water, because a line centred on the waterline itself is half under the lagoon and reads as a ring drawn round the island. It is **lace and not a stripe** — one octave of gradient noise creeping slowly along the shore, so the line is scalloped and breathes without any surf being simulated.
- **What the references show that was NOT built**, recorded so it is not re-derived: the **reef** — pale sandbars and dark coral patches reading through the lagoon's turquoise, which wants a bed texture the water shader would have to sample; **coconuts**, a **hut** and an **outrigger** on the far shore, which the prop sheet draws well and which are each a mesh of their own; and the far shore is still a ridge with a colour rather than a place.
- **Measured against `main`** (desktop, Testbed, fixed cameras, 3840×1600 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, alternating builds, medians of 12): at the play pose **8.76 / 8.76 ms before against 9.02 / 9.02 after**, +0.26; from the elevated three-quarter view with the whole island in frame **9.42 / 9.42 against 9.74**, +0.32. The spreads are 0.05 ms or less on seven of the eight runs, and the shader sets differ (`1111f0cc` against `783ec82c`) because the foam line is a real edit to `Tropical.fx`. ⚠ **The play camera pays that cost while seeing almost none of it** — the island's coping hides the near sand and the dressing starts at 40 units, so what this pass adds is seen from the front end's orbit and the scene picker; the foam line is a terrain-shader term paid over every sand pixel either way, and the dressing's instances are submitted whether or not the frustum keeps them.

### Tall palms (#555)

The owner: *"the palms on the beach scene are too small / too low."* They were 12 units to the crown against an island 26 in radius standing 5 over the sand, so from the play camera they topped out around the island's own rim and read as shrubbery on the horizon; a real coconut palm is 15–25 m against an island tens of metres across.

- **`Height` 12 → 22**, so the typical crown stands some 16 units over the island's cap and into the sky behind the cluster, and the tallest roll (1.18 per variant × 1.25 per plant) near 32. **The trunk and the crown were raised much less than the height**, which is the #445 references' proportion: a tall coconut palm is a slender pole a few percent of its height across under a head about half its height wide, not a parasol as wide as the palm is tall, which is what 5.2 on 12 was. `TrunkRadius` 0.34 → 0.38 (0.46 was tried first and photographed as a colonnade), `FrondLength` 5.2 → 7.2, and `SwayStrength` 0.45 → 0.62 with the fronds, since `Palm.fx` adds it in world units at the tip.
- **The trunk's flare is a bole at the foot now, not a taper over the whole trunk.** `RootRadius` ran a straight 1.55× to 0.85× from root to crown, which at 12 units read as a sturdy young palm and at 22 made every trunk a cone — still 1.2× at half height. It is `1 − 0.12t + 0.55(1 − t)⁶` now: the same 1.55× at the root, settled within the first quarter, then a near-even pole to 0.88× at the crown.
- **The crown is what is tested against the front end's orbit** (above), and **the chapter tour's lagoon stand rides at 20° instead of 8°** (the viewpoint table): at 8° and 2.1 stand-offs out the lens stood at crown height in the middle of the palm ring. Photographed through `tour` frame by frame: the stand now looks over the palm tops out to the lagoon and the far shore, and no frame of the flight passes through a crown.
- **The shadows came along for free** — the palms already cast into the sun's map (#471), and longer trunks throw longer shadows over the sand; from the elevated camera none reaches the island. Everything planted after the palms off the one rng stream (the rocks, the dressing) was re-rolled by the new placement loop, which rolls each palm's variant, yaw and lean *before* it places it.

### Real palms, not paper (#557)

The owner, on the tall palms of #555: *"they don't look like real palms, paper or plastic models at best."* The references drawn locally for it (the `design-references` skill, `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\557`: a grove in afternoon light and a back-lit crown from below, several seeds each) against the shipped palm named the faults plainly: every reference frond is a midrib with a fringe of hanging leaflets the sky shows through, the crown is dark inside and glows yellow-green only where the sun comes through a leaf, and the trunk is a light grey ringed pole. The shipped frond was one flat tapered strip, lit the same from both faces and never in its own shade; the trunk a dark smooth brown tube.

- **The frond is geometry now, not a card.** `PalmMesh.AddFrond` lays a narrow pale midrib and 40 pairs of leaflets along it (18 on a dead frond): angled 31–43° forward off square, folded down out of the frond's plane in a V (deeper towards the tip, nearly shut on the dead skirt), each bent down along its length in two segments, longest a little inside mid-frond, with 7 % torn off and the first seventh of the stalk bare — the crown's spokes, which is what the back-lit references show most plainly. Each leaflet's two edge normals lean apart, a crease the light breaks across. The leaflet reach is clamped so no tip passes the frond's own end, which keeps `FrondReach` — and with it #555's orbit test and every palm's placement — unchanged; the captures from above are the same arrangement before and after.
- **⚠ The strips were double-sided with the TOP face's normal on both faces**, so a frond's underside lit exactly as its top did. The leaves are single-sided now, drawn with `CullNone` (the frond draw alone; the solids after it go back to the shared culling), and `Palm.fx` turns the normal to the face the camera sees with `SV_IsFrontFace`. That is half the triangles of doubling them, and the transmission below needs to know which face it is looking at anyway.
- **The dead skirt moved from the wood draw to the leaf draw** (it is leaves), and the wood draw gained the **boot** — a short swollen spindle of old frond bases over the trunk's tip, without which the fronds sprout from the end of a pole — and **six to ten coconuts** hanging round it.
- **⚠ The trunk had cracks up its whole length.** It was a chain of separate tube segments, each building its ring frame off its own axis, so no two segments shared a ring and every joint was a slit the sky showed through: a column of bright cyan ticks up every trunk in the before captures. It is one swept tube now on the bow's own plane (the bow is planar, so one binormal serves the whole trunk), 16 rings of 10 sides, each ring's axis off the path's direction at its station. The eight radius bulges that were the ring scars are gone from the mesh: nobody could see eight on a 22-unit trunk.
- **`Palm.fx` has a palm material behind a per-draw `PalmShading`**, for the sway strength's reason: the palms' two draws set 1, the rocks and the dressing keep 0 and the plain shading, since their `TEXCOORD0` is a real texture coordinate. The palm mesh's `TEXCOORD0.y` says which part a vertex is (`PalmMesh.Part`: trunk, live frond by age, dead frond, coconut, boot) and where on it. Leaves: the sun through the far face (`LEAF_TRANSMISSION` 0.28, tinted yellower, up to 3.5× looking straight into the sun), occlusion rising along each frond from the crown and the sun half-blocked near its heart (the shadow map is too coarse to say that at a crown's scale), a darker underside that sees the crown rather than the horizon, a small waxy sheen, the oldest fronds going ochre, bleached tips, a pale midrib and a grey-brown dead skirt. Trunk: matte grey-brown bark (`TrunkColor` 0.10/0.078/0.052 → 0.25/0.215/0.17, the references' light grey), 120 leaf scars that wander a little round the trunk and **fade to their mean once a pixel spans one** (`fwidth` of the scar coordinate, taken before the per-part branch, since a gradient under a divergent branch is undefined), fibre streaks faded the same way, a darker browner top, and occlusion at the foot and in the crown's shade. The new colours are `PalmConfig.AgedFrondColor`, `DeadFrondColor` and `CoconutColor`; `FrondColor` went a little greener (0.075/0.155/0.045 → 0.06/0.165/0.03), because the yellow now comes from the light through the leaves.
- **Measured** on the desktop (RX 6900 XT; Testbed `scene=tropical sceneseed=0 sky=1 nopost nocap`, 1600×900, paired before/after runs, no other BS3D or Testbed running): the play camera at ssaa 2 **2.64 → 2.97 ms** (+0.33, four pairs, each run's own spread 0.01 ms); the low grove camera at ssaa 2 **2.22 → 2.68** (+0.46, three pairs); the **Low tier's program** (`detail=0 ssaa=1`) at the play camera **1.70 → 1.69**, no difference over three pairs (±0.05). So there is no reduced path: at Low the added triangles and the material cost nothing measurable here, and at the authored look the cost is under half a millisecond at 3200×1800. **The laptop is not measured.** If its Medium or Low frame misses budget on the beach, a frond mesh with fewer leaflet pairs (`LIVE_PAIRS` as a `PalmMesh` parameter) is the obvious reduced path.
- **Left as it is:** the sun's shadow map is visibly blocky where the leaflets' shadows fall on the sand near a low camera. The leaflets give the map detail it cannot hold at 260 units over 4096 texels; that is the map's resolution and not the palms', and the strips showed it too, less often.

## The volcano

The fifteenth `SceneKind` (#223): the island stands on the flank of an **erupting volcano**. A cone of black basalt rises a couple of hundred units off to one side with its crater against the sky, gullies raked down its flank and thin incandescent rivulets threading down it from the rim; rivers of lava — dark crust over an open glowing core — run in those gullies and out onto the plain, one of them passing the arena; a lava lake churns in the crater; the field around the island is glassy black lava with glowing cracks wandering through it; fountains spray in streaks on real ballistic arcs out of the crater and two side vents, an ash column lit orange from below opens into a head the wind takes, the cloud deck over the crater glows, and ash drifts through the whole frame. `Volcano.fx` + `LavaFountain.fx` + `Ash.fx` with `VolcanoSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Fountains, Eruption, Ash — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=volcano` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like every scene that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reached it only by loading a level naming it until #380 put every scene on V. **The campaign's eighth chapter, The Eruption, plays all ten of its levels here** (Breach to Paroxysm, the lava ball style, dome 9).

**It is the first scene whose ground is the light, and that is the whole reason it exists.** Every other backdrop takes its light from the sky and the strongest lamp in the game is the savanna's campfire; here the crater and the flows are real `SceneLights` point lights throwing red-orange **up** at the underside of the cluster, the island and the gun, while the dome goes on lighting their tops. Slot 0 is the crater (it takes the eruption envelope on top of its base glow); every other slot is a **flow front travelling downhill**, swelling in and dying out over its run so it arrives, passes and is gone — the first moving lamps in the game, and the reason `VolcanoLightPosition` takes a time at all. Slots 1 and 2 both ride the river aimed past the arena, a third of a span apart, so there is nearly always a front near the play field.

- **The readability constraint is a dial, not a taste.** Red and orange balls hang over red-orange lava. `VolcanoSceneConfig.LightStrength` (0.22) is what the ground is allowed to contribute, and it is set against the campfire — the only other lamp of this kind — which is (2.4, 1.0, 0.32) at range 32 where this is `LavaHot` × 0.22 at range 130. The first pass ran 0.55 at range 190 and turned the island's stone gold. Verified in capture with `Thirteen_Colors.json` hanging over the cone from the play camera: all thirteen read, the reds included.
- **The rivers run where the ground drains.** The gullies are a bearing term in the height field, and `SceneRenderer.SnapToGully` solves that same term for its floors and lays each river in one — four passes of a contraction, since the shape's `2·sin(3b)` bend is small against 22 gullies. Laid on arbitrary bearings the flows crossed the channels obliquely and read as paint on a cone; in the channels the ground dips where the lava is and the flank reads as drainage. River 0 is aimed past the arena and then checked: if the snap walks it inside the island plus two river widths, it takes the next gully out.
- **⚠ The CPU mirror of the height field carried its own gully band from the day the scene was built** (`VolcanoGroundY`, now `TerrainMirror.Volcano`: 1.15/0.45/1.05/0.62 of the radii against the shader's 1.3/0.30/1.15/0.85), so on the lower flank, where the flow fronts' lamps run, it put the ground up to seven units above the channel the shader draws, and at a side vent's radius three and a half. Found reading the two side by side in #509 and set to the shader's figures; nothing had ever compared them, because a lamp 2.5 units over a surface and one 9 units over it look alike from the arena.

- **The summit shipped as a sharp point, and the crater term could never have fixed that on its own.** `VolcanoMassing`'s flank is `ConeHeight · pow(1 − r/ConeRadius, ConeProfile)` evaluated at the pixel's own radius `r`, which is maximal exactly at `r = 0` for any profile — its slope there is `−ConeProfile / ConeRadius`, never zero. Subtracting a crater term that is *also* maximal at `r = 0` cannot move the true summit off that point; it can only steepen the final approach to it, which is what a "crater" reading as a nozzle tip was — caught by looking at a capture, not by the maths. The fix clamps the radius the flank itself is evaluated at to `CraterRadius`, so the flank plateaus at its own rim height for every point inside it, and the crater is cut into *that* plateau (`smoothstep(CraterRadius, 0, r)`, a bowl full-depth at the vent and zero at the rim) rather than into a cone that kept climbing underneath it. Outside `CraterRadius` nothing changes — same flank, same rivers, same gullies — because the clamp is a no-op there. Mirrored in `TerrainMirror.Volcano` as the file's own comment requires (vent and light placement read this, not the shader).
- **Three figures were wrong on the first pass and each was wrong for a stated reason.** The **haze** was the desert's 420 to the dome's own horizon colour, which under a cream horizon painted the entire cone the colour of sand — the volcano vanished and a dune stood in its place; it is 900 to `HorizonColor × HazeTint` now, an ash pall rather than the lit sky. The **halo** (how far a flow visibly heats the ground beside it) was six river-widths, and with the arena standing beside the river that passes it the halo covered the whole foreground and the entire plain glowed; it is three. And `LavaHot` was (7.5, 2.4, 0.3), which ACES takes straight to white-yellow — past a point, adding radiance to a saturated colour only desaturates it, and a white river is a lit crack rather than molten rock.
- **The eruption is an event on a schedule, and the schedule is a pure function of the wall clock.** `SceneRenderer.VolcanoEruption(time)` puts one burst in each period, hashing *where in the period* it starts and *how big it is* off the period's own index so it never becomes a metronome, with a fast attack and a long decay. One figure drives the jets' reach, the plume's density and the crater's light together, so a burst reads as one event — and because it holds no state, the game, the Testbed and the map editor all see the same eruption at the same second. **Light first, sound a beat behind**: the eruption *sound* was deliberately deferred here so it would land with #219's thunder and share one mechanism rather than be invented twice, and since 2026-09-05 it has — `SceneEventSounds` rides this very function's schedule and speaks `ProceduralAudio.PlayEruption` from the crater 0.78 s later, which is 266 units over 343 a second. See "The thunder is BUILT" in the storm's section for the whole of it.
- **The fountains are ballistic, in the vertex shader.** One static billboard buffer, its first slice drawn as the plume and the rest as the jets — two index ranges over the one buffer and two techniques, so neither pass touches the other's particles and neither carries a runtime branch. Each blob picks a vent, a lifetime, a launch azimuth and a tilt (sqrt-distributed, so the cone fills evenly rather than crowding its axis), and flies `p = vent + dir·v·t − ½g·t² + wind·drag·t²`; it is thrown white-hot and its colour *is* its age, which is what makes the top of a jet darker than its root with no extra particles. Life is set so most blobs land back near their vent, and the terrain's depth hides anything that goes below the surface.
- **`BlendState.AlphaBlend` in MonoGame is PREMULTIPLIED** — `(One, InverseSourceAlpha)` — so a shader returning a straight colour has that colour *added* at full strength on every layer. The smoke column was written non-premultiplied and 900 puffs of 0.085 grey came out as a **white steam plume brighter than the sky behind it**, which sent this looking for a bug in the colour being pushed. The plume and the ash both premultiply now. (`Snow.fx` and `Spray.fx` do not, and were left alone: they were tuned by eye against that behaviour, and #208 holds the snow.)
- **Its dome was chosen by looking** (#207's lesson), against 16 and 13: **dome 9**, a dim mauve-and-slate dusk with no bright band anywhere in it. Dome 16 is darker at the zenith but its horizon is a bright cream and it stands its sun disc up beside the cone, both of which compete with the crater for the eye. Entering the volcano defaults the dome to 9 by the sea's and savanna's own mechanism, overridable as always. It is an `IsSolidTerrainScene` — the island's footprint is cut out of the flank and the dark pit shaft backs the drain's glass — and it does **not** replace the sky.
- **Measured when the scene was built** (the figures after #509 are at the end of the section; desktop GPU — Ryzen 9 5900X / Radeon RX 6900 XT — Testbed, **fixed camera**, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 4 (23 Mpix, a 4K-class High load), dome 9 pinned, `fpscap=150`, 16 s runs with the first four readings discarded). Wide vantage with the whole cone in frame: **volcano 96.0 FPS / 10.4 ms**, desert 124.8 / 8.0, mountain 117.7 / 8.5. Play-level vantage beside the arena: **volcano 90.8 / 11.0**, desert 117.9 / 8.5. So it is the dearest terrain scene by about **2.5 ms** at this load — two `VoronoiEdge2` evaluations (nine hashes each) plus four gradient-noise taps for their warps, over a full-frame terrain. **No APU figure is claimed**, and that is the gap: the tiers exist for the weak machine and #209 defends a 75 FPS budget there, so the particle counts, the halo and the crackle are dials from day one and a reduced program is where a tier entry would go. (#509 built that program, `VolcanoReduced`; the APU figure is still owed.)
- **The campaign's eighth chapter plays here** — The Eruption, ten levels (#295 moved the lava ball style here from the Reveal; #395 is the palette's own story).
