The note that stood here until #509 said no level did, "because `Tools/LevelGen` and `Game/Levels` were held by another agent's work while this was made"; the block has been written and shipped since, and the note had simply never been taken down. **Its chapter intro opens on a prologue since #530** — a pass along the flank towards the erupting summit and a climb over the rim into the crater, the shot the tour's stand outside the cone could never make; `VolcanoIntroShots` builds both off this config through `SceneRenderer.VolcanoGroundHeight`, the CPU mirror above made public. See "The volcano opens on a prologue too" in `docs/game-feedback.md`.

### Redrawn from references (#509)

The scene had been tuned by eye through three stated misses and never against a picture of lava. #509 drew 33 references locally (the `design-references` skill; they live in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\509` and not in this repository): a flow channel close up, a flow front, an eruption from a distance, a crater rim at night, a flank at dusk, an ash column, a fountain, a lava field from eye height — three seeds each — and nine img2img redraws over the Testbed's own play and wide frames (strength 0.5 and 0.65, `prompts-509-*.json`). **Every one of them said the same things, and the first build had most of them the other way round.**

- **A flow is mostly DARK.** Every close reference draws a skin of chilled grey crust carried on the melt, with the incandescence showing as an **open core** down the middle of the channel, as hairline cracks in the crust, and as a thin bright line along each bank where the crust tears from the levee. The first build lit a warped honeycomb of cells across the whole width, which from the play camera read as a giraffe's hide laid on the cone. `FlowRadiance` now composes: the crust (`CrustColor`, lit, plus a dull `CrustGlow` that fades down the run), the fissures in its bed (the old anisotropic Voronoi, thinner, at `CrackGlow` — fixed in the ground since #554, below), the core, which is `CORE_AT_VENT` 0.85 of the half-width under the vent pinched to `CORE_AT_FRONT` 0.22 by the front and torn wider in slow patches along the way, and the bank line.
  - **The fissures stay put and the crust rolls over them (#554).** Until #554 the cracked web was the crust's own raft cracks, read in the *scrolled* coordinate, so the whole glowing pattern slid down the flank with the melt; the owner read it the other way round — the glowing seams are the channel's bed and the lava flows through and over them. So the flow is **two layers moving differently**: the fissure network is the same anisotropic Voronoi read in the channel's **unscrolled** coordinate (the distance from the cone's axis, `ConeR`, where everything the melt carries reads `Along`), and the crust rafts are a gradient noise in the scrolled coordinate, 1.4 × 5 `PlateSize`, thresholded so `RAFT_COVER` of the channel is crust. A fissure under a raft shows only `RAFT_SHOW` (0.2) of its glow; one in the **lead** between two rafts glows in full, and the lead itself shows the melt as a dull moving glow (`LEAD_GLOW`, 0.15 of `LavaCool`, under the core's — more and the channel turns back into the lit width #509 took away). The core, its streamlines, its tears, the surge and the bank line are unchanged, and so is the crater's lake (its plates *are* carried, a lake's crust being plates).
    - **Proved in motion, not by eye**: two frames 2 s apart from one fixed Testbed camera beside the flow past the arena (`scene=volcano sceneseed=0 sky=9 nopost campos=-46,0,0 camtarget=-64,-13,-16`, `shot=6,8`). A crude line detector (luminance minus its own 5 px blur, over the channel) overlaps between the two frames at **IoU 0.36 before and 0.82 after**: before, the whole web lights up in the difference image because it has moved; after, the web is silent and what differs is the core's streamlines and the leads drifting down over the fixed seams. From the play and wide vantages the flow reads as it did — the web is under a pixel there and `detail` fades both layers to their means.
    - **Cost: nothing measurable.** The new layer is one gradient-noise tap and a smoothstep, and it sits inside the flow's data branch, so only flow pixels pay it: `VolcanoPS` compiles to 2256 instruction slots against 2187 (`VolcanoReducedPS` 1345 against 1274; `d3dcompiler_47`, `ps_5_0`, O3). Timed in the Testbed (desktop 6900 XT, 1600×900 at ssaa 4, dome 9, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, 18 s runs, first four readings dropped, alternating builds) the medians of the three clean pairs were **15.19 against 15.20 ms** at the camera beside the flow above and **14.03 against 14.07** at the play pin. ⚠ Not a quiet machine: other sessions' Game captures ran through the whole hour and a clean window never came, so each kept pair had one other BS3D.exe running beside it, and every pair in which either run was visibly disturbed (up to five others, readings spread to 50 ms) was dropped — six of twelve. Read it as "no difference visible at this noise", not as a figure to the hundredth.
  - **The core's streamlines are STRIPES, not contours.** The melt shears downstream, so the references draw parallel striations lying along the flow. The first cut drew the zero contour of a noise stretched 10:1 along the flow — and a contour closes on itself round every saddle, so the core filled with long glowing *eyes*. It is `cos(across · 2.2 + 2.5 · bend)` cubed now, the phase bent by a noise stretched along the flow, faded to the cube's own mean (5/16) as the stripes approach the pixel.
  - **The flows are NARROW under the summit** (0.4 of their width at the rim, full width by 0.55 of the cone's radius). At full width from the rim the five flows between them paved the whole upper cone, which no reference drew; a flow leaves its vent as a channel and spreads as the slope eases.
  - **Behind a data branch on the flow mask**: its Voronoi and three noise taps had been paid by every pixel of the terrain, flow or not, since #223.
- **The flank is black rock with thin glowing threads down it, not a glowing web.** The old summit term lit the plates' seam web over the whole upper cone; every eruption reference draws a dark cone streaked with fine incandescent lines from the rim. `Rivulets` draws them: the zero contour of a `GradientNoise3` read on a **circle** in noise space (`(cos b, sin b) · 5` and the cone radius along the third axis), so they wander as they descend and close round the cone with no seam — atan2's bearing tears along −X, the cavern's veins' old scar. A coarse field round the cone, constant down it, decides which threads run and how far each gets (0.32–0.72 of the radius). Behind a data branch on the upper three quarters of the cone.
  - **The summit is OXIDISED**: a dark rust-red (`ScoriaColor`) in patches off the same broad field as the grey scoria, strongest at the rim — every flank reference turns that colour towards its crater.
  - **The crater holds a lava lake**: plates of dark crust drifting outward over it, their seams incandescent (three times `CrackGlow`), open where it wells up in the middle, and its walls lit by it — the one place in the scene a Voronoi web is the right picture, a lake's crust being plates where a flow's is streaks. Behind a data branch on the crater.
- **The halo is kept faint** (`river.Halo² × 0.07`, from 0.3): at the old weight the five flows' halos met under the summit and washed the whole upper cone orange, where every reference keeps the rock between flows black.
- **Cooled lava is glass.** Every ground-level reference draws black pahoehoe catching the sky as a slate sheen — the only thing that tells a lava field from a heap of soot at night. A Fresnel-weighted reflection (`SheenStrength`) on the young basalt and the flows' crust, not on the scoria or the oxidised summit, and **on the crests of the ropy relief**, because an even film reads as a wet floor.
  - **It reflects mostly the ZENITH, not the horizon uniform.** Dome 9's `HorizonColor` is a warm lit band far brighter than the storm deck the scene actually stands under; reflected in full at grazing it turned the whole field brown mud. 8 % of it is kept, so the sheen still follows the dome.
  - **The relief's grain runs ACROSS the downhill direction**, the way pahoehoe folds into ropes. It ran downhill until #509, which the sheen then drew as a brushed-metal field of streaks converging on the cone.
- **The field cracks.** Sinuous glowing cracks wander through the black field in a few patches, more of them beside the flows where the ground is still hot underneath: the zero contour of a two-octave noise (one octave's contour is a smooth curve and read as a glowing wire laid on the ground), a fixed world half-width of 0.22 units, antialiased off the noise's **own slope** — `|n| / (|∇n| · footprint)` is the distance to the contour in pixels, to first order.
  - **⚠ Threshold a noise near zero against one assumed steepness and a saddle paints a blob.** The first cut did (`|n| < w`, the rivulets' way), and wherever the field lingered close to zero across a wide area it drew fat orange shapes. The second took the slope as `fwidth(n)`, which cannot sit in a branch, so every terrain pixel paid for cracks only a few patches have. The slope comes out of `Clouds.fxh`'s `CloudNoiseD` analytically now, which lets the whole crack sit behind a data branch on its patch mask — worth **0.4 ms** of the wide vantage's 23 Mpix frame against the `fwidth` version.
- **`VolcanoReduced` gives up the rivulets and the field cracks together** — the pair #509 brought in off the flows, the forest's and the mountain's pattern (a lone reduction buys nothing on an occupancy-bound pass). The flows, the lake, the sheen and the haze stay on every tier. Selected by `SceneDetail`, so the Game's `Low` takes it; photographed in the Game at `quality=low` to prove it draws.
  - **And since #540 it gives up the flank's cost on the APU as well**, because that is where the chapter was missing `Low`'s budget and a layer probe (`SceneRenderer.VolcanoLayers`, the Testbed's `volcano=`) said the plume, the jets, the blaze and the ash cost nothing measurable there while the terrain was 5.2 ms of a 14.9 frame. The reduced program drops the combed relief and its derivative normal, an octave of the rock's patches and two of the vertex program's scoria, **ends each flow's front on two crossed sines instead of the gradient noise the full program bends it by** (that noise was five taps a pixel inside the river loop and 0.95 ms on its own), and runs on a 256-vertex grid rather than 360 (`VOLCANO_GRID_N_REDUCED`, rebuilt when the tier crosses the line). Game at `Low`: **Caldera 18.31 → 14.49 ms**, Paroxysm 17.35 → 13.47. The figures in full are under the #540 table in `docs/game-shell.md`.
- **The fountains are STREAKS, and they have a heart.** Every fountain reference is a spray of bright arcs — which is what an eye sees of spatter moving that fast — and a field of round blobs read as confetti. Each blob is a quad whose long axis is its velocity's component across the line of sight, `cross(v, toCamera)` having exactly that speed as its length, times `StreakTime` (0.07 s) — so a blob crossing the frame draws an arc segment and one flying at the lens stays round, which is what it is from there. Thrown hotter than the flows (`LavaHot × 1.35` at birth), because every reference's fountain is yellow-white at the vent where every flow is orange. And the blaze where the jets leave the vent, which no count of streaks adds up to, is one soft additive quad over the crater (`GlowStrength`, the `Glow` technique over the buffer's first quad), depth-read so the rim hides its lower half from a low camera and the light seems to come out of the crater.
- **The plume is an ash column with a head.** Every column reference is a stem opening into a head two or three times its width that the wind takes; grown only linearly with age, the first build's spread kept a tube from crater to top. The swirl radius now grows with `age + 2.4·age²` and the drift with `t²`, so the head opens and bends downwind while the stem stands. A puff is shaded as the sphere it stands for — its lower side takes the crater's glow (`PlumeGlow`, 3× `LavaCool`), its upper side what little the night sky gives — with its rim and density broken by a noise in its own frame so a stack of puffs builds lumps rather than a column of soft discs. The under-light falls off smoothly with height (`exp(−4.5·age)`), where the first build cut it at a third of the way up, and the column no longer thins to a quarter between bursts: every reference has a column standing over its volcano.
- **The cloud deck over the crater glows** — in every eruption the references drew, the cloud above it is lit orange from below. The deck is the sky's (`Sky.fx`), so the scene reaches it as **a light on the ground**: `SceneRenderer.TryGetGroundGlow` hands the crater's position, a colour and a reach to `CloudField.SetGroundGlow`, which both hosts state every frame (clearing it for every other scene, so none inherits it), and the sky shader adds that colour to the cloud's underside with a Gaussian fall-off along the plane from the column over the crater, more in thick cloud than in a wisp. It rides `VolcanoEruption`, so the deck flares with each burst. Black for every other scene, which makes the term an exact no-op in the hottest pass in the game rather than a branch. The map editor draws no deck, so it cannot show it — #220's disc and the weather are the precedent.
  - **⚠ Held low for the cluster's sake** (`DeckGlow` 0.11). From the Game's play pose the cluster hangs directly in front of the deck over the crater, where the glow is brightest. At 0.45 the Testbed's wide frame looked like the references; at 0.22 the Game's play pose still put a saturated red-orange sky behind the red and yellow balls — the Testbed's play camera, aimed lower, had not shown it. **The readability constraint is the scene's first rule, and a backdrop behind the cluster counts as much as a light on it.**
- **Measured against `main`** (desktop GPU — Ryzen 9 5900X / Radeon RX 6900 XT — Testbed, fixed camera, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 4 = 23 Mpix, dome 9, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, 18 s runs with the first four readings dropped, medians, alternating builds, shader sets `78ec3486` against the final one): **wide vantage** (`campos=90,30,140 camtarget=-45,40,-250`, the whole cone and the plume in frame) **10.0 → 11.1 ms**, `VolcanoReduced` 10.5; **play vantage** (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`) **11.6 → 11.6** (11.56/11.68/11.53 against 11.68/11.59/11.59), `VolcanoReduced` **10.3** — cheaper than `main`, because the flows, the lake and the crust's Voronoi moved behind data branches that the old program paid on every pixel. **No APU figure yet**, the gap this section has named since #223; the reduced program is where it would be spent.
  - **⚠ Two of the thirty-two runs fell from ~11.7 to ~4.9 ms partway through**, one of each build, with nothing in their logs to say why — both discarded and re-run. Seen while the owner was working at the machine; a Testbed launched with PowerShell's `-WindowStyle Minimized` is *activated* (SW_SHOWMINIMIZED) and took keystrokes from whatever the owner was typing into, which is how the day's first contaminated runs were caught (`[balls]` and `[campin]` lines nobody scripted). Launched with `SW_SHOWMINNOACTIVE` through `CreateProcess` instead, it takes no focus and measured the same as a visible window (10.00 against 10.02 ms).

## The storm

The seventeenth `SceneKind` (#219): the arena hangs in open air among broken cumulus, with lightning breaking through the space between the cells. `StormClouds.fx` + `StormSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Clouds, Surface, Flash, Air — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=storm` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like every scene that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reached it only by loading a level naming it until #380 put every scene on V.

**⚠ IT WAS BUILT AS TERRAIN FIRST, THE OWNER REJECTED IT ON SIGHT, AND THE REASON IS THE MOST TRANSFERABLE THING IN THIS SECTION.** The first build drew the storm as a displaced grid at the island's foot — the same camera-centred terrain mesh every ground sibling uses — with convective turrets standing out of it. It photographed as landscape from every camera the game has, and the verdict was "it does not look like clouds at all, more like some strange rocky land". **Three separate rounds of fixing its SHAPE each made it a better landscape and none of them made it cloud:**

- the turret profile was `smoothstep(1.0, shoulder, d)` — a smoothstep run *backwards*, which holds a flat crown inside `shoulder` and then falls off hard. Flat top, steep wall, hard silhouette: that is a **mesa**, and it photographed as a field of buttes. Rounding it to `pow(1 − d², k)` fixed the mesas and left snow;
- the folded-noise fields were written `1 − |n|`, which is **ridged** noise: it peaks along the noise's zero contours, and a zero contour of a smooth 2-D field is a *line*, so it draws ridges — it is the standard ridged-multifractal terrain primitive. `|n|` peaks at the noise's *extrema*, which are points, so it draws rounded blobs with creased troughs: a cumulus field. **One character, and it decides whether a white lumpy surface reads as rock or as cloud.** Written the wrong way round it came out as crumpled ice ridges, sharper and more obviously stone than the smooth version it replaced;
- and with both fixed it still read as a snowfield, because **the fault was never the shape**. A height field is a *surface*: one height per XZ, so it can be lumpy but it can never be **broken**, and torn cumulus with sky showing between the cells is not expressible in it at all. Nor can its silhouette be anything but a hard geometric edge, where a cloud's dissolves — that is a property of the medium, not of the outline. Brightening it made it snowier; lowering the whole deck bought nothing and left the pit shaft hanging in the air.

**So the clouds are volume now**, and the height field is gone with everything built on it (`Storm.fx`, the turret lattice, the anvil, the clearing, `StormDeckConfig`). What replaced it is soft-edged **billboard puffs clustered into cumulus cells**, alpha-blended, scattered through a volume around and below the arena with real gaps between them — the sea's spray and the mountain's snow pattern, which #151 measured 2000 of at exactly nothing.

**It is therefore no longer an `IsSolidTerrainScene`, and this is the one place the first build's own reasoning has to be read as retracted.** It argued the flag from what the deck *did* — it was drawn as a grid, sat at the terrain level, needed the island's footprint cut out of it and needed the pit shaft behind the drain's glass — and every one of those facts died with the deck. There is no surface to cut a footprint out of and nothing for an opaque shaft to stand in, so the drain looks straight through onto sky and cloud exactly as it does over the space scene (verified from a camera under the island). That makes `OpenBelow` true and hands the drop cinematic its dive to about y = −66 — which is fine here and was checked rather than assumed: the field's own `InnerRadius` keeps every cell at least 105 units out, so directly under the island there is open air, and the dive looks out at cloud rather than through it. It is still **not** `ReplacesSky` — the open air above the cells is the point.

- **A cell's shape is what makes it cumulus, and it is built rather than noised.** Puffs are laid on a profile widest through the middle and tapering at both ends, biased low so the body is heavier than the crown, with each puff's own radius shrinking as it climbs — that gradient *is* the cauliflower. Laying them in a plain ellipsoid gives a bun, which is what the height field's turrets already were.
- **⚠ Every puff carries the middle of the cell it belongs to, and without it the field reads as bubble wrap.** That is not a figure of speech — it is the first thing the eye names. A billboard shaded from its own disc alone gets a full light-to-dark gradient across it and a crisp circular edge, so a cell built of them is a heap of glossy balls. The shader reconstructs the sphere the disc is a section of (one square root) and then blends that normal towards the direction out of the cell's own middle (`MassNormalMix`), which makes the **cell** the thing being lit and lets the individual puffs disappear into it.
- **`EdgeSoftness` runs the opposite way from how it reads, and it was written down backwards once.** It is where a puff's falloff *starts*, so **low is soft and high is hard**: at 0.05 the puff fades from near its middle, at 0.9 it is a solid disc with a rim on it — and a field of those is the bubble wrap again. The outline also carries two turns of gradient noise on the quad's own corner, because a perfect circle reads as a sprite however well it is shaded; on the corner and not on world space, so it rides with the billboard instead of swimming across it as the camera turns.
- **The gaps are the subject.** What makes the scene read as cloud rather than as a ceiling is that sky shows *between* the cells and *through* them, so the two dials that matter most are the cell count against the field's radius and `PuffOpacity` (**0.30**, deliberately low). Filling the volume evenly turns a cloudscape back into a lid.
- **The field is built once and moves in the vertex shader — on a band aligned with the wind, since #532.** Cells are laid uniformly over a band `OuterRadius` long up- and downwind of the arena and `BandHalfWidth` across it, carried downwind at `DriftSpeed`, **wrapped** through the band's downwind end back to the upwind one, and **steered round the arena** — all three as one offset per cell off the cell's own middle (`StormClouds.fx`'s `StormCellOffset`), so a cell moves as one body and no puff can be left behind by a wrap. ⚠ **Until #532 it was an annulus drifting with no wrap at all**, on the argument that "a wrap-around would tear a cell in half, which is the one artefact a cloud cannot survive" — true of a wrap applied per *puff*, which is why this one is applied per *cell*, and 280 units past the 500-unit far plane, where nothing is in frame to tear — which since #551 is `StormClouds.fx`'s `STORM_FAR_THIN_*` instead, a thinning of each puff's opacity over 380–460 from the lens that does softly what the far plane did as a hard cut, because the far plane went to 2000 with the open-ground scenes' far field and would otherwise have put every wrap and both of the band's sides in frame. **The annulus drifted out of the sky.** Photographed from a fixed camera on the deck looking upwind: the horizon was cloud at 10 s, a cell stood on the camera at 300 s (its puffs as sheets across the whole frame) and at 600 s there was **not a cloud in the upwind sky**, while the downwind camera still had its horizon full; and from the Game's own front end a cell stood *on the arena* at 120 s, the balls hanging inside it, and another at 600 s. That was the owner's "the clouds are in one small part" on #510, and it was **time, not count** — the field at launch covers every bearing, and a cell count would have bought nothing. Two more things the same missing bookkeeping broke: `InnerRadius` held for about a minute, the nearest upwind cell's arrival, and **the strike went off where its cell had been *built*** — inside it for the first half minute and in the air it had left ever after, whatever the flash bullets below say about going off inside one. The steer is a Gaussian bump on the along-wind coordinate, R wide, that pushes the cross-wind one outwards — by the whole of R on the arena's own lane, fading to nothing three R abeam, so the cells that would have stood in the island's disc are spread over the ring out to 3R rather than piled on its rim — where **R is `InnerRadius` plus the largest cell's radius** (`StormCellClearance`): it is the *puffs* that clear the arena, because the first cut steered the middles to `InnerRadius` alone and the Game's front end photographed its lens inside a puff at ten seconds, the whole frame milk. The lane's own cell stands `R·e^(−a²/2R²)` abeam, `a² + R²·e^(−a²/R²)` has its minimum, R², at a = 0, and every other cell stands further out than that one — so the clearance holds at launch as it does after an hour, and the band is built with no hole in it; a cell whose lane runs through the island flows round it at under a unit a second sideways. `StormBackdrop.StormCellPosition` is the shader function's host copy, kept in step by hand: the strike asks it where the hashed cell stands this second and walks on to the first within 420 units, so it is still a pure function of the period index and the clock. **Photographed after from the same three cameras at the same five seconds**: the upwind horizon is full of cloud at 600 s, the downwind one still is, and the front end's arena stands in open air with the band all round it at every one of the five — 180 cells now rather than 150, on the owner's "there should be more of them", which with the band 7 % smaller than the annulus is about a quarter more cloud per unit of sky (14 040 quads under the 16 000 cap). **Measured against the pre-change binary** on the #510 rig (Testbed at the play pose, dome 20, 3840×1600 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc fpscap=400`, 70-second windows with the first six readings dropped, two alternating pairs out of two copies of the exe whose `[build]` stamps differ): `main` **8.72 and 8.71 ms** against the band's **8.91 and 8.92** — +0.19 and +0.21 ms for a fifth more cells and the steer's `exp` and `sqrt` per vertex, and the spreads (8.05–9.88 against 8.65–9.38) say the pairs measured the build and not the weather. Unsorted, which is a real approximation and an acceptable one — every puff is the same near-white medium, so two of them the wrong way round changes the blend weights and nothing the eye can name. Depth-**read** and never depth-write: a soft-edged billboard that wrote depth would punch its own quad's silhouette out of everything behind it, which is the hard edge this whole scene exists to avoid.
- **The shared cloud field cannot be used for a deck below the arena, and there are three independent reasons — all read before anything was built.** (1) `Sky.fx`'s ray–plane crossing is not sign-safe from above: `climb = max(direction.y, 0.02)` is clamped *positive* while `(CloudPlaneY − CameraPosition.y)` goes negative once the plane is below the eye, so every direction returns a negative distance and samples a mirror point behind the camera. (2) A second gate makes it moot anyway — `smoothstep(0.0, CloudHorizonFade, direction.y)` is zero for every ray at or below the horizon, so the shared field draws **nothing** in the lower hemisphere. (3) `CloudSunlight()` does not return 1 above the plane; its own `max((CloudPlaneY − worldPosition.y) / climb, 0.0)` degenerates to sampling the point's own XZ column, which under a storm preset returns about `ShadowFloor` (0.16) — a deck field with a live coverage gain would put the arena, the balls and the gun at a permanent 16 % of sun. And the `Cloud*` uniform namespace on a scene effect belongs to the sky's weather regardless, the host pushing `frame.ApplyClouds?.Invoke(effect)` into every scene effect every frame. Hence own uniforms under own names, and **`StormBackdrop.Draw` deliberately does not invoke the cloud hook** — this field does not want a cloud shadow cast on it, it *is* the cloud. All three reasons survived the rewrite unchanged: they are about the shared field's own gates, not about what the storm is drawn with. (Every preset's plane is *above* the arena: Scattered and Clear 190, Broken 175, Overcast 215, Storm 140.)
- **Its `Weather` is `Clear`, and left alone it would have been actively wrong.** The base class defaults to `Scattered`, so a scene whose whole subject is a storm below the arena would have drawn Earth cumulus *above* it at the same time and sandwiched the player between two unrelated weathers. Mars overrides it for the same class of reason.
- **It brought a twentieth `SkyDome` palette, and dome 11 is the proof that it needed one.** Every one of the nineteen existing domes warms towards its horizon, because that is what haze at sea level does; at altitude the horizon goes **paler and bluer** instead. The reason it was originally *forced* was the grid: a terrain scene's distance fade must arrive at the dome's exact `HorizonColor` to hide the finite mesh's own edge, so whatever the dome's horizon is, is also what every far surface becomes — and under dome 11 (the set's brightest daylight, and the first pick here) **the cloud deck photographed as beige desert dunes**. ⚠ **That forcing argument died with the grid** — a field of billboards has no mesh edge to hide and never has to reach the horizon colour — but the dome stays, now on the plainer ground that a pale blue-white horizon is what altitude actually looks like and is what keeps white cloud reading as white cloud. Dome 20 is a deep blue zenith over a pale blue-white horizon, generated the way dome 19 was: colour as a function of captured vertex height rather than painted by eye. The scene also carries its own `HazeTint` for the mid-distance so it stays cloud-coloured under any of the twenty — the outback's two-stage arrangement, and its recorded trap that aerial perspective can behave correctly and still give the wrong picture.
- **The flash is an event on a schedule, and the schedule is a pure function of the wall clock.** `SceneRenderer.StormFlash(time)` puts one strike in each period, hashing *where in the period* it starts and *how big it is* off the period's own index so it never becomes a metronome, with a near-instant attack, a fast decay and a **flicker** over the envelope — real lightning is a train of return strokes, and that stutter is most of what separates it from a light being switched on. Holding no state, the Game, the Testbed and the map editor all see the same strike at the same second. It is `public` for the reason `VolcanoEruption` is: so a sound can hang off it without owning the schedule.
- **It reaches the arena as a `SceneLights` point light far below, on the planetshine recipe — because that is the only channel that works when the deck is out of the play frame.** `TryGetStormFlash` mirrors `TryGetMoonEarthshine`, and `SceneLights.Apply` gained a sixth branch that is self-gating in **time** as well as in kind: between strikes it returns false and the scene takes no slot at all. A lamp far along −Y gives `dot(N, L)` ≈ 1 on every downward-facing normal and ≈ 0 on every upward one, which is precisely "the deck below flashed" — the undersides of the balls, the island's coping and the gun's carriage catch it and their tops keep the dome. ⚠ **The range must comfortably exceed the distance or the lamp arrives as literally nothing**: the shader's attenuation is `saturate(1 − dist/range)²`, exactly 0 at `dist ≥ range`, hence the 3× recipe.
- **Three figures were wrong on the first pass and each was found by measurement rather than by eye.** The **glow's reach** was derived as `TurretSpacing × 0.8` = 136 units, which sounded like "its own cell and a little beyond" and was in fact a patch *smaller than the distance to the strike* (a strike stands 173–348 units out), so the glow landed almost entirely off frame and the flash read as simply not working — diagnosed by deleting the falloff outright, whereupon the identical build drove the deck's mean luminance from 189 to 255, which is what said the plumbing was sound and only the radius wrong. It is its own dial (`GlowReach`, 430) now. The **cloud was overexposed** at a near-white 250 of 255, so the flash had nowhere to go; `TopColor` and `AmbientStrength` both came down. And the **glow's strength** window is narrower than it looks: at 2.6 a strike moved the mean by 3 of 255 and was invisible, at 14 it washed the near cloud to featureless white and took its form with it. 7 works because the term is weighted towards the *shaded* side — a discharge lights cloud from within, so it shows where the sun does not already.
- **⚠ And a strike goes off INSIDE A CELL, not at a hashed radius.** Placed by radius and bearing alone it lands in clear air about as often as in cloud, and a discharge with nothing around it lights nothing — which matters more than it sounds, because from most cameras the glow *is* the flash, the channel itself being inside the cell it went off in. The cells' own built middles are kept when the field is generated (`_stormStrikeCells`, every cell), the strike picks one off the same period index everything else about it is hashed from, and since #532 asks `StormCellPosition` where that cell stands *now* — walking on to the first within 420 units — because the field moves and until #532 the strike did not move with it.
- **The channel is drawn, and it is generated entirely in the vertex shader.** #219 asked for it in as many words, and the first build left it out with the note that light with no source in the frame reads as the sun blinking. The buffer carries only which bolt a vertex belongs to, how far down it stands and which side of the channel it is; the path — the lean, the fork's spread, the stepped-leader kinks, how far each branch reaches — is hashed off the strike's own period index, so a bolt costs no per-frame CPU work, is frozen for the length of one flash, and is the same one in every executable at the same second. It is a camera-facing **ribbon** rather than a tube: a lightning channel is a filament far thinner than a pixel at this distance, and what is actually being drawn is its glare. Additive, because a discharge adds light to what is behind it and never hides it — drawn with alpha over cloud it reads as a painted stripe — and drawn at all only while an envelope is running, so between strikes it is the whole cost of the lightning.
- **⚠ The channel has to run through the band the cells occupy.** Its first cut ran from above the arena down past the whole field, which put its brightest end in clear air over the island and its tail below anything a camera can see, so what reached the frame was a white line crossing the arena rather than a discharge inside the weather. It runs `LayerTopY + 8` down to `LayerBottomY + 18` now.
- **The flash was verified deterministically rather than by catching a strike, and the reason is worth keeping for anyone testing a short event here: nothing in `TestOptions` pins the wall clock, and a 0.45 s strike is shorter than the capture harness's own timing slop.** Four captures aimed at a computed strike peak came back identical to 0.1 of a luminance mean. So the period was temporarily collapsed until a strike is always running, and the scene shot with `DeckGlow` at 7 and at 0: deck mean luminance **189.4 against 155.5**, a 33.9 difference of 255, independent of when the frame landed. (The volcano only escaped this because its burst lasts 4.5 s.) A `time=` pin on `TestOptions` is what would make this a one-shot check; it is not built.
- **Measured, and the medium cost about a millisecond** (reference APU — Ryzen 7 5700U + integrated Radeon — Testbed, fixed camera at the play pose `campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`, dome 20, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 2, `fpscap=150`, 110 s runs with the first 50 readings discarded, median of the rest, two passes). The **terrain build against the cloud build, both drawn in the same session out of two worktrees**: **26.56 ms against 27.55** — 26.316/26.810 and 27.548/27.548, so the spread is under 2 % and the difference is real. Eleven and a half thousand alpha-blended billboards for one millisecond is the same not-fill-bound answer #104, #151 and the sea's own 2000-particle spray all got on this class of machine. In the same session's three-scene pin the storm sits at **30.03 ms against Mars's 33.11 and the meadow's 25.93** — cheaper than the scene before it, well inside the terrain band. ⚠ **The two sessions' absolutes differ by 2.5 ms for the same scene and that is the machine, not the build**: this laptop's iGPU shares one 15 W package budget with its CPU, so only figures taken in one session may be compared (the trap is #151's, written up in the benchmark skill). No desktop figure is claimed.
- **The thunder is BUILT, and it landed with the volcano's boom exactly as #223 was promised.** Both are one-shots baked in `Game/Audio/ProceduralAudio.cs` beside the firework's report, scheduled by a small `SceneEventSounds.cs` ticked from `BS3DGame.Update` right after the ambience. The design this bullet used to describe as a plan is the design that shipped, down to the ambience row: **not** mixed into the bed, because a bed is a sealed 16-second loop and an event baked into one repeats on a fixed interval — a metronome, the very failure the hashed schedule exists to avoid.
  - **The sound rides the LIGHT's own schedule, never its brightness.** `SceneRenderer.TryGetSceneEvent` hands over the event's *index* and the wall-clock second its light began, both drawn from the same hash `StormFlash` and `VolcanoEruption` draw their envelopes from (`StormStrikeSchedule` / `VolcanoBurstSchedule`, one function each so the two cannot drift). An edge detector on brightness would have heard one strike as four: both envelopes flicker within a single event on purpose — a strike has return strokes.
  - **The delay is the real one.** The world is metric by construction (a ball is one unit, the island 26 in radius), so the flight time is distance over **343 units a second** and nothing is authored: a strike at the cloud field's inner radius arrives in 0.31 s and one at its outer radius in 2.3 s, and that spread is how a player tells a near strike from a far one. Measured over 45 s on the front end: seven strikes, distances 155–418 units, delays **0.45–1.22 s**, each scheduled once and spoken once. The volcano's crater stands 266 units out, so its boom is a flat **0.78 s** behind the flash.
  - **Unplaced for the thunder, placed for the boom**, and both are the scene's own geometry. A strike goes off inside a cell of a deck that surrounds and underlies the arena, and by the time its sound has spread over that deck there is no direction left in it; a crater is somewhere, and saying where is half of what the boom is for.
  - **No crack, ever.** `StormFlashCenter` holds every strike out past the clearing on purpose, so the nearest is still over a hundred units away and the air has taken the leading edge off long before it arrives. One roll therefore covers the whole range, with distance spent on level and pitch. The roll itself is a slow, coarse, irregular **gate** over a mid band — that is what makes thunder surge rather than hiss, and without it this is wind. Baked figures: thunder 4.60 s, peak 0.646, RMS **0.132**; eruption 5.00 s, peak 0.675, RMS **0.144** — against the firework report's 0.30, because these play under a bed.
- **Its ambience bed is thin high-altitude wind** — a wide steady rush with none of the desert's grit and none of the sea's rhythm, because at this altitude there is nothing for the wind to move against, under a very low near-steady swell that is the deck itself working.

### Checked against photographs at last (#510)

The scene whose own header records getting the cloud's shape wrong three times without ever looking at one. 15 references rendered locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\510`, not in this repository) on the three vantages the issue named: cumulonimbus cells from an aircraft window, a deck from above, a cell lit from within by a discharge, a bolt's channel and branching, and a dusk anvil — three seeds each. **The field's structure is untouched** — the cell profile, the annulus sampling, the crown taper, the drift, the gaps, the flash schedule and the channel are all as they were. Two things the references disagreed with, and both are the same defect arriving twice.

- **⚠ `MassNormalMix` fixed the SHADING and the bubble wrap was still there, because what the eye counts is the COVERAGE.** The normal blend makes the cell the thing being lit, which is real and worth keeping, but every puff still draws its own disc of alpha — and every disc in a cell was the same size, a uniform band of 0.22–0.40 of the cell radius, a ±29 % spread. **Balls of one size tile, and a tiling is exactly what the eye names.** Every aircraft-window reference shows a cell built of big lobes with smaller lobes standing on those and smaller again on them; the size distribution *is* the cauliflower as much as the crown taper is. The roll is squared over a much wider band (0.12–0.58), which keeps the mean about where it was — 0.12 + (0.58 − 0.12)/3 = 0.27 against 0.31, so a cell has the same body and the total quad area comes out 5 % *lower* — while giving it a long tail of real lobes and a crowd of small billows to break their outlines.
- **⚠ And the silver lining was being drawn on every puff, which is the glossy-ball signature arriving through the ADDITIVE term.** The rim rides `saturate(r2)`, so it brightens towards a puff's own edge — correct for a cloud's edge, and a cell's interior is not made of edges. Every billow inside the cell wore its own bright ring, which is precisely what `MassNormalMix` exists to prevent, one term further down the shader. It is gated on the cell's own silhouette now: `MassNormal` is the direction out of the cell's middle, so a puff square-on to the eye has `|dot(MassNormal, towardsEye)|` near 1 and takes none of the lining, and one out at the cell's edge has it near 0 and takes all of it. One dot and an abs, on a value the vertex shader already hands over.
- **What the references say that this pass did NOT build**, recorded so it is not re-derived: (1) every cumulonimbus in them stands on a **flat layered stratus floor**, where these cells float with clear sky under them — that is a second population of wide flat puffs and a real cost, not a dial; (2) a real bolt's **branches taper to hair-fine tips and end**, where the channel's forks keep their width, and its glare lights the *air* around it in a violet halo well beyond the cloud it left. Judging the channel needs a strike caught on camera, and this scene has no `time=` pin — the flash bullet above records that the last verification had to collapse the period temporarily to do it.
- **⚠ Measured, and the measurement is the finding as much as the number is: a short window cannot measure this scene.** The field drifts bodily downwind (`DriftSpeed` 1.6), so a 16-second run samples whatever cloud happens to cross the camera, and five alternating 16 s pairs on the reference desktop (3840×1600 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, the play pose) gave `main` **8.57–10.40 ms** against the change's **9.18–10.02** — a run-to-run spread of 1.83 ms around a median difference of 0.22, which is to say the pairs measured the weather and not the build. It is #151's trap with drift in place of an orbit, and the answer is the same: sample longer than the thing that moves. **Two 70-second pairs (64 readings each) resolved it, and the change is CHEAPER**: `main` 11.36 against 9.75 on the first pair and **8.48 against 7.42 on the second**, whose spreads are tight (8.20–9.09 and 7.30–7.68) and which is therefore the pair to quote — a 12.5 % saving, in the direction the arithmetic predicts, since the squared roll makes the big puffs that dominate overdraw rare and takes 5.3 % off the total quad area. Shader sets `24412c54` and `1111f0cc`.
- **And the storm's own cost swings within a single run by more than any change this pass made**: 10.03 to 15.85 ms over one 70-second window as the field drifts across the lens. Worth knowing for the adaptive probe, which measures a few seconds and steps a tier off what it sees.

## Mars

The sixteenth `SceneKind` (#277): rust-red cratered ground scattered with dark stone, under a dusty butterscotch sky with two small moons in it. `Mars.fx` + `MarsSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Terrain, Surface, Air, Moons — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=mars` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like every scene that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reached it only by loading a level naming it until #380 put every scene on V.

**It is deliberately not a red-palette copy of the Moon (#125), and the reason is atmospheric rather than aesthetic.** The real Moon has no air at all, which is the *only* reason that scene replaces the sky and closes its horizon with planetary curvature (see "The Moon (Game)"). Mars keeps a thin one, so it stays an ordinary `IsSolidTerrainScene`: real ground under a real dome, the island's footprint cut out of it, the dark pit shaft backing the drain's glass, and no dive for the drop cinematic. What *does* transfer from the Moon is the crater field itself (`CraterLayer`/`TurnCrater`/`CraterField`/`MareBase`) — lifted into `Mars.fx` **verbatim**, because it is generic height-field math with nothing Moon-specific in it; only the colour it is coloured by and the constants that tune it are Mars's own. What does not transfer is the Moon's highland belt and curvature, both of which exist purely to close a horizon with no air to fade into — Mars closes its horizon the ordinary way instead, the outback's own two-stage haze fade to the dome's exact `HorizonColor`.

- **A stone field stands on the plain, and it is the outback's `RockLayer` lattice, ported verbatim a second time.** Real rover photographs are not a skyline of a few tall formations but ground littered with rock at every scale, so this is retuned rather than reused as-is: two lattices (`RockSpacing`/`RockChance`/`RockHeight` for boulders, a second finer `PebbleSpacing` tier rotated against the first for the litter between them), both with `ribDepth` 0 and a low `maxElongation` — Mars rock is wind-worn, not water-cut, so there are no gullies and no desert varnish to speak of. The stones are coloured **dark volcanic basalt** rather than a darker version of the ground's own rust (`BoulderColorDeep`/`BoulderColorBright`): real Mars rock is the one thing on the plain that is not red, dusted with the same colour everything else wears, and that contrast is most of what makes a scattered field read as *rock* rather than as lumps of the dust it stands in. This was added after the scene first shipped bare and cratered (the issue's own stated fallback) — a first look at it read as a plain missing exactly this.
  - **The boulders are sized for the litter they are, not for the mesh they stand on, and that costs something up close.** `MARS_GRID_N`/`MARS_EXTENT` give a cell of about 2.8 world units — sized for the crater field's tens-of-units features, the same grid every camera-centred terrain scene here shares. A 3–6 unit boulder spans only one or two of those cells, so its *silhouette* (real mesh geometry, coarse) reads softer up close than its *shading* (a per-pixel normal off the continuous height function, smooth) would suggest — a gap the outback's own monoliths never hit, because theirs are tens of units across against the same cell. At the distances the play camera actually holds — the island's own foot to the haze, never a lens standing on top of a single stone — this does not show; it would if anything here were framed for a close inspection of one rock; it deliberately is not (`docs/scenes.md`'s convention: measured, not assumed — this line records the boundary that was checked before shipping past it).
- **The sky is a nineteenth `SkyDome` palette, not a new code path.** None of the eighteen existing ones is right for a dust-scattering Martian daytime sky, which gets *brighter* near the horizon and dimmer at the zenith — the opposite of Earth's blue-zenith Rayleigh falloff. Nothing in `SkyDome.ApplyPalette`/`BuildRamp` assumes which end is which; they only average and resample whatever 92-vertex palette they are handed. So the nineteenth entry is pure **data**, generated as a function of each captured vertex's own height (five colour stops interpolated by the vertex's share of the mesh's Y range) rather than hand-painted, because no `.dae` for a Martian sky ever existed to paint by eye. Its own sun (#220) sits high — `(58°, 50°)` — a thin-aired midday sun rather than one of the set's many dusks.
- **Phobos and Deimos are not the Moon's Earth, and copying that mechanism would have been the wrong instinct.** The Earth is drawn inside `MoonSky`, a full-screen sky-*replacement* pass that owns the entire pixel — it only exists because the Moon has no dome to draw instead. Mars keeps its dome, so its two moons are their own small technique (`MarsMoons`) sharing the sky-replacing scenes' full-screen quad and `InverseViewProjection` machinery, **depth-read** against what `MarsTerrain` just wrote (the Moon's own measured reason — depth-read after the ground, never before it) and, unlike the Moon's opaque sky pass, **alpha-blended**, because this composites two small discs over a dome and terrain that are already drawn rather than replacing the frame. Each moon is `MoonDisc`, the Earth's ray-sphere test stripped of continents, weather and an atmosphere rim — neither moon has an atmosphere of its own to put one on.
  - **Neither moon is drawn at its true angular size, and that is a deliberate reversal of the Earth's own rule.** The real Phobos subtends at most ~0.2° and Deimos under 0.06° — a twentieth and a hundredth of the Earth's own carefully-kept 30-pixel disc, i.e. sub-pixel points that would simply never resolve. They are sized instead for the same legibility the Earth was sized *down* to, just from the other direction (`MarsMoonsConfig`'s own doc has the figures).
- **The stone field's cost lands inside the terrain-scene band, not outside it.** Measured on the reference APU (Ryzen 7 5700U + integrated Radeon), Testbed, the play-camera vantage (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`), dome 13 pinned (so the comparison is the terrain shader's own cost, not two different domes), windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 2, `fpscap=150`, 16 s runs with the first four readings discarded: **Mars 32.1 FPS / 31.2 ms** against **outback 31.5 / 31.7** on the same pin — the same class, Mars if anything a shade cheaper despite the added stone lattices, because it carries none of the outback's gully/varnish/heat-shimmer terms. Not run against a paired alternation (`alt=`), so a half-millisecond either way is inside this machine's own run-to-run noise rather than a claimed ranking; what the figure supports is "the same band", not "faster than".
- **What #277 leaves out on purpose, and it is one real gap rather than several now**: a foreground dust-haze/dust-devil overlay (`Spray.fx`'s or the volcano's ash's machinery). Left for later — a bare, stoneless plain would have been the gap that mattered, and that one did not ship.
- **The skyline and the ground's own materials came later (2026-09-17)**, because on a survey of every scene Mars still read as the flattest: a plain running dead level to a haze line, and close up a checkerboard of tiles. The additions were drawn from references rendered locally (layered mesas, dark basaltic dunes, a rover-height plain, cracked bedrock). Every one has its own dial on `MarsTerrainConfig`/`MarsSurfaceConfig`:
  - **A ring of layered mesas** (`MesaField`; `MesaHeight` 60, `MesaInnerRadius` 300, `MesaThreshold`). A low-frequency noise is **thresholded**: a steep smoothstep turns its high ground into flat tops with cliffs around them, which is what a mesa is. Pulling a third of the profile towards quarter steps gives the flanks the ledges of eroding layers, and the ring's own ramp feeds the threshold, so the mesas rise out of the plain as it recedes instead of starting at a circle. At 45 % towards the steps the ledges drew dark vertical runs down the cliffs, and at a 240-unit radius and 70 units tall the nearest mesa filled a quarter of the frame. The haze (460) does the rest: they stand in the dust the way every reference's buttes do.
  - **Strata on the cliffs:** pale and dark sediment (`StrataColorPale`/`StrataColorDark`, `StrataFrequency`) banded by world height with a noise wobble, blended by how far up the landform a point stands and by the cliff's steepness, so the dusty tops stay rust.
  - **Dark basaltic sand in drifts** (`SandColor`, `SandCoverage` 0.30). A noise stretched three times along the wind, only on level ground, carries **ripples** square to the wind. Without them a drift read as a cloud shadow lying on the plain.
  - **Pale bedrock slabs** (`SlabColor`, `SlabCoverage`): flat plates where the dust is thin, cracked along a noise's zero-crossings and faded out against the footprint.
  - **The tiles were the grain.** Two of its three terms were a hash held constant over a 1.3- and a 5.5-unit square (`floor(xz * 0.75)`, `floor(xz * 0.18)`), which steps at every edge. They are gradient noise now; the fine term stays a hash, being sub-pixel by the time its cells could show.
  - **No CPU mirror of `MarsHeight` exists** (nothing is planted on Mars), so the ring changed the height field with nothing on the C# side to keep in step.
  - **It costs, and most of the cost is the mesas.**
    - **The runs:** the Testbed against unmodified `main`, pinned cameras, 1600×900 at ssaa 2× (3200×1800), `nocap`, three alternating pairs.
    - **Authored:** **+0.39 ms** from the play level (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,6,-60`, 2.38 → 2.77) and **+0.85 ms** from above the plain (`campos=0,25,20 camtarget=0,-13,120`), where far more of the ring is in view.
    - **Three rounds of cutting,** each measured:
      - one sand octave instead of two, a sine instead of a noise for the ripples' wobble, cracks only where there is a slab, the strata only where there is a mesa, and the mesa taken out of the pixel's centre tap: from **+0.60 / +1.20** to +0.39 / +0.80;
      - moving the mesas out of `MarsHeight` so the near plain skips them entirely: within noise, so the ring's own evaluations are what the far field pays;
      - the coarse grain's noise, which is 0.04–0.06 ms.
    - **`MarsTerrainReduced`** is the forest's pattern again: `MarsTerrain(input, detail)` behind two techniques, selected by `SceneDetail`, so the Game's `Low` takes it. It drops the sand, the slabs, the strata wobble and the coarse grain and keeps the mesas, which are geometry and must light the same on every tier: **+0.22 / +0.49 ms**. It was photographed at `detail=0` to prove it draws.
    - **Since #540 it runs on a coarser grid as well** (`MARS_GRID_N_REDUCED`, 256 against 360, rebuilt when the tier crosses the line) — the volcano's step, for the same reason: on the APU at `Low` Mars was **+6.1 ms over the meadow**, 1.7 of it per vertex (the crater field and both stone lattices are evaluated at every vertex before the pixel's three taps). Paired in the Testbed at `Low`'s settings, 47 cycles: **16.70 → 15.02 ms**, and photographed identical from the play pose and a low one across the plain. Two cuts to the program were measured beside it and **not** taken: dropping the pebbles' lattice (−1.12 ms) left the plain visibly emptier of its small dark stones, and dropping the fourth crater octave with an octave off both reliefs (−0.25) took the small craters that make the field read as Mars. In the Game's front end (`scene=mars preview=One`, the orbiting camera, three alternating pairs, best of each): **19.15 → 16.74 ms** — still a hair over `Low`'s 16.1 there, and only a menu scene.

## The polar icesheet

The eighteenth `SceneKind` (#222): a flat white expanse carved into sastrugi by the wind, with a crevassed pressure front standing out of it at distance, under a low sun. `Polar.fx` + `PolarSceneConfig`. It is the desert's machinery a third time — a camera-centred `AcquireGridMesh` grid snapped to its cell, displaced in the vertex shader, with the base normal taken **per pixel** from the height field's gradient — and what makes it a scene of its own is not the terrain but the **material**.

**White in reflection, cyan in transmission. That opposition is the whole look**, and it is what #222 asks the scene to be about: the sky reflects off the glaze as white, and the light that goes *into* the ice comes back out cyan, deeper the further it travelled. A crevasse is therefore not a dark slot but a slot that **glows**; a wind-scoured flank is bright where it faces the sky and blue where it faces into itself. The pieces are all borrowed — the sea's Fresnel sky reflection and its subsurface term, the balls' translucency, the desert's sheen lobe — and this is the first scene that makes any of them the subject.

**⚠ The shadows carry the picture, and that is the ACES trap answered rather than dodged.** An all-white field flattens into one tone, because everything lit sits up where the tone curve has no contrast left — the clouds and the Moon both learned it. Three things keep this one legible: the snow's albedo is **not** 1 (0.72/0.76/0.82, cool), the shadow's colour is the **sky's** and therefore blue (snow the sun misses is lit by the zenith alone), and the sastrugi carry their own trough shading independent of the sun. That last one is load-bearing rather than decorative: measured on the dome sweep below, under a **high** sun a flat field's `ndotl` is nearly constant, so with the trough term at ±0.12 the whole field photographed as a sheet of white paper. It is ±0.42 now.

**⚠ The sastrugi are a SURFACE and not a terrain, which was this scene's first real mistake.** Built as displacement at the scale a sastrugi actually has — a few metres across — each one landed at about one grid cell (`POLAR_GRID_N` 420 over `POLAR_EXTENT` 1200 is a cell of 2.86 units), so the mesh could not hold one and the field photographed as coarse dunes with facets on them. An icesheet *is* flat; what the eye reads on it is texture. The geometry carries the broad swells and the front; the sastrugi are a normal perturbation with their own shading, band-limited on the standard rule — exactly as the desert's dunes are geometry and its ripples are not. They are sampled in **wind space** and stretched along it (`DriftStretch` 4.5), because an isotropic field of the same amplitude reads as gravel (#117's finding, arriving on snow).

**⚠ The pressure front is tilted plates, and it took three builds and the owner's rejection to get there.** Smoothed with a noise field, the belt photographed as a line of **breaking waves** — what any smooth ridge under a cyan material reads as, and the one thing this scene cannot afford with a frozen sea sitting next to it in the eye's filing system. Quantised into blocks in **polar** coordinates it photographed as something worse, and the owner said so: *"the crooked columns at the back — it isn't even clear what they are, it looks more like a graphics glitch."* The cells were wedges that grew with distance, so their hashed heights met along radial seams, and at 26 units high over blocks 10–20 across a slab was a column; a row of columns with no name is a glitch. Real pressure ice is a rubble belt of plates, **wider than they are tall**, shoved up and leaning. So the lattice is in **world** space (`SlabSize` 17, one size everywhere), each cell is a **tilted plane** rather than a flat-topped block (`SlabTilt` 0.55, the lean direction hashed per plate), and the front stands **11** units rather than 26 — well under a tenth of its own 70-unit depth.

**⚠ And it is a front rather than a ring.** Closed all the way round, the belt read as an arena wall and the scene stopped being an expanse — which is the one thing #222 asks for above everything else. It covers a sector (`RidgeBearingDegrees` 200, `RidgeSpanDegrees` 150) and fades out over its own edges, so a player orbiting the gun gets both halves of the postcard in one level: ice rubble on one side, open ice to the skyline on the other.

**⚠ The cracked ice is where the level is played, and the first build had none there.** The owner's second report: *"I don't see the cracked ice glowing light blue anywhere."* Both halves of the reason were structural. The crevasses were gated on the pressure front's own strain — true of a real sheet, and useless here, because the front stands 300 units out and no camera goes near it. And on a flat plain the transmission had nothing to work with, since it needs thick ice in the line of sight and a plain offers neither a crevasse wall nor a steep flank. Three things fixed it, each confirmed in `BS3D.exe` from the play camera and not only in the Testbed (whose F10 camera stands low enough behind the gun that the island hides the plain — a trap for anyone judging this scene there):

- **Crevasses open in fields across the plain** (`CrevasseField`: a low-frequency mask over the slots, plus the front's strain on top), and the clearing the island stands on is kept whole — a crack under the arena would be a hole the player cannot fall into.
- **Blue ice** (`BlueIce`, `BlueIceThreshold`): patches where the wind has scoured the snow off and the glacier is bare. Not an invention — whole regions of a real sheet are swept bare and glass-hard, and they are the blue in every polar photograph that has blue in it. Its edge is a **rim** (a 0.05-wide smoothstep on the field) and not a glow; at 0.20 it photographed with a soft halo tens of units wide. The sastrugi are flattened on it, since scoured ice is polished.
- **The crack reads as a crack.** It went through three wrong readings on the way. Wide, shallow and lit by its own profile, it was a **welt** — brighter than the snow, standing proud of it. Darkened, it was a **ramp**: a shallow trough with a pale floor, because the glow peaked in the throat. What reads is narrow (`CrevasseSharpness` — 8, a slot about 3 units across, when this was written; 4 and about 6.7 since the ⚠ below), deep enough in the geometry to have walls the sun can shade (`CrevasseDepth`, 8 then, 3 since), darkened in the throat (`CrevasseDarkening` 0.22), with the transmission peaking **on the walls** where thin ice is lit through from the side — and the wall's Fresnel held back, because a steep face seen from the plain is at a grazing angle and was mirroring the horizon instead of showing its own colour. The grid did not hold a 3-unit slot cleanly and the walls came out jagged, which was taken for the right kind of wrong in a crack in ice — until the ⚠ below measured what it actually drew.

**The dome is the scene's biggest dial, and it was chosen by photograph** (dome **13** — `POLAR_DEFAULT_SKY_DOME` in the Testbed, `POLAR_SKY_DOME` in the Game, the seventh scene to state one). This scene's content is a material and a material shows whatever the light does, so the dome moves it further than it moves any other backdrop here. Measured on four:

| Dome | Sun | What it gives |
|---|---|---|
| 11 | 55°, cyan over a sandy horizon | a credible polar noon, but a high sun on a flat field leaves `ndotl` nearly constant, so the sastrugi read only through their trough shading |
| **13** | **13°, teal horizon into indigo** | **the one.** The low sun rakes the sastrugi into relief, the front glows cyan against it, and the crevasses read as slots of light |
| 16 | 4°, cream over a near-black zenith | the ice takes the warm light and reads **golden-brown** — which a real sheet does at sunset, and is a look, but is not this one |
| 17 | 42°, cream horizon, pale teal | white on white: the cyan never fires, because transmission needs the sun **behind** the ice and a high sun is never behind anything |

The general rule those four state: this scene wants a **low sun and a cold horizon**. #222 says the same in words ("the low sun is half the picture"), and #220 is what made it stateable at all — a dome carries its own sun direction now, so choosing the dome chooses the rake.

**The sparkle is the ground half of #222's diamond dust**: one hash per fine cell, lit only where the crystal's own facet points near the mirror direction, so the near snow twinkles as the camera moves instead of glowing evenly. It is faded out well **before** the cells reach pixel size, which is the desert's grain rule — a hard per-cell value faded at the cell size spends its last few metres as a crawling speckle. The **airborne** diamond dust (`Snow.fx`'s machinery pointed at glints rather than flakes) is not claimed by this pass.

**Cost.** Measured in the Testbed against the desert, which is the scene it shares its machinery with: same camera, `ssaa=2`, 1600×900, `fpscap=400`, medians of 24 windows, two paired runs each — **polar 32.56 / 32.70 ms against desert 27.98 / 28.36**, i.e. about **+16 %** of frame time. It was **+7.9 %** before the rework above, and the difference is honest work rather than waste: crevasse fields across the whole plain and the blue-ice field add about six gradient-noise evaluations a pixel (the crevasse mask in each of the three height taps and in the pixel shader's own lookup, two for the blue ice). ⚠ Two wastes were found on the way and only one of them was worth anything. `CrevasseField` sampled the pressure ridge itself, so one height evaluation cost two ridges and a pixel twelve; passing the ridge down took it to four and bought **1.2 ms**. `BlueIce` was sampled twice in the pixel shader; sampling it once moved the median **0.14 ms**, inside this measurement's own spread, and is recorded as the tidying it was rather than as a saving. The obvious next lever, if the scene ever needs one, is the crevasse mask: it is far lower in frequency than the 1.2-unit spacing of the normal taps, so all three could share one sample.

**What this pass does not claim**: the five LevelGen designs, a place in the campaign's block order and the music that goes with it (a scene is not a chapter — eleven of the twenty scenes are named by shipped levels, and a backdrop with no block is the normal state), and airborne diamond dust. **The polar-night dome with an aurora over it, guessed at here as #205's likely shape, is not what #205 turned out to be** — see "The aurora" below: a wooded night rather than an icesheet's, on the argument that "night forested land" in the issue's own words named the ground as much as the sky. The two scenes share no code beyond `Stars.fxh`'s lattice and the Moon's sky-quad pattern, both already shared with the Moon before either existed. The ambience bed **is** here — a long flat wind with spindrift hissing over it, deliberately not the mountain's gusts, since those two are the scenes this one has to be told apart from.

### Checked against photographs (#511)

15 references rendered locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\511`, not in this repository): a pressure ridge on sea ice, a crevasse from above, a windswept plain under a very low sun, broken floes from the air and a bare glacier surface, three seeds each. **No term was added or removed** — every change here is a figure that had drifted away from what this file's own header says the scene is.

- **⚠ The header says the crevasses are "displaced SHALLOW and shaded DEEP", and the numbers said the opposite.** `CrevasseSharpness` 8 puts the slot at about **3.3 world units** across (the field is `sin(x · 0.075)` cut at `|lines| < 1/8`) and `CrevasseDepth` 8 dropped it **eight units** into that — deeper than wide, on a grid whose cell is about 2.5. The desert's own rule is that a feature narrower than several cells falls between vertices, and that is exactly what happened: the dip was smeared across two or three cells into a broad V whose flanks came out near-vertical, so `steepness` saturated, `iceness` went to 1 along them and they drew as bare ice **brighter than the snow** — the bright welt this file's own comment says was argued out once already. Measured on a scan across one of them at play height: the band ran luminance **155–169 against 137–162** for the snow either side, and cyan with it. Sharpness **8 → 4** (a 6.7-unit slot, about two and a half cells) and depth **8 → 3** puts the geometry back inside what the grid can hold, and the bright ribbons are gone from the plain.
- **⚠ And the signature feature could never reach the camera.** `ClearingRadius` 70 keeps the sheet whole out to `0.8 ×` it and only lets a crevasse open fully at `1.5 ×` — so nothing cracked existed **inside 56 units**, on a scene whose island is only 26 in radius. The owner's original report on this scene was that the cracked ice glowing blue is nowhere to be seen, and the fix then was to stop gating crevasses on the distant front; the clearing was still holding them two island-widths away. **70 → 42** keeps the arena whole for the reason the shader states (a crack under the island would be a hole the player cannot fall into) while letting the nearest one open at about 34 units.
- **The plain was one lavender tone where every reference is carved.** Both low-sun references show gold on the sunlit crests against deep blue in the troughs, and the contrast is *form* shading — real ridges with a lit and a shaded face. Here `DriftAmplitude` 0.22 tilted the sastrugi's normals so little that `ndotl` barely varied across them, and `AmbientStrength` 0.5 then filled what shade was left with the sky's own colour. **0.22 → 0.48** and **0.5 → 0.40**: the drifts read as carved snow rather than as a pattern printed on a sheet, and the troughs take the dome's blue instead of the average of everything.
- **⚠ A hypothesis about the blue-ice patches was tested and REFUTED, and the refutation is the useful part.** A scoured patch photographs as a pale cyan lagoon painted on the plain, brighter than the snow, where every reference's bare ice is slightly *darker* than snow and mottled with dust and old cracks. The obvious cause is the mirror — the sastrugi are deliberately flattened off such a patch (they are snow shapes, and carving them into bare glacier would be worse), so it has no relief left to break the reflection. Holding that reflection back the way a crevasse wall's already is made the patch **brighter**: (133.7, 180.3, 210.6) against (127.7, 173.2, 207.9) over the same 3000 samples, because what a lower fresnel hands back is `snow × 0.55 + transmission`, and on a blue-ice patch that is brighter than the sky it was mirroring. **The transmission is what makes it bright** — and that term is deliberate, being the one that answered the report that this scene's cyan was nowhere the camera looks. So whether the patch is too bright is a question about `TransmissionStrength` and `BlueIce`'s weight in `thickness`, and it is the owner's call rather than a defect to quietly tune. The change was reverted; the measurement is in `Polar.fx` beside the line.
- **Measured against `main`** (desktop, Testbed, fixed camera at the play pose, 3840×1600 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc`, `fpscap=400`, alternating builds, medians of 12): **12.94 / 13.01 ms against 12.30 / 12.24** — about 0.7 cheaper, consistently, with tight spreads either side. **The shader set is the same hash on both halves** (`1111f0cc`), because every change here is a config figure and the only edit to `Polar.fx` is a comment: so this pair isolates the five numbers and nothing else. The saving is most likely the crevasses no longer cutting near-vertical flanks into the grid, which lowers the terrain's silhouette and hands a little more of the frame to the sky.
- **What the references say that was NOT changed**, recorded rather than re-derived: the crevasse photographs are **glowing blue walls around a near-black throat**, which is precisely the design the shader states and still does not photograph as — the throat's darkening is keyed to the sharp slot while what the eye sees is the whole trough; and the scene's overall **lavender cast is the dome's**, where the references are gold-and-blue, which is a choice about the sky rather than about the ice and was left to the owner.

## The aurora

The nineteenth `SceneKind` (#205): a night sky over forested land with a strong, clearly pulsing coloured aurora overhead, the stars still showing through and around it. `Aurora.fx` + `AuroraSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Terrain, Aurora, Stars, Lighting — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=aurora` on any command line, on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like every scene that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reaches it either way since #380 put every scene on V.

**It is the second scene in both families at once, after the Moon (#125).** `IsSolidTerrainScene` — real forested ground, the island's footprint `clip()`ed out, the dark pit shaft backing the drain's glass, and therefore no dive for the drop cinematic. `ReplacesSky` — no dome, no cloud deck, black clear, its own light rig through `TryGetLightRig`. The two memberships answer disjoint questions exactly as the Moon's doc argues they must for the combination to be sound, and the aurora does not disturb that argument — it repeats it a second time rather than complicating it.

- **The ground is Forest.fx's reduced floor, and deliberately not the full one.** `TerrainHeight`, `TerrainNormal` and `NeedleRelief` were copied term for term and are **shared since #581**: both shaders include `ForestGround.fxh`, the one copy, which is kept in one change with `TerrainMirror.Forest`, the CPU mirror both scenes plant on. `ForestFbm` is the forest's as it stood before #281 and stays the aurora's own. **⚠ The copy's promise had lapsed until #579, which is why it is not a copy any more:** the aurora was written on 09-14 and #281 rewrote the forest's `NeedleRelief` from four plane-wave sines to isotropic noise three days later without touching this copy, so the aurora kept the relief the owner had read on the forest floor as "lines and waves" — now the #281 one here too; what does **not** come along is `ForestPS`'s two expensive extras, the triplanar normal variation and the procedural tree shadows — this scene only ever compiles the equivalent of `ForestReducedPS`'s body. Two reasons, not one: a night scene lit by its own dim, coloured glow shows the least of exactly the detail those two extras buy, and — Mars's own precedent for leaving out the outback's gullies and varnish — porting a whole second scene's heaviest tier on spec, unmeasured, is a cost nobody asked for. `SunColor`/`ZenithColor`/`HorizonColor` are pushed **every frame** from `SceneRenderer.AuroraGlowColor` rather than once from a dome, for `TryGetLightRig`'s own reason restated: a dome-derived sun on a domeless scene would be a lie, and here it would additionally be a *frozen* one.
- **The wood is a second, independent planting of `ForestScatterRenderer`, not the daytime forest's own.** Each of the three hosts already keeps exactly one `_forestScatter` field; this adds a parallel `_auroraScatter`, constructed from `AuroraSceneConfig.Terrain` (its own `ForestSceneConfig`) rather than reusing the daytime instance. The alternative — one shared wood lit two different ways — was rejected because `ForestScatterRenderer.ApplySkyTint` bakes a tint into the meshes' cached material colours; two callers retinting the same cache from two different scenes on alternating frames would fight rather than share. The cost is real and was accepted rather than overlooked: a second full set of fifteen meshes and twenty-five renderers, sky-lit and disposed alongside the first in every host. **That independence paid for itself across two rounds of feedback** (below): both "the forest not so much" and, once the wood itself was denser and darker, "little mushrooms with a brown leg and a green cap" were answered entirely inside `AuroraSceneConfig.Terrain` — `ConiferFraction` (0.94, then 1), `Count` 380 — without moving a single tree the daytime forest plants.
- **⚠ Pure conifer (`ConiferFraction` 1), not "mostly" — the broadleaf species was tried and reported back as mushrooms.** It is winter (the owner's own reading, and it fits: an aurora forest is a boreal one, and conifers keep their needles where broadleaves do not), and the first attempt kept the broadleaf's full daytime trunk and shrunk only `ForestTreeConfig.CrownRadius`/`CrownHeight` (3.1/5.6 down to 0.55/0.9) for a "bare for winter" tree. The owner's own words once it was up: **"little mushrooms with a brown leg and a green cap."** Exactly right, and not fixable by further shrinking — a round leaf-lobe crown reads as a round leaf-lobe crown at any size, it just moves from "small tree" to "mushroom" as the trunk-to-crown ratio grows more stick-like. `ForestScatterRenderer` has no bare-branch mesh to reach for, so rather than hunt for a proportion that stops reading as a cap from some angle, the species is left out of this planting entirely.
- **The sky is the Moon's sky pass verbatim, the Earth's compositing replaced by ribbons.** `AuroraSkyVS`/`AuroraSkyPS` are `MoonSkyVS`/`MoonSkyPS` copied whole — the same NDC quad (the *shared* full-screen quad — `_spaceQuad` then, `BackdropServices.FullScreenQuad` since #580 — a fourth consumer after space, the dream and the cavern, now a fifth counting the Moon), the same ray reconstruction, the same three `StarLayer` calls off `Stars.fxh` (a third effect now pushing its uniforms through the identical two lines `ApplySpaceParameters` (in `SpaceBackdrop` since #580) and `ApplyMoonParameters` already write), the same dither against 8-bit banding in the void. `AuroraBackdrop.Draw` (`DrawAurora` until #580) draws the terrain first, depth-writing, then the sky quad depth-**read** against it — the Moon's own measured order (its doc: the opposite interleave was an 8× frame blow-up there). That order was followed on the strength of the Moon's own measurement, not re-derived here.
- **The ribbons are additive over the stars, on purpose, and that is the literal answer to the issue's own wording.** `Aurora()` bands the upper sky by elevation (`AuroraBandHeight`/`BandSoftness`), folds it into curtains, sharpens the fold with a power curve, colours low-to-high by elevation *within* the band (`ColorLow` oxygen green to `ColorHigh` the rarer violet fringe), and pulses on two summed clocks. Added to the star layer rather than composited over it (`sky += Aurora(...)`, never a `lerp`), it can brighten what is behind it but never fully erase it — the storm's lightning channel's own reasoning, and the direct reading of "stars partially visible through/alongside the aurora".
- **⚠ The curtains were `Fbm2Combed` on an unwrapped `atan2` azimuth at first, and that is the exact fault Mars's own gullies section already names: "what a rib count taken off an `atan2` bearing cannot do … the failure the cavern's mineral veins shipped with once."** A third ship of it, caught only once the camera actually orbited the scene: `atan2(dir.x, dir.z)` jumps from +π to −π on one fixed vertical line in the world (`dir.x = 0, dir.z < 0`), and a noise function fed that jump as a coordinate jumps with it — not a seam that moves with the camera, one standing still in the world for the camera to sweep past and photograph. Reported in exactly those terms ("a distinct vertical seam… the same kind of problem other scenes solved"). The fix is the same shape the gullies' own **unit circle** is and the cavern's veins now are: stop computing the angle at all. `AuroraDriftSpeed` now rotates the sample **direction** about the vertical axis (a rotated vector has nothing to wrap) and the streaks are `Fbm3` on that rotated direction, its own vertical axis compressed by `AuroraCurtainWarp` for the elongation `Fbm2Combed` used to give in 2D — 3D noise on a direction has no chart underneath it to seam on, where 2D noise on an unwrapped angle always will.
