Confirmed by orbiting the free camera a full circle around the scene and watching for the line rather than by reasoning about it — the same "measured, not assumed" rule this document holds everywhere else.
- **⚠ The first authored pulse speed measured as no pulse at all, and only a real capture caught it.** `PulseSpeed` shipped its first value at 0.35 rad/s — an 18 s cycle — on the reasoning that a slow breath reads as more natural than a fast one. Two Testbed captures five seconds apart (`shot=3,8`, `scene=aurora`, the establishing vantage) came back visually indistinguishable: the phase term had genuinely moved, but not by enough for a glance to catch, which is a different failure from "the code is wrong" and one no amount of reading the shader would have found. Raised to 0.9 (a 7 s cycle) and re-shot at `shot=2,4,6`, the same gap is unmistakable — the curtain fan visibly rotates between frames. The issue's own "clearly pulsing" is doing real work here: a pulse has to be measured against a viewer's patience, not against what looks organic on paper.
- **The ground's own brightness took two rounds, because the first cut only reached one of the two lights that actually stand between the sky and the wood.** `AuroraGlowColor` — the slow hue-drift clock the ground, and (through `ApplySkyTint`) the trees, read off the same config the sky pulses from — first carried the sky's own `Intensity` straight across at half strength, and the establishing shot read as sunlit rather than night: the sky pass draws thin bright ribbons against a black void, but that same magnitude feeding the ground shader's hemisphere term integrates over the *whole* dome instead of a thin band, and reads far brighter for it. Cut to 0.22×, which was believed settled and written up as such. **It was not**: the owner's own second look at a ground-level capture ("the aurora looks good, the forest not so much") landed on the *other* light — `AuroraLightingConfig`'s rig, which lights the balls, the island, the gun and (through the same `ApplyTo` walk every renderer here takes) the trees' own base shading, independent of `AuroraGlowColor` entirely. That rig had kept the "~1 per channel" `KeyTint`/`BackTint` convention every other sky-replacing scene's rig uses (the Moon's raking sun, the dream's and the cavern's own undimmed coloured keys) — and none of those three lights a populated `ForestScatterRenderer`, so nothing before this scene had actually tested that convention against a lit wood. `KeyTint`/`BackTint` came down by roughly half again (`AuroraLightingConfig`'s own class doc carries the warning and the figures) and `AuroraGlowColor`'s multiplier a further 0.22× → 0.09×, the two cuts made together because they answer the same complaint from two different lights and a single capture cannot attribute which one a change belongs to when both move at once. Confirmed this time against a ground-level capture rather than the establishing shot alone — the ground level is where the wood actually reads.
- **A little falling snow, asked for in the same round of feedback ("it's winter") — and `DrawSnow` had to stop being the mountain's own before it could answer for a second scene.** It took a `SnowConfig` (`_mountainConfig.Snow` or `AuroraSceneConfig.Snow`) rather than reading `_mountainConfig.Snow` directly, and its look uniforms moved from `ApplyMountainParameters`'s config-apply-time push into `DrawSnow` itself, pushed fresh every call: the two scenes share one `Snow.fx` effect and one flake buffer, so whichever config last applied is not the same question as whichever scene is actually being drawn this frame, and only the per-frame push can answer the second one. The buffer itself stays the mountain's alone to build (sized off `_mountainConfig.Snow.FlakeCount` — by `BuildSnowBuffers` until #580, where the `Snowfall` service took the buffer over) — a second buffer for a second `FlakeCount` was not worth it for a config that is mostly asking for a *slower, thinner* fall, not a differently-sized one — so a caller's own `FlakeCount` is clamped to the buffer's real capacity (`_snowFlakeCapacity`) rather than trusted. The now-redundant `ApplySnowParameters` (superseded by the per-frame push) was deleted rather than left standing unread. **#580 reversed the per-frame push the other way**: each scene got its own clone of `Snow.fx`, so a clone's slots can only ever hold its own scene's look, and an `ApplySnowParameters` came back to push it once at load.
- **No seventh `SceneLights` branch, and that is a choice rather than an oversight.** Every existing `ReplacesSky` scene but the dream and the cavern carries one — planetshine, earthshine, the storm's flash — because each has a *specific* off-axis source (a planet, an Earth, a lightning bolt) the ordinary key light's fixed direction cannot reach, and the metallic drain beads need a real point light for their highlight. The aurora's light source is the whole overhead sky, which is already what the key light's own fixed direction is closest to standing in for; a second point light aimed at the same place the key already answers for would be the one thing this file's own convention warns against — a duplicated answer to a question already asked once.
- **No `TryGetSceneEvent` either, for the plainer reason that there is no event.** The storm's flash and the volcano's burst are each a rare, scheduled, self-contained thing with a beginning and an end that a sound can hang off (`SceneEventSounds.cs`). The aurora's pulse is continuous for as long as the scene is up — closer to the campfire's flicker than to a lightning strike — so it needed no schedule, no index and no one-shot audio; `AuroraGlowColor` and `Aurora.fx`'s own `AuroraSkyTime` are both plain functions of the wall clock, not state anything has to track between frames.
- **The ambience bed is the daytime forest's, stilled.** `ProceduralAmbience.BakeScene` gained an explicit `case SceneKind.Aurora`, deliberately not left to the default arm — that arm's own comment names two scenes that once landed there by accident (cave drips on the Moon, then vacuum over the outback) and says so for exactly this reason. The bed is the same two wind/rustle bands the forest already has, quieted and slowed (night air moves less than day air), under a third, very faint, very slow high shimmer on its own unhurried cycle — not offered as a literal recording of what an aurora sounds like, since nobody has one, but as a suggestion that what is overhead is not silent the way a plain night sky would be.
- **Cost is a single-run sanity check, not a measurement, and it is named as such on purpose.** One Testbed run each, same machine, same play-camera vantage (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`), dome 1, `nopost nocap logfps`, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 2: the aurora read **761–765 FPS (1.31 ms)** against the daytime forest's **365–366 FPS (2.73 ms)** at its own full (unreduced) tier — the aurora cheaper by roughly half, which is the direction the reduced-tier ground and the absence of a cloud/wind-shadow term both predict. Not run as a paired alternation (`alt=`) and not run on the reference APU, so neither figure is a claim this class of machine will see — it is the shape a catastrophic regression would have broken, and it did not. **Re-read after the winter retuning** (58 % more trees, the snow overlay) on the same pin: **758–762 FPS**, inside this run's own noise against the figure above — on this machine, at least, neither addition moved it. **Re-read again after the seam fix's move from `Fbm2Combed` to `Fbm3`**: **710–728 FPS**, a real if modest drop (roughly 1.31 → 1.38 ms) consistent with 3D gradient noise costing more per octave than 2D — four extra corner hashes an octave, four octaves. Not paired, not on the reference APU, and small enough that a seam nobody wants back was not a saving worth chasing.
- **Not in any shipped level, and that is deliberate scope rather than an oversight** — the same scope the polar icesheet drew for itself just above, independently. The issue names a scene, not a chapter: no LevelGen design, no campaign slot, no ambience bed beyond the one above (which belongs to the backdrop, not to a block). A scene with no block is the normal state here — twenty scenes exist and the campaign names eleven of them — and the aurora simply joins the seven others this document already carries as unphotographed rather than introducing some new kind of gap. Its viewpoint (the table above) is stated from its own config and is therefore untested against an actual chapter intro; photograph it the moment a level names the scene. **Its intro opens on a prologue since #531** — a low pass between the spruces and a drift over the snow looking up, both found among the planted trees (`AuroraIntroShots`, off `ForestScatterRenderer.Scatter`), photographed through the front end's `tour`; the stand's look-at came down from 260 units up to just over the hills' canopy, and is no longer flown in the Game after a prologue. See "And the aurora, since #531" in `docs/game-feedback.md`.

### A boreal night, from references (#462)

The owner, from play: **the aurora moves too fast and the forest reads as primitive** — render references first, then rebuild the wood against them; it is dark, so the detail can be cheap and suggestive. The references were already on disk: #489's img2img over this very scene (`C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\489`, strength 0.5–0.8, seeds 11–13). All of them agreed on the same handful of things, and the pass is those things, in the order they mattered:

- **⚠ The value structure was inverted, and fixing it was the biggest single change.** The floor was a dark winter moss under dark trees lit green by the key light, so the clearing read as one black mass with lit Christmas trees standing on it. Every reference put the wood on **snow**: a pale floor catching the sky's light under near-black spruces. The floor is now a cool white pigment (`ForestColor` 0.58/0.64/0.76, hollows 0.26/0.30/0.38, needle relief cut to a third — relief that reads as needles on moss reads as grit on snow), and the needles went near-black (`ConiferColor` 0.011/0.026/0.019). No shader change: `AuroraFloor` already took all three colours from the config.
- **A white floor lit only by the aurora is a green-painted floor.** The glow is almost pure green (`ColorLow` has next to no red), so `AuroraSceneConfig.GroundStarlight` — neutral, cool starlight and airglow — is added to the ground's hemisphere term beside it; that is what keeps the snow white with a green cast rather than green. The hills' painted canopy came down from 0.95 to 0.7 so snow shows between the dark on the slopes: at full strength the ground past the clearing was one black mass.
- **Boreal spires, not garden spruces.** The daytime conifer is a broad cone of four to six whorls; planted in rows here it read as a Christmas-tree lot. The references stand tall, narrow, ragged spruces crowded into a wall, and it is the **count of whorls** far more than the proportions that says so — the same crown at four tiers reads as a stack of cones. `ForestTreeConfig` grew `ConiferTiers`/`ConiferTierSpread` (the aurora rolls nine to thirteen) and `ConiferRaggedness` (the spread of whorl widths and the skirt edge's wobble, together; the aurora's is 2), with the crown two thirds as wide and a third taller. Every new dial defaults to what the forest was built with **and consumes the seed exactly as before**, so the daytime wood is not one vertex different. A regular stack of many thin whorls reads as a pagoda at close range; the raggedness is what breaks it. `ForestScatter`'s tree clearance used to be the daytime crown radii as constants — it reads them off the config now, or spires half as wide would have been spaced as garden spruces.
- **850 trees on the hills too, not 380 in the clearing** (`MaxRadius` 430, 36 clusters), so the skyline behind the island is a ragged line of tips against the aurora the way every reference's is, instead of a smooth dark ridge.
- **Dead wood, which every reference had and the scene had none of.** `SnagMesh` — a standing dead spruce: a bent, tapering trunk to a snapped splinter, and ten to seventeen short hanging branch stubs, one in five a longer surviving bough — and the savanna's `DeadwoodMesh` lying in the snow. Both are new kinds on the shared `ForestScatterRenderer`/`ForestScatter` (`ForestSceneConfig.Snags`/`Logs`, **count 0 by default**, so the daytime forest plants none; forty snags and eighteen logs here). They are scattered **after** the four original kinds, on purpose: all of them draw from one rng stream, so anything scattered earlier would have re-planted the whole daytime forest. The snags join the trees' spacing list (a dead spruce inside a living one is the overlap the eye catches); the logs keep their own. ⚠ **The first cut read as bamboo** — thin, pale, straight and olive: the forest's default silver dead-wood grey, carried a third of the way to the aurora's green by `ApplySkyTint` and lit by the green key over the warm bark texture, came out a pale yellow-green shoot. Thicker (0.55 at the base) and a dark cool grey fixed it; a snag reads by its crooked, stubbed silhouette against the snow, not by being pale.
- **The motion** was two clocks and both were too fast, as the issue suspected. `DriftSpeed` 0.15 → 0.02 rad/s: 0.15 turned the whole curtain field about the zenith rigidly, 8.6° a second, so the sky read as a carousel; 0.02 is a turn in about five minutes. What keeps it alive is new: `MorphSpeed` slides the noise domain along the curtains' own long axis, so the folds **re-form where they hang** instead of travelling. `PulseSpeed` 0.9 → 0.4 (a 16 s breath under the shader's slower second clock): #205 raised it from 0.35 because two stills five seconds apart looked identical — a test of whether a still shows a phase difference, not of how a sky reads in motion, which is the owner's complaint and which a still cannot judge. **Judged by the numbers here, not by watching**; the owner's eye is the test.
- **Rays.** Every reference, and every photograph of an aurora, shows fine vertical striation inside a curtain; the folds alone read as soft green smoke. One more octave of the same 3D gradient noise on the same rotated direction, at a far higher frequency across the sky (`RayScale` 24) and stretched a further seven times along the long axis, **cuts into** the curtain's brightness (`RayStrength` 0.55) rather than adding to it, so the folds keep their shape and gain structure. It slides four times faster than the folds re-form — the slow shimmer rays have — and fades as a ray nears the pixel's own size, or a comb finer than the pixels crawls as moiré. The first cut at 40 read as fur near the zenith.
- **The island and the gun take the aurora's colour** — the owner, mid-pass: *"the glow should reflect its light's colour onto the cannon and the island."* The rig had been static on purpose (a gun strobing with the sky's pulse reads as a fault), and it still does not pulse: `AuroraLightingConfig.GlowTint` (0.6) carries the key light and the sky ambient towards the aurora's current **hue** at their own brightness (`AuroraBackdrop.TowardsHue`, `ShiftTowardsSky`'s idea applied to a light), the ground bounce half as far, the back light not at all — a back light that stays starlight keeps every edge from going one colour. It follows the slow hue drift, so the rig moves: `TryGetLightRig` takes the wall clock now, `SceneRenderer.AnimatesLightRig` says which scene's rig moves, and `SkyLightRig.StepSceneLight` is stepped every frame by all three hosts and answers whether the rig changed. **It steps rather than slides** (`AURORA_RIG_STEPS`, 128 across the colour range), because the Game's re-light walks its renderers through an iterator: a continuous hue would re-light, and allocate, every frame, where a step comes about once a second at most (the mix moves at most 0.175 × 0.045 ≈ 0.008 a second, one 128th) and moves a tint by under a percent.
- **⚠ And that exposed a disagreement #205 had shipped with: the drift was never in the sky.** `DriftHueSpeed` was documented as the sky's own slow hue drift that the ground's wash approximates — but `Aurora.fx` never drew it. It was a CPU clock only, and it swung the ground's light the whole way from pure green to pure violet, so for half of every two-minute cycle the clearing went violet under a sky that stayed green. Nobody had seen it on a dark moss floor; the first capture with the island taking the same light came out **lavender under a green curtain**. Now one number (`SceneRenderer.AuroraHueShift`, ±half of `HueSwing` 0.35) shifts the sky's own green-to-violet ramp in the shader, and the ground and the rig read their colour off the same shift around `AURORA_LIGHT_MIX` 0.3 — the band's light as a whole is mostly green, because the lower folds are the brighter and the larger part of what a camera near the ground sees. Judged against captures, not integrated.

**Cost, measured as a paired A/B against `main` in the Testbed** (the desktop's RX 6900 XT, `scene=aurora sceneseed=0 nopost nooverc`, 1600×900 at ssaa 2 — a 3200×1800 target —, no map, simulation frozen, `logfps nocap`, main and branch alternated four times, the median of each 22-second run): at the play-camera vantage (`campos=0,-4,30 camtarget=0,-8,0`) **2.70–2.73 ms before, 2.92–2.93 after, +0.21 ms**; from above the clearing with the whole wood in view (`campos=80,25,140 camtarget=0,-5,0`) **2.45–2.50 before, 2.78–2.79 after, +0.32 ms**. Each pair agreed with the others to 0.05 ms. One branch run read 6.95 ms and is left out: three earlier rounds had run while a local LLM held the same card and read 10–17 ms on both builds, which is what that looks like. Not attributed between the wood (850 trees of many more whorls against 380, the dead wood) and the sky's rays, and not measured on the APU — the scene is in no shipped level, so it reaches a player only through the front end's scene pick.

**Left for the owner's eye**: whether the motion is now right (it cannot be judged from stills — see the motion bullet), and whether the snow's green-teal cast under the aurora is the right amount. **Not done**, and named so nobody mistakes it for an oversight: snow lying on the spruces' branches (the one reference feature with no cheap route — the crown is one tint per draw, so it wants either a second lathe per variant following the skirts' tops or a new up-facing term in the shared instanced shader, and both are worth doing only if the owner misses it), and ground mist between the trunks, which two of the references show.

## The Grid

The twentieth `SceneKind` (#393): an early-1980s computer-graphics backdrop, drawn from **named mathematics rather than noise** — flat-shaded, cheap because of that rather than despite it, in the vocabulary a computer had *before* it could afford to fake reality. `Grid.fx` + `GridSceneConfig` (named nested groups — Terrain, Towers, Lighting — the named-groups shape; why it outlived the map editor's grid is under Space above), `scene=grid` on any command line (`tron` parses the same way, a second key for the one word every capture of this scene gets called), on the Game's scene menu and in its random pick; like every scene that once sat past the cycle's end, the editor reaches it either way since #380 put every scene on V.

**It is the third scene in both families at once, after the Moon (#125) and the aurora (#205).** `IsSolidTerrainScene` — a flat floor, the island's footprint `clip()`ed out, the dark pit shaft backing the drain's glass. `ReplacesSky` — no dome, no cloud deck, black clear, its own light rig through `TryGetLightRig`. The two memberships answer disjoint questions exactly as the Moon's doc argues they must, and the Grid does not disturb that argument a third time either.

- **The floor is genuinely flat, and that is the honest source of most of this scene's cost.** `TerrainHeight` is a constant (`GridLevelY`), not a field — no octave sum, no `PerturbNormalFromHeight`, no per-pixel gradient normal, because the normal is always straight up. That in turn means the camera-centred mesh itself can be coarse: every other terrain scene's grid density exists to hold a displacement sampled at vertex resolution, and a floor that never displaces loses nothing by skipping it — `GRID_MESH_N` is 8 against the hundreds the desert or the mountains carry, at the same 1200-unit `GRID_EXTENT` the Moon and the aurora use. The issue's own thesis (the cheapness is the shading model, not a resolution or supersampling reduction) holds without a single reduced-tier technique: there is nothing left to reduce.
- **The structure is a plain grid plus a Hilbert curve traced along it — one motif from the issue's list, chosen for the reason the issue itself gives it: no chart, no angle, nothing to seam.** `GridHilbertIndex` is Wikipedia's `xy2d`, ported to an unrolled `GRID_HILBERT_BITS`-iteration bit-recursion (6 bits, a 64-node tile — `GRID_HILBERT_N`). The curve's nodes are lattice **vertices**, on every `TraceStride`-th line (2: a trace line, then a plain one — a curve through every node lights half of the lattice's segments, which from a grazing camera is most of the floor), and a segment is lit where its two end nodes are **consecutive** along the curve. The two segments a pixel can be beside share a corner node, so it is three index lookups a pixel, the same as the first cut's. Deliberately **not** the spiral, the concentric rings or anything else built on `atan2`/azimuth: this document already carries three independent ships of the same fault (Mars's gullies, the cavern's veins, the aurora's own ribbons — see "The aurora" above) — an unwrapped angle fed into anything jumps at the one line where it wraps, a seam fixed in the *world* rather than moving with the camera. A grid cell index and a curve-consecutiveness test use no angle at all, so there is no chart underneath either to seam on.
  - **⚠ The first cut drew the rungs, not the curve — and both traps it recorded were that one fault seen from two sides.** It lit the boundary between two consecutive *cells*, which is the edge the curve crosses, at right angles to it, so what stood on the floor was a ladder of disconnected rungs rather than a path. Counted during #393's review by a probe restating the shader's own arithmetic, over 301×301 nodes: **5 390 nodes with no lit segment, 18 027 dead ends and 28 549 T-junctions**; in the establishing shot (`campos=0,15,150 camtarget=0,-13,0`) the "trace" was a scatter of brighter lines in a uniform lattice. The two ⚠ findings of the first cut were honest observations of it: a same-width trace "measured as no trace at all" from play distance — widening it (`AccentWidthScale` 2.4) made the rungs bolder, and is kept for the real curve — and a naive fold "put the seam through the arena", a seam that existed because the tiles never joined (below).
  - **The tiles chain, so there is no seam anywhere.** The classic curve enters a tile at its (0, 0) node and leaves it at (N−1, 0) — the same side — so one tile's last node and the next tile's first along +X are neighbours, and lighting that one extra link (`GRID_HILBERT_LAST` → 0) turns each row of tiles into a single infinite curve. Over the same 301×301 nodes, across several tile boundaries, **every node has exactly two lit segments**: no dead end and no discontinuity, and a top-down render shows the tile boundaries only where they are drawn in. The half-tile offset before the fold stays, as placement only — the arena on a tile's centre rather than on a four-tile corner. `GridMod` is unchanged: a bitwise AND against `N − 1`, correct only because the tile size is a power of two, and close to free where an integer `%` measurably is not (`X3556`).
- **The lines are filtered per axis, and keep their share of light with distance.** The first cut antialiased both line directions by one isotropic `length(fwidth(xz))` and never dimmed a line as it fell below a pixel, so at a grazing angle the lines running away from the camera smeared as wide as the ones crossing it, and the far floor became a bright band of fat lines along the horizon — plain in the establishing shot. `GridAxisFootprint` measures a pixel along each axis separately, and `GridLineMask` never draws a line thinner than a pixel but dims it by the share of that pixel it really covers, then fades to the average coverage once a pixel spans half the spacing — Ben Golus's pristine-grid filtering, restated in world units. The same shot after: the band is gone, the far lattice is an even faint grain, and the trace reads in the near and middle field. The solids' seams go through the same function.
- **A second motif, added on the owner's own request after the first review of the floor alone: distant solids whose windows run Conway's Game of Life** — the issue's second-ranked candidate ("probably the cheapest, most legible starting point"), and the first place this scene's geometry actually follows MAGI/SynthaVision's combinatorial-solid discipline rather than merely citing it. `GridTowerConfig` places `Count` (18) plain rectangular prisms on a ring around the arena (`RadiusMin`/`RadiusMax` 140–380, well outside anywhere a level is actually played), each a deterministic draw from `Seed` (393 — so the Game, the Testbed and the map editor all stand the same solids in the same places, the same reason the map itself is shared between the three). `BuildGridTowers` retries a placement (a plain footprint-circle check against everything already placed, up to twenty attempts) rather than risking two large solids overlapping now that there are enough of them for it to matter. **⚠ It did not know the landmark until #559, and four seeds in ten put a solid's footprint circle on the ring's.** The ring (#512) is built after the solids, from its config alone, so their overlap test never saw it; `sceneseed=3` stood a cube straight through it, which the chapter intro's ring shot found by refusing to fly through. `BuildGridTowers` now asks `GridLandmarkShape` — the one answer `BuildGridLandmark` also builds from — where the ring will stand, and rejects any draw whose footprint circle comes within the same 15-unit gap of the ring's footprint rectangle (`GridRing.FootprintDistance`). That rule is **hard**, unlike the solids' own: after the twenty attempts a solid may still barely touch another, but it takes the first later draw that clears the ring (at most 200). A scratch mirror of the placement over 10 000 seeds: **4 022 → 0** seeds with a footprint circle on the ring, the tightest clearance now exactly the 15-unit gap; the rng only moves where a draw used to land on the ring, so 5 693 layouts changed and `sceneseed=0` — the reviewed one — is not among them.
- **⚠ Towers alone read as a skyline, and the owner said so on the first review: "there should be more of them, and some should be cube-shaped, so they read as abstract digital objects rather than buildings."** A tall, narrow prism is architecture almost by definition — proportion is most of what tells the eye "building" — so `CubeFraction` (0.4) of the count are instead large, close-to-equilateral **cubes** (`CubeSizeMin`/`CubeSizeMax`, 45–75 world units a side, a little per-axis jitter so no two are literally identical) rather than towers (`TowerHeightMin`/`Max` 40–85, `TowerFootprintMin`/`Max` 12–22). The cube's size is not arbitrary: at the shipped `WindowCellSize` (2.4) a 45–75-unit face shows most or all of a 32×32 Life board at once, which is what makes it read as a block *displaying its own generation* rather than as a facade with some windows lit. **Every solid has a roof** since #393's review — the first cut roofed only the cubes, on the argument that the play camera never sees a tower's top — because the Testbed's and the map editor's free cameras can look down on any of them, and the solids are now drawn with their far faces culled, where an open top would show the void straight through.
- **⚠ Without seams the solids were patterns floating in the dark, not objects — and "light only from the seams" is the issue's first rule.** A solid's body (`BodyColor`) is within a few codes of the void behind it, so in the game-camera capture taken before the review the two towers behind the gun were two columns of lit windows inside a bloom halo, with no outline at all. Every edge of every face now glows (`EdgeWidth` 0.9, `EdgeColor` past the glare threshold): each vertex carries its face-local position and the face's size (`FaceLocal`), each face draws half the line along its own border, and two faces meeting at a corner make one line of the full width, filtered by the same `GridLineMask` as the floor. After: both towers read as solids from the play camera, and every cube and tower reads as a wireframe object in the establishing shot and behind the Game's front end.
- **⚠ The first cut's boards were random soup kept alive with random flips, which gave no "variants" at all.** The owner's second review asked that "the state of the Game of Life should also differ between the individual towers/objects — a different seed, showing different nice variants", and the first answer gave each solid its own board seeded at 28 % density, flipping three random cells every generation so it could not freeze. A random start on a 32×32 torus is an anonymous soup that settles into still lifes and blinkers — measured over 400 soups, stale (period ≤ 2) after a median of **365** generations, p10 146, p90 883 — and a random flip every generation wrecks any oscillator it lands beside. (Nor was it a flip: it assigned a random value, so about half of them changed nothing.) `GridLife` now stamps each board, centred, with a **named pattern** from a deck: for a tower a glider fleet, a lightweight, middleweight, heavyweight or mixed spaceship convoy, or a pentadecathlon or a pair of them, turned so a ship travels up or down the tower (a glider fleet, being diagonal, spirals round it); for a cube a pulsar, a quartet of pulsars, Kok's galaxy, a figure eight, a tumbler, octagon 2, a pentadecathlon, a methuselah (R-pentomino, diehard, acorn), a glider fleet or a convoy. Every entry was run against the rules on this exact torus in all eight orientations during the review: the convoys period 4 moving two cells, the gliders period 4 moving one diagonally, the pulsars 3, Kok's galaxy and the figure eight 8, octagon 2 5, the tumbler 14, the pentadecathlons 15; the methuselahs burn out after 419 (R-pentomino), 135 (diehard) and 75 (acorn) generations.
- **A board moves on rather than being stirred.** A board whose generation equals the one two before it — a still life, a field of blinkers, or extinction — holds that for four generations and is then stamped with its next pattern, which leaves every oscillator of period three or more and every spaceship untouched; nothing in either deck has period one or two. A pattern also yields after `LifePatternGenerations` (360, three minutes at the shipped step), so a long level shows more than one. The boards are seeded from the placement stream, so every host and every run show the same patterns on the same solids, and that stream still spends exactly as many draws per solid as the reviewed layout did, so no solid moved.
- **The board wraps round the solid like a label.** The four sides share one board coordinate running round the perimeter, in the order they meet corner to corner, so a glider crossing a corner walks on onto the next face and the panes line up across it; the board's centre — where every pattern is stamped — lands on the middle of the side facing the arena, half-way up, so the face the play camera sees shows its pattern whole. A roof maps the centre to its own. **⚠ Centred on the pattern, not on the board — which the owner caught by eye.** A pattern with an odd width, such as a narrow tower's three-wide pentadecathlon, cannot be centred on the board's centre line, which falls between two cells: it sat half a pane to one side, so one margin was a whole pane wider than the other. `GridLife.CentreX`/`CentreY` report where the stamped pattern's centre really landed — checked against the stamped cells for every pattern in all eight orientations — and `GridLifeCentreOffset`, pushed per draw, shifts that solid's board by the difference in both axes. The first cut gave every face its own random crop of the board, with nothing continuous across a corner.
- **The windows keep a phosphor's afterglow.** The texture carries two generations — red the current one, green the one before — and `GridTowerPS` lights a cell alive now outright and a cell alive only in the generation before at `exp(-age / PhosphorDecay)` (0.12 s; `GridLifeAge` is pushed per draw), so a glider leaves a one-generation trail and a pattern being replaced goes out rather than off. Checked on two Testbed captures 0.25 s apart: in the first the cells that had just died are visibly dimmer than the live ones, in the second they are gone. The panes are feathered over a pixel and fade to their open area once a pixel spans half a pane, so they no longer crawl as the camera moves. Point-sampled off the texel centre, as before, so a discrete board never blurs across a pane.
- **One shared draw buffer, but one draw call per solid — the texture is what forces it.** `BuildGridTowers` bakes every solid's geometry into one combined vertex/index buffer and records each solid's own `(startIndex, primitiveCount)` range into it (`_gridTowerRanges`); `GridBackdrop.Draw` binds that buffer once, loops the ranges and rebinds only the current solid's `GridLifeTexture` and `GridLifeAge` between calls. A few dozen draw calls a frame is nothing next to the city's own thousands-of-buildings pass.
- **Stepped on the CPU, row-parallel, on eighteen staggered clocks.** A board's row is exactly a `uint` (`GridLife.SIZE` is 32), so a generation is carry-save addition of the eight shifted neighbour rows, all 32 cells of a row at once (`GridLife.StepRows`) — bit-exact against the plain rules over 500 random boards × 200 generations, **2.9 µs for all eighteen boards** in Release against 176 µs for a plain `bool[,]` loop like the first cut's, and **zero allocations** over 20 000 generations, pattern changes included. Each board ticks every `LifeStepInterval` (0.5 s, the issue's own "a few generations a second, not per-frame") on its own phase, spread evenly across the interval, so the solids keep their own time rather than one clock's and eighteen uploads never land on one frame; leaving the scene and coming back fires at most one catch-up step per board and keeps the phases.
- **The sky is the plainest of the five — now six — sky-replacing passes: `VoidColor` and a dither against 8-bit banding, nothing else.** `Stars.fxh` is deliberately not included — a starfield is space's, the Moon's and the aurora's look, and this scene's void is meant to read as *nothing drawn* rather than as another night sky. `VoidColor` itself is darker and more cyan-leaning than the Moon's or the aurora's, on the same "not exactly zero" rule every domeless scene's void follows — a frame that goes to zero reads as a hole rather than as a void.
### The lineage drawn on directly (#512)

The scene's own closing credit named two follow-ups it deliberately did not claim — Information International's photoreal polish and Digital Effects' small tumbling solids — and left a **hero object or landmark prop** unclaimed with them. 15 references rendered locally (the `design-references` skill; in `C:\Users\panrd\AI\sd\out\512`, not in this repository), and **not photographs**: this is the one scene that must not read as photoreal, so the prompts asked for the era's own visual language — early-80s CG film stills, a concept sheet of 1982 landmark solids, vector-arcade wireframes, a circuit-terrain flyover and small tumbling solids.

- **⚠ The solids' bodies were ONE flat colour on every face, so a cube was a wireframe outline and not a volume.** Every reference of this vocabulary — and the film the scene credits — does the one thing a 1982 renderer could afford: **one value a face**, off the angle between that face and a fixed direction. No light, no falloff, no per-pixel anything. `GridTowerVertex` carries the face's outward normal now (`right × up`, `BoxMesh`'s own invariant, which is why one helper serves a side face and a roof alike), and the body is `BodyColor · (floor + (1 − floor) · N·L)` against `FaceLight`. The floor (0.22) is well above zero on purpose: a face that vanishes into the void loses that side of the silhouette and leaves the seams to carry it alone.
  - **`BodyColor` had to come up with it, four-fold, and that is not a brightening.** The old value was within a few codes of the void — deliberately, since the seams were added to answer exactly that — so scaling it by a facing term changed nothing the eye could find. At `0.011/0.033/0.049` the *dark* faces land about where every face used to be and only the lit one rises, which is the whole of the effect: nothing got darker, one face lit up.
- **The landmark is a ring standing on edge**, far out on the floor with its own Game of Life running round its band (`GridLandmarkConfig`). Of the five shapes the concept sheet drew — a stepped ziggurat, a faceted polyhedron on a pedestal, a slotted tower, a ring on edge, a stack of cubes — the ring is the one silhouette **nothing else in this scene has**: every other solid here is a box, so a ring reads as a landmark from any bearing, which is the whole job of one. It is still a combinatorial solid, a faceted torus of `Segments` (28) trapezoid segments of four flat quads each, so it stays inside the `BoxMesh` vocabulary the scene credits to MAGI/SynthaVision rather than sweeping a tube. Its plane faces the arena, so the play camera sees a ring and not an edge-on bar, and it rests on the floor.
  - **⚠ The segment joins must not glow, or the ring is a barrel of ribs.** The seam shader lights all four borders of every quad, so a faceted ring drawn the way a tower is would show one bright rib per segment — 28 of them. Each quad reports a face-local X pinned to the middle of a deliberately wide face, so its two *across* borders can never come within an edge width of a pixel; only the long borders draw, and what the eye gets is a pair of clean rails running round the ring.
  - The windings all come from one identity and none is guessed: with `(side, up, planeNormal)` right-handed, the tangent × the plane normal is the radial direction and the radial × the tangent is the plane normal, which gives the outer band, the inner band and the two flanks their orientations in turn.
- **⚠ The Testbed re-seeds every scene's arrangement per run unless `sceneseed=` pins it, and an A/B without it is two different scenes.** The first before/after pair for this pass came back with a visibly different set of solids in the two halves and no change made to the placement code at all. `sceneseed=0` is the arrangement that shipped; every capture compared here passes it. (The aurora's own cost section already used it; it is written down here because this is where it cost a capture.)
- **Measured against `main`** (desktop, Testbed, fixed cameras, `sceneseed=0`, 3840×1600 at ssaa 2, `nopost nooverc`, alternating builds, medians): at the play pose **1.81 / 1.81 ms against 1.82 / 1.82** — one hundredth, which is the flat-shade dot and nothing else — and from the vantage where the ring fills a third of the frame **1.26 against 1.23**, i.e. no measurable cost at all, the landmark being one more range in a buffer that is already bound. ⚠ **`fpscap=400` measures nothing on this scene**: both halves sat at exactly 2.50 ms, the cap, because the Grid is cheap enough to clear 400 FPS at 7680×3200. The figures above are at `fpscap=2000`. Shader sets `1111f0cc` and `3a4151f0`.

- **Deliberately backdrop-only, the issue's own recommended default.** The balls, the island and the gun stay on the ordinary lit `InstancedModel.fx` path, tinted by `GridLightingConfig`'s cool cyan-blue rig exactly as every other sky-replacing scene's rig tints them — nothing here gives the shared instanced path a second, black-body/emissive shading mode. The issue names that as the bigger, separate ask and explicitly does not require it of a first cut; this pass does not claim it.
- **No landmark, so the viewpoint is the sea's own argument turned on a floor instead of water: low and close is what shows the lines raking off to a vanishing point, where an overhead look flattens the whole pattern into a texture** (confirmed by the same debug captures that found the trace's own width fault — the true top-down shots used to isolate `GridHilbertIndex` read as a photograph of a swatch, not a place). Unphotographed: no shipped level names this scene yet.
- **No seventh `SceneLights` branch and no `TryGetSceneEvent`, for the aurora's own reasons restated a third time.** There is no off-axis source this scene's fixed key light cannot already stand in for, and nothing here is a scheduled, self-contained event with a beginning and an end — the floor is either lit or it is not, for as long as the scene is up.
- **The ambience is an electrical hum, not weather this void has none of.** `ProceduralAmbience.BakeScene` gained an explicit `case SceneKind.Grid`, for the same reason the aurora's did rather than being left to the default arm (that arm's own comment names two scenes that once landed there by accident and says so for exactly this reason). A narrow, nearly steady low band stands in for "the power is on" — `Swell`'s depth barely open, because a hum's whole character is that it does not breathe — under a very faint, very slow high whine on its own unrelated cycle, an old CRT's own line frequency rather than a literal recording of anything.
- **Cost is a single-run sanity check, not a measurement, and it is named as such on purpose — the reference desktop this time, not the APU.** One Testbed run each (6900 XT, the play-camera vantage, dome 1, `nopost nocap logfps`, windowed 1600×900 at ssaa 2): the Grid read **2167–2219 FPS (0.45–0.46 ms)** against the cavern's own **918–923 FPS (1.08–1.09 ms)** at its full tier — the cavern being the cheapest scene this document had measured before this one (`#209`'s 5.96 ms, under different, gameplay conditions this sanity check does not claim to reproduce). Not paired, not on the reference APU, and not measured against a level the way the cavern's own figure was — but it is the shape the issue's own thesis predicted, and on this machine it held: a scene with no height field and no starfield came in at under half the cost of the previous cheapest pass. **⚠ Not re-measured since the solids and their Game of Life were added, nor since #393's review** (per-axis line filtering and a second lattice mask on the floor with the same three index lookups; a seam mask, feathered panes and the phosphor on the solids; a roof each). The CPU side of the review was measured offline instead — eighteen boards a generation in 2.9 µs, zero allocations — and every run of that review stayed under `fpscap=75` for the reason below. A hard reset on this machine mid-session (the desktop fault the benchmark skill's own `fpscap=` section already carries, still unfixed) put the rest of that session's verification under `fpscap=75` on the owner's own instruction, which cannot discriminate a scene already running in the thousands of FPS from one running in the hundreds — the plateau it reads is "cheaper than 13.3 ms", not a figure. The addition is a handful of quads and a 32×32 CPU simulation stepped twice a second, so it is expected to move the number above by far less than a single reduced-tier technique buys elsewhere in this game — but that is an expectation standing in for a figure, not a re-measurement, and the next session with headroom to run `nocap` should replace this paragraph rather than build on it.
- **⚠ IT HAS A CHAPTER SINCE #420, and this bullet is what that retracts.** What stood here said the scene was in no shipped level and that the scope was deliberate — true when #393 shipped, and answered by the twelfth block: ten levels, each a named mathematical construction, inserted eleventh so the Mirage keeps the campaign's last word. The chapter-intro tour that had never been seen is photographed now, and the floor's own Hilbert curve has a level of its own hanging over it. What the original scope note said, for the record — the same scope every scene this document ends with had drawn for itself independently: the issue names a scene, not a chapter, and is explicit that campaign placement, the remaining named motifs (a spiral, a Mandelbrot "screen" — Conway's Game of Life on a bank of windows, the issue's own second-ranked candidate, shipped above as a direct follow-up in the same branch) and a black-body shading mode reaching the balls and the gun are separate asks. Land the backdrop first; the rest is a follow-up on an already-shipped scene, the same shape the meadow's rosettes, the forest's tree shadows and the aurora's winter retune each took.
- **Credit, carried from the issue into the shader's own header as well as here, so the reasoning survives past whoever next touches this file.** The backlit optical-printer compositing process that gave TRON (1982) its glowing costumes is the literal model for the shading rule: a near-black body lit only by its own bright seams — here the grid lines, the Hilbert trace and every edge of every solid — fed to the same bloom (`PostProcessPipeline`/`GLARE_THRESHOLD`) every other emissive surface in this game already blooms through, not a flat colour with a glow layer added on top. MAGI/SynthaVision's combinatorial-solid vocabulary is this project's own procedural-mesh house style already (`BoxMesh`, `LatheMesh`, `MeshBuilder`), and the solids hold to it directly rather than only citing it — plain rectangular prisms built to `BoxMesh.AddFace`'s own four side-face parameters and winding, nothing sculpted. Robert Abel and Associates' circuit-patterned digital terrain flyovers are the direct ancestor of the Hilbert tracery. Information International's photoreal polish and Digital Effects' small tumbling solids are both named in the issue as follow-ups a future hero object or prop could draw on; neither is claimed here.
